Fluid perturbations from expanding bubbles in first-order phase transitions
Alberto Roper Pol

Abstract
We study the power spectrum of the velocity field induced during a first-order phase transition occurring in the radiation-dominated era. We focus on the phase of bubble expansion, assuming that it ends with the onset of the sound-wave regime. The main result we present is a refined template for the velocity spectrum at the beginning of the sound-wave phase, which can be used for studying the resulting anisotropic stresses and gravitational wave production. In particular, we find that the breaks in the velocity spectrum are not associated to the bubble size and the sound shell thickness, as previously proposed, but to the position of the discontinuities. This distinction is particularly relevant for supersonic deflagrations, as it implies that the intermediate slope is more pronounced and the two breaks are more separated when the wall velocity approaches the Chapman-Jouget speed, instead of the sound speed. We also show that the asymptotic branches of the velocity power spectrum are determined by the integral over the single-bubble profiles at large scales, and by the discontinuities of the velocity profiles at small scales. Furthermore, we study the dependence of the two breaks and the intermediate slope on the distribution function of the times of bubble nucleation (exponential and simultaneous). All the results presented in this work have been included in the public Python package CosmoGW.



1
Introduction

Phase transitions in the early Universe occur when a scalar field undergoes a process of symmetry breaking, presenting a new vacuum state that becomes degenerate to the previous state as
the critical temperature
T_{c}
is reached.
At later times, the temperature continues to drop and the symmetry-broken state corresponds to the true vacuum of the
scalar-field potential.
When the phase transition is of first order,
the potential barrier separating the symmetric and broken phases is crossed
via tunneling, leading to the formation of broken-phase bubbles

[
78
,
34
,
87
,
114
]
,
which expand and collide with other bubbles, leading to the production of gravitational waves (GWs)

[
121
,
65
,
81
,
83
,
27
]
.
According to the Standard Model (SM), both the electroweak
[
76
]

and the QCD
[
115
]
phase transitions occur as crossovers and,
therefore, they would not lead to significant GW production.
However, first-order phase transitions are found in extensions of the SM that have been proposed,
for example, to provide the required conditions for baryogenesis at the electroweak scale
or to produce particles that could act as dark matter candidates
(see reviews for the electroweak phase transition in
refs.
[
31
,
119
,
10
]
and references therein).
If this is the case, the resultant GW signal, produced
at the electroweak scale, could be probed by the Laser
Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA)
[
30
,
6
,
31
,
12
,
35
,
29
]
.
Furthermore, if the lepton asymmetry in the early Universe is large, the QCD
phase transition could become of first order

[
111
,
122
]

and could be probed by Pulsar Timing Array (PTA) experiments
[
110
,
42
,
41
]
.
The reported evidence for a GW background
detection in 2023 by the NANOGrav
[
3
,
2
]
, the EPTA and InPTA
[
7
,
8
]
, the PPTA
[
96
]
, and the CPTA
[
123
]

collaborations
has led to an increasing interest in looking
for beyond the SM (BSM) physics also around the QCD epoch
[
92
,
9
,
2
,
49
,
88
,
112
]
.

A cosmological first-order phase transition in the radiation-dominated era
features a first stage when scalar field bubbles
in the broken phase nucleate and expand.
As the bubbles expand, the primordial plasma around
the bubbles is perturbed
in regions confined to concentric fluid shells
around the bubble walls
[
83
,
23
,
48
]

and the friction with the primordial plasma can make the
bubble walls reach a terminal velocity
\xi_{w}

[
67
,
15
,
16
]
.
If the friction is not strong enough, the bubbles might run away
and not reach a terminal
velocity.
Determining which terminal velocity the bubbles reach (if any)
is a complicated task that is under
study in the literature
[
15
,
16
,
85
,
86
,
4
,
46
,
120
]
but, in general, we expect bubbles in
a first-order electroweak phase transition to reach a terminal velocity
[
16
]
.

GWs start to be sourced when bubbles (or the associated
fluid shells perturbed around the bubble walls) collide,
breaking the spherical symmetry of an ensemble of expanding
bubbles.
The GW sources are the anisotropic stresses associated with
the gradients of the scalar field
[
121
,
65
,
81
,
83
,
24
,
66
,
71
,
72
,
37
]
and with the
fluid perturbations induced in the primordial plasma by the broken-phase bubbles,
in the form of sound waves
(compressional motion)
[
61
,
62
,
64
,
63
,
79
,
94
,
102
,
60
,
38
,
70
,
39
,
69
,
106
,
113
,
21
,
40
,
28
,
36
,
116
]
and turbulence (vortical motion)
[
80
,
93
,
43
,
74
,
75
,
25
,
99
,
102
,
73
,
107
,
103
,
18
,
100
,
108
,
11
,
98
]
.
Eventually, the phase transition completes, and all the Universe is in the
symmetry-broken phase.
GW production by the bubble wall collisions stops, but
fluid perturbations continue to source GWs
even after the phase transition is completed.
Therefore, unless the amount of energy transferred from the false vacuum
to the fluid is negligible (e.g., when the
bubbles run away), the production of GWs is expected to be dominated by
the fluid perturbations
[
82
,
31
]
.
In the limit of small fluid perturbations,
these can be described as a superposition of sound waves, as found in numerical simulations
of the fluid-scalar field
dynamics

[
61
,
62
,
63
,
70
,
69
,
28
]
.
This is the assumption at the base of the sound shell model

[
64
,
60
]
, which has been extensively
used to evaluate the detectability of GWs from first-order phase transitions with different experiments
[
30
,
31
,
51
,
59
,
9
,
57
,
53
,
12
,
56
,
2
,
98
,
49
,
29
,
32
,
5
,
58
,
118
,
117
,
1
]
.
In recent work, the GW spectrum from sound waves
was revisited
[
106
,
113
]
, extending its applicability to all frequencies and
including the effect of the Universe expansion
[
106
,
28
]
.
The sound shell model has also been applied to deviations with respect
to an ultrarelativistic equation of state, i.e., for

c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq\tfrac{1}{3}

[
54
,
55
,
89
]
,
to include next-to-leading
order corrections due to curvature perturbations
[
55
]
,
and to inverse phase transitions
[
14
,
13
]
.
Since the GW spectrum in the sound-wave regime
increases with the sourcing time,
it is usually assumed to saturate at the time when nonlinearities develop

[
61
,
31
]
.
Indeed, due to the expected formation of shocks

[
95
]
, nonlinearities leading to
vorticity become relevant and eventually
lead to the development of turbulence
[
26
,
25
,
20
,
19
,
105
]
.
Therefore, at the time of shock formation or
as the strength of the phase transition increases, nonlinearities are expected
to arise, leading to a decay of the total kinetic energy
that has an
impact on the resulting GW amplitude
[
38
,
39
,
28
,
40
,
36
]
.
Furthermore, due to the interactions between the scalar field and the fluid,
numerical simulations show that the bubbles might slow
down before colliding in strong deflagrations,
leading to a suppression of the GW spectrum
[
38
,
36
]
,
which has been included in
GW studies in refs.
[
57
,
56
,
89
]
.

In this work,
which is a follow up of ref.
[
106
]
,
we investigate the properties of the velocity perturbations
and of the bubble nucleation history
that ultimately will determine the
GW spectrum from sound waves in the linear regime of fluid perturbations.
We revise the double-peak structure
reported in previous work

[
61
,
62
,
64
,
63
,
60
,
70
,
69
,
98
,
113
,
21
,
28
,
116
]
.
We focus on the characteristic scales of the
velocity spectrum, which will then enter in the GW spectrum via the
associated anisotropic stresses.
Here, we show how the velocity spectrum scales arise from the properties of the single-bubble
profiles
[
48
]
.
The precise relation between the scales of the GW spectrum and those of the fluid velocity
spectrum will be presented
in a separate publication
[
104
]
.
A thorough study of the asymptotic
limits of the velocity spectrum provides us analytical expressions of its
different amplitudes, which are
based on the discontinuities of the velocity profiles,
and allows us to make predictions on the spectral breaks and the intermediate
slope.
We also investigate and reconcile different findings for the
tensor structure of the two-point correlation function of the velocity field
in previous work
[
24
,
71
]
.
Our analytical and numerical templates can be used to reconstruct
the velocity spectrum arising from first-order phase transitions
during the bubble expansion phase and during the sound-wave regime, according
to the sound shell model.
We include our models and computations in the public Python package
CosmoGW

[
109
,
101
]
, together with a tutorial to reproduce
the results presented in this work and in ref.
[
106
]
.

Outline.

In Sec.
2
, we
study the hydrodynamics of the first stage of
uncollided expanding bubbles
and review the fluid self-similar single-bubble profiles in App.
A
.
We then characterize
the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
that corresponds to the
velocity power spectral density before collisions.
This function is crucial since it also determines the
velocity spectrum after collisions and, hence, the GW spectrum

[
60
,
106
]
.
In Sec.
2.3
, we investigate the asymptotic limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
and relate them to
the discontinuities that arise in the fluid self-similar profiles.
We then provide single and double broken power law templates for

{f^{\prime}}^{2}
in Sec.
2.4
.
We show in Sec.
2.5
that quadratic and linear-constant toy models
(see also App.
B
)
can accurately reproduce
the relevant spectral features of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
for subsonic deflagrations
(quadratic), hybrids (linear-constant), and detonations (quadratic).
The analytical study of the correlation and spectral functions of the velocity field produced by the superposition of bubbles is then provided in
Sec.
3
.
We review the general properties of the correlation and spectral functions of homogeneous and isotropic
fields in App.
C
.
In particular, we study the relations between the velocity field correlation function
in coordinate space and its power spectral density in momentum space,
and test results that apply for generic
irrotational fields to our case.
In Secs.
3.1
and
3.2
, we take
the ensemble average over randomly distributed locations of bubble nucleations,
leading to a statistically homogeneous and isotropic velocity field.
In Sec.
3.3
, we study the time
evolution of the power spectral density
of the velocity field, considering two common distributions for the
times of bubble nucleation (exponential and simultaneous).
We review the details and present computations for
these distributions in App.
D
.
To conclude the treatment of the bubble expansion phase, we show in Sec.
3.4
how spherical symmetry implies that the anisotropic stresses that source GWs before collisions are identically zero.
In Sec.
4
,
we deal with energy conservation and the evolution
of the kinetic energy density in the limit of small perturbations,
for a single expanding bubble in Sec.
4.1
, and
for multiple bubbles in Sec.
4.2
.
Noting that the kinetic
energy density during the sound-wave regime in the sound shell model takes
the same value as for a single bubble, in Sec.
4.3
we define
an effective starting time of the sound-wave regime,

t_{\rm sw}
.
In Sec.
5
, we propose an approach based on this deterministic time
to compute the velocity spectrum
and compare it to the one obtained in the sound shell model,
which models collisions via the introduction of a bubble lifetime
distribution function (see Sec.
D.3
for details).
We present the analytical asymptotic limits
in Sec.
5.1
, and provide in Sec.
5.2
the position of the spectral
breaks at the beginning of the sound-wave regime, as a function
of the breaks found in Sec.
2.3
, and the
considered nucleation time distributions.
We then apply the single and double broken power law fits, already used in Sec.
2.4

for
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
, to the power spectral density of the velocity at the beginning
of the sound-wave regime.
The result is an analytical template that minimally depends on the properties
of the single-bubble fluid profiles and the nucleation time statistics.
The velocity spectrum eventually determines the anisotropic stresses sourcing GWs in the sound-wave
regime
[
64
,
60
,
106
,
113
,
54
,
55
,
13
,
104
]
.
We will explore how the results
presented in this work impact the resulting GW spectrum
in a separate publication
[
104
]
.
Finally, we present our conclusions in Sec.
6
.

Fourier convention.

In this paper, we use the following Fourier convention for a generic field
u(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})
,

u(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=\int u(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}}{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}\,,\quad\quad u(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})=\int\frac{u(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{-i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}}{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\,,

(1.1)

distinguishing fields in coordinate and Fourier space only by their arguments.

2
Fluid perturbations from expanding bubbles

In this section, we focus on characterizing the
initial perturbations induced in the fluid
around expanding uncollided broken-phase bubbles.
The analytical study of the velocity field correlations in this regime
(see Sec.
3
) and across collisions
is a crucial step towards an accurate estimate of the velocity
spectrum in the sound-wave regime
[
64
,
60
,
106
,
104
]

and the resulting GW production after percolation
[
31
,
59
,
10
,
29
,
32
]
.
In Sec.
2.1
, we describe the velocity and enthalpy fields induced by a superposition of expanding bubbles and investigate the
Fourier transform of the single-bubble profiles in Sec.
2.2
.
The radial profiles are computed following ref.
[
48
]
,
reviewed in App.
A
, using
CosmoGW

[
101
,
109
]
.
In Sec.
2.3
, we study the asymptotic limits of the
function
f^{\prime}
, which describes the Fourier transform of the velocity field,
and show that they are related to the presence of discontinuities in the velocity profiles,
and to the causality properties of the velocity field.

f^{\prime}
is a key element since, as we demonstrate in Sec.
3
,
it determines the shape of the velocity spectral density.
We provide in Sec.
2.4
templates for the envelope
of the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
in terms of single and double broken power laws.
The properties of
f^{\prime}
can be recovered using simplified toy models for the velocity profiles.
We
present the analytical calculations related to these toy models in Sec.
2.5
:
they are expressed in terms of simple analytical functions that can be found by fixing
a few constants given the
single-bubble velocity profiles.
In Sec.
5
, we evaluate how the scales and slopes that appear in the templates of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}

determine the power spectral density of the velocity field at the starting
time of the sound-wave regime,
F_{\rm L}
.

Denoting respectively as
p
,
e
, and
w=p+e
the pressure, energy density,
and enthalpy of the fluid, the energy-momentum tensor of the cosmological fluid,
T_{\mu\nu}
,
under the assumption of local thermal equilibrium, takes the perfect fluid (pf) form,

T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm pf}=w\,U_{\mu}U_{\nu}+p\,g_{\mu\nu}\,,

(2.1)

where the metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
reduces, in flat space-time, to the Minkowski one

\eta_{\mu\nu}={\rm diag}\{-1,1,1,1\}
.
The 4-velocity is
U^{\mu}\equiv\gamma(1,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{})
,
where
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}\equiv{\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(t)/{\rm\,d}t

is the fluid velocity, with
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(t)
representing the local position of a fluid parcel,
and

\gamma\equiv(1-v^{2})^{-1/2}
is the Lorentz factor.
The normalization condition is
U^{\mu}\,U_{\mu}=-1
.

Before bubble collisions, the total energy-momentum tensor of the
primordial plasma includes both the perfect fluid and the scalar
field contributions,

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}=w\,U_{\mu}U_{\nu}+p\,g_{\mu\nu}\,+\partial_{\mu}\varphi\,\partial_{\nu}\varphi\,-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}g_{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\,\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\,,

(2.2)

where
\varphi
describes the order parameter driving the phase transition,

w=T\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}
is the enthalpy, and
p=-V(\varphi,T)
is the pressure.
The effective potential
V(\varphi,T)
is given by the sum of the tree-level zero-temperature part
V_{0}(\varphi)
and the thermal part
V_{T}(\varphi,T)
.
To model the fluid perturbations due to the
expansion of bubbles, we make the following simplifying assumptions:

•

We assume that
the duration of the phase transition is short
compared to the time scale given by the Hubble rate
H_{*}
, i.e.,
\beta/H_{*}\gg 1
.
The characteristic duration of the phase transition is estimated as the inverse
of the bubble nucleation rate
\beta

[
31
]

(see App.
D

for details on the nucleation time distribution functions).
Therefore, we fix
a\approx a_{*}
and
H\approx H_{*}\ll\beta
,
and we neglect the expansion of the Universe
in the bubble expansion phase.
Hence, the conservation of the energy-momentum tensor is described in flat space-time
\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
.

•

We restrict ourselves to
the bag equation of state

[
33
,
48
]
to describe the
pressure and energy density of the perfect fluid.
In the bag equation of state,
the pressure is described with a thermal
contribution from radiation and a vacuum
contribution
from the scalar field,

p_{\pm}=-V(\varphi_{\pm},T_{\pm})=\tfrac{1}{3}a_{\pm}T_{\pm}^{4}-\epsilon_{\pm}\,,

(2.3)

where
T
is the local temperature of the plasma and

\epsilon_{\pm}
represents the vacuum energy in the broken (
-
) and the symmetric (
+
) phases.
In the bag equation of state, it is further assumed that
\epsilon_{-}=0
and
\epsilon_{+}=\epsilon
,
where the bag constant
\epsilon
only depends on the background nucleation
temperature
T_{n}
and characterizes the difference in the effective potential between the broken
and symmetric phases.
The enthalpy and energy density are

w_{\pm}=T_{\pm}\frac{\partial p_{\pm}}{\partial T}=\tfrac{4}{3}a_{\pm}T^{4}_{\pm}\,,\qquad e_{\pm}=w_{\pm}-p_{\pm}=a_{\pm}T_{\pm}^{4}+\epsilon_{\pm}\,.

(2.4)

Under this assumption, we restrict the dynamics of the scalar field to the
bubble wall thickness, assuming that, out of the bubble wall, the scalar field takes
the value corresponding to either the broken or the symmetric phase.
Hence, we will describe the fluid perturbations at both sides of the bubble walls considering
the energy-momentum of the perfect fluid given in Eq. (
2.1
),
since the gradients of the scalar field vanish
outside the bubble walls, and we will treat the bubble walls as a discontinuity in the
fluid solutions (see App.
A
for details).

2.1
Superposition of single-bubble profiles

In a first-order phase transition, once the broken-phase bubbles are nucleated,
they expand due to the potential difference between
the true and the false vacua, described by the bag constant in the bag equation
of state.
The friction exerted by the plasma particles on the bubbles can balance this
effect, resulting in a steady expansion at a terminal bubble wall velocity
\xi_{w}
.
In this case, the velocity and enthalpy perturbations of the plasma around the single-bubble
wall reach self-similar radial profiles,
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
and
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
, where

\xi\equiv r^{(n)}/t^{(n)}
is the self-similar variable,
with
r^{(n)}\equiv|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}|
being the radial distance to the nucleation center
of the
n
-th bubble
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}
and
t^{(n)}\equiv t-t_{0}^{(n)}
being
the respective duration since its nucleation time

t_{0}^{(n)}

[
48
]
.
The subindex ip indicates each of the “individual profiles” induced by a single expanding bubble.

We review the calculation of the radial profiles
v_{\rm ip}(\xi)
and
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
in App.
A
,
following the description of refs.
[
48
,
60
]
.
As discussed in Sec.
1
, since the duration of the phase transition
is assumed to be much shorter than the Hubble time,
\beta/H_{*}\gg 1
, we neglect
the expansion of the Universe in the phase of bubble expansion.
We provide, as part of the public Python package
CosmoGW

[
109
,
101
]
,
the
hydro_bubbles
library and a tutorial to compute
the self-similar profiles for different wall velocities and strengths of the phase transition,
together with the calculations required to compute the velocity spectrum presented in this work,
as well as the resulting GW spectrum, following
the description of ref.
[
106
]
.

The resulting velocity and enthalpy fields can be expressed as the superposition of
the fluctuations induced by
N_{b}
individual expanding
spherically symmetric
bubbles in a volume
V

[
64
]
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{b}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}^{(n)}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{b}}\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}}{}^{(n)}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,,\qquad w(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{b}}w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,,

(2.5)

where we stress that
\xi\equiv r^{(n)}/t^{(n)}
depends on the bubble
n
.

Since the bubble wall thickness is usually several orders of magnitude smaller
than the characteristic scale of the fluid
[
67
,
48
]
,
the fluid fields are approximated to be discontinuous across the phase boundary.
The boundary conditions at each side of the bubble wall depend on the specific value of
the wall velocity and the strength of the phase transition, leading to three types of solutions:
subsonic deflagrations, supersonic deflagrations (hybrids), and detonations
[
48
]
.
The matching conditions across the bubble wall and the different types of
hydrodynamical solutions are respectively presented in Secs.
A.2
and
A.3
.

The radial profiles of the velocity and enthalpy fields,
v_{\rm ip}(\xi)
and
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
,
for the different types of solutions are shown in Fig.
1
for a benchmark value of
\alpha=0.1

and a range of wall velocities
\xi_{w}
.
The parameter
\alpha\equiv\tfrac{4}{3}\,\Delta\theta/w_{n}=\tfrac{4}{3}\,\epsilon/w_{n}

characterizes the strength of the phase transition
in terms of the energy-momentum tensor trace anomaly,

\Delta\theta\equiv{\textstyle{1\over 4}}\eta_{\mu\nu}(T^{\mu\nu}_{+}-T^{\mu\nu}_{-})={\textstyle{1\over 4}}(\Delta e-3\Delta p)=\epsilon
, and
w_{n}
is the
background enthalpy of the fluid at the nucleation temperature
T_{n}

[
31
]
.
The profiles are computed using the bag equation of state as discussed above.

Figure 1
:

Solid lines illustrate the radial
velocity
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
(left)
and enthalpy
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
(right) profiles
for different values of the bubble wall velocity
\xi_{w}

and the benchmark phase transition strength
\alpha=0.1
.
Vertical black, dashed lines are at the sound speed

c_{\rm s}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
and at the
Chapman-Jouget speed
v_{\rm CJ}
[see Eq. (
2.6
)],
indicating the boundaries between
subsonic deflagrations, hybrids, and detonations.
Dotted lines correspond to the positions
\xi_{\text{sh}}
where a shock forms
for deflagration solutions [see Eqs. (
A.22
) and (
A.23
)].

Figure
2

shows the different types of solutions that are
allowed as a function of
\alpha
and
\xi_{w}
.
The boundary between subsonic and supersonic deflagrations is indicated
by the speed of sound,
c_{\rm s}
, while the Chapman-Jouget speed, which
depends on
\alpha
[see Eq. (
A.20
)],

v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)=\frac{1+\sqrt{\alpha\,(2+3\alpha)}}{\sqrt{3}\,(1+\alpha)}\,,

(2.6)

determines the boundary between supersonic deflagrations and
detonations.
As previously indicated in ref.
[
48
]
, deflagrations (either subsonic
or supersonic)
are only allowed when
\alpha_{+}\leq\tfrac{1}{3}
,
where
\alpha_{+}\equiv\tfrac{4}{3}\,\Delta\theta/w_{+}=\tfrac{4}{3}\,\epsilon/w_{+}
,
being
w_{+}
the enthalpy evaluated
immediately in front of the bubble wall (see details in App.
A
).
The energy released by the phase transition
can increase the enthalpy in the perturbed regions.
Therefore, in general
w_{+}\geq w_{n}
, only having
w_{+}=w_{n}

for detonations as the region outside the
bubbles is not perturbed in this case.
This implies that
\alpha\geq\alpha_{+}
and the upper bound on
\alpha_{+}\leq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}

corresponds to an upper bound on
\alpha<\alpha_{\rm max}\simeq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}(1-\xi_{w})^{-13/10}
, found in ref.
[
48
]

(see Fig.
2
for a comparison between the numerically computed
\alpha_{\rm max}
and this analytical fit).

Figure 2
:
Regions of allowed solutions for the phase transition strength
\alpha
, evaluated at the nucleation temperature and the wall velocity
\xi_{w}
.
The upper bound on
\alpha
corresponds to
\alpha_{+}=\tfrac{1}{3}
,
being the largest value allowed for deflagrations (see discussion in App.
A
).
This bound is computed numerically and
compared to the analytical fit,
\alpha_{\rm max}\simeq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}(1-\xi_{w})^{-13/10}

[
48
]
. The boundary between subsonic and supersonic deflagrations is given by the dashed
vertical line, indicating the speed of sound,
c_{\rm s}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
.
The boundary between supersonic deflagrations (hybrids) and detonations is indicated by the Chapman-Jouget speed,
v_{\rm CJ}\,(\alpha)
[see Eq. (
2.6
)].

2.2
Self-similar fluid profiles in Fourier space: the
f^{\prime}(z)
function

The Fourier transform
of the velocity field of a single expanding bubble,

v_{i}^{(n)}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})=\hat{r}_{i}^{(n)}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
, is

\displaystyle v_{i}^{(n)}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=\int v_{i}^{(n)}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}}{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}=[t^{(n)}]^{3}e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}}\int\hat{\xi}_{i}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,e^{i{\boldsymbol{z}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}{\rm\,d}^{3}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,,

(2.7)

where
z_{i}\equiv t^{(n)}\,k_{i}
,
z=|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle z$}}{}|
,
\xi_{i}=r_{i}^{(n)}/t^{(n)}
,

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}
,
and
\hat{\xi}_{i}=\xi_{i}/\xi=\hat{r}_{i}^{(n)}
.
Using partial integration, we can write
[
64
,
60
]

v_{i}^{(n)}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=-i\,[t^{(n)}]^{3}\,e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}}\,\hat{k}_{i}\,f^{\prime}(z)\,.

(2.8)

The function
f(z)

corresponds to the following one-dimensional integral
along the radial coordinate
\xi
,

\displaystyle f(z)=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}j_{0}(z\xi)\,\xi\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\quad\Rightarrow\quad f^{\prime}(z)=-4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}j_{1}(z\xi)\,\xi^{2}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(2.9)

where
j_{0}(x)=\sin x/x
and
j_{1}(x)=\sin x/x^{2}-\cos x/x
are spherical
Bessel functions of the first kind.
The velocity field has compact support in
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
,
where
\xi_{\rm f}
and
\xi_{\rm b}
indicate the front and back positions
of the fluid shell, such that
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}=0
at
\xi\in(0,\xi_{\rm b})\cup(\xi_{\rm f},1)
(see Fig.
1
).
Therefore, the integral in Eq. (
2.9
) always converges.

As discussed in Sec.
A.3

and summarized in Table
1
,

\xi_{\rm b}=\min(\xi_{w},c_{\rm s})

in general.
For detonations,
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
,
whereas
for deflagrations,

\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
,
being
\xi_{\text{sh}}
the position
where
a shock forms
(see the velocity profiles in Fig.
1
).
While detonations only present one discontinuity
at
\xi_{w}
,
all deflagrations present two discontinuities: one discontinuity at
\xi_{w}
and a second
discontinuity at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
For subsonic deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\lesssim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}
, while
a shock still
forms at
\xi_{\text{sh}}\simeq c_{\rm s}
,
the jump in the velocity
becomes negligibly small.

Table 1
:
Discontinuities in the velocity profiles and their relation to the scales of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.

\xi_{\rm f}

\xi_{\rm b}

\xi_{\text{sh}}\geq c_{\rm s}

\xi_{w}

2\,(\xi_{\text{sh}}-\xi_{w})

\tfrac{5}{3}\,(\xi_{w}+c_{\rm s})

\tfrac{4}{3}\,(\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s})

The function
f^{\prime}(z)
is a crucial element in understanding the resulting
fluid perturbations.
As we will see in Sec.
3
,
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

is involved in determining the velocity field
spectrum during the bubble expansion phase.
Furthermore,
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
also enters in the velocity spectral density of the sound-wave phase,
according to the sound shell model
[
64
,
60
,
106
,
54
,
55
]
.
As a consequence, the resulting GW spectrum is affected by the
characteristic scale dependence of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
In particular,
it is usually argued that the GW spectrum produced by sound waves
is characterized by two length scales: the mean size of the bubbles at collisions,

R_{\ast}
, and the fluid shell thickness
R_{\ast}\Delta\xi=R_{\ast}(\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b})
,
leading to a double-peak structure of the spectral shape

[
24
,
71
,
72
,
61
,
62
,
64
,
63
,
60
,
59
,
70
,
21
,
98
,
106
,
28
,
29
,
31
,
30
,
10
,
116
,
69
,
113
,
39
,
40
]
.
Motivated by understanding this double-peak
structure,
we investigate the velocity field spectrum
and its characteristic scales,
finding that, in general,
the latter differ from

R_{\ast}
and
R_{\ast}\Delta\xi
.

2.3
Asymptotic limits of
f^{\prime}(z)

In this section, we analyze the behavior of
f^{\prime}(z)
and determine the scales at which its slope changes.
We first study the asymptotic large-scale (
z\to 0
) and small-scale (
z\to\infty
) limits of

f^{\prime}(z)
,
and investigate at which scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
do these limits
respectively take over.
In Sec.
2.4
,
we then provide a template for the envelope
of the
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
function.
We find that, in general,

z_{2}
is determined
by a new scale

\tilde{\Delta}\xi
.
We confirm that,
for detonations,
this is indeed the fluid shell thickness,

\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\Delta\xi\equiv\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b}
.
However,
for deflagrations, we find that
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
is instead
the distance between discontinuities
in the velocity profile,
i.e.,

\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\xi_{\text{sh}}-\xi_{w}
.
Note that
\Delta\xi
and
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
are equal
for subsonic deflagrations,
but differ for hybrids (see Table
1
).
Furthermore, we find that,
contrary to what has been
proposed previously in the literature,
also
z_{1}

depends on the self-similar profiles via
the linear combination

\xi_{+}=\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm b}=\xi_{w}+c_{\rm s}
for detonations and

\xi_{+}=\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{w}=\xi_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}
for deflagrations.
In general, when two discontinuities are present, as in deflagrations,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are determined by the positions
of these discontinuities,
\xi_{w}
and
\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
For subsonic deflagrations, these correspond to the positions of the front (
c_{\rm s}
) and the back (
\xi_{w}
) of the fluid shell, while for supersonic deflagrations, the back of the shell (
c_{\rm s}
) differs from the position of the discontinuity (
\xi_{w}
).
When only one discontinuity is present, as in detonations,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are determined by the positions of the front (
\xi_{w}
) and the back (
c_{\rm s}
) of the fluid shell.
Then,
z_{1}
is given by the sum of these scales and
z_{2}
by their difference.
Based on these findings, we
provide analytical templates of the function
f^{\prime}(z)
that depend on

z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
In Secs.
3
–
5
we will demonstrate precisely how
f^{\prime}(z)
determines the
velocity field power spectrum
at the initial time
of the sound-wave regime.
As we will see, an average over both bubble nucleation
locations and times is necessary.
It is only after this averaging procedure, that the scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
can be related to their corresponding wavenumbers
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, and therefore can be compared to averaged quantities like, e.g.,
R_{*}
.

2.3.1
Large-scales limit

To understand the large-scales limit,
we Taylor expand
f^{\prime}(z)
around
z\rightarrow 0
,

f^{\prime}(z)=f_{0}^{\prime}\,z+{\cal O}(z^{3})\,,\quad{\rm with\ \ }f_{0}^{\prime}=-\frac{4\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(2.10)

which converges due to the compact support of the velocity profiles.
Hence, in the large-scale limit, the function

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
has the following behavior,

\displaystyle{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)={f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}\,z^{2}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\quad\text{for \ }z\to 0\,,

(2.11)

with the
z^{2}
slope being a consequence of causality.
Indeed, we show in Sec.
3
that
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
provides the longitudinal component of
the velocity field power spectral density, and, since the velocity
field is irrotational, it should
scale proportional to
z^{2}
in the
z\to 0
limit, as required by causality (see App.
C

for a review of the properties
of spectral functions of statistically homogeneous and isotropic fields).
Figure
3

shows the
function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
divided by
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}z^{2}
for benchmark phase transitions with
\alpha=0.1

and a range of
\xi_{w}
.
The numerical
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
obtained using the velocity profiles
shown in Fig.
1
are compared to the analytical fits and toy models that
we study, respectively,
in Secs.
2.4
and
2.5
.

We find from the numerical evaluations of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
that
deviations from the branch growing as
z^{2}
occur at

z_{1}=2\pi/\xi_{1}
(indicated by vertical solid lines in Fig.
3
)
with

\displaystyle\xi_{1}\equiv\frac{2\pi}{z_{1}}\simeq\begin{cases}\,\tfrac{4}{3}\,(\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{w})\ &\rm{for\ deflagrations}\,,\\
\,\tfrac{5}{3}\,(\xi_{w}+c_{\rm s})\ &\rm{for\ detonations}\,.\end{cases}

(2.12)

As discussed above, we confirm that the position
z_{1}

only depends on the sum of the
discontinuity locations
for deflagrations,
\xi_{+}=\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{w}
, and
the sum of the front and back locations for detonations,
\xi_{+}=\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm b}=\xi_{w}+c_{\rm s}
.

Figure 3
:

The function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
characterizes the spectral
density of the velocity field. It is shown, normalized by its asymptotic form
in the
z\to 0

limit,
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}z^{2}
[see Eq. (
2.10
)], in
colored solid lines, evaluated
at different wall velocities
\xi_{w}
for a benchmark
phase transition strength
\alpha=0.1
.
The thick solid lines represent the single or double broken power
law fits given in Sec.
2.4
.
The dotted lines are computed using the
corresponding toy models: quadratic profiles for subsonic deflagrations
and detonations, and linear-constant profiles for hybrids
(see Sec.
2.5
).
The positions of the scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, where the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

transitions to its asymptotic forms for
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
respectively,
are indicated with vertical lines and computed using the fits provided in Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.16
).
The asymptotic limit
z\to 0

and the envelope
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
in the
z\to\infty
limit [see Eq. (
2.15
)]
are shown in red solid lines.
Their intersection corresponds to
z_{\rm cross}
, defined in Eq. (
2.19
).
The type of solution is determined by
\xi_{w}
, where subsonic deflagrations
occur for
\xi_{w}<c_{\rm s}
, hybrids for
c_{\rm s}\leq\xi_{w}\leq v_{\rm CJ}
,
and detonations for
\xi_{w}>v_{\rm CJ}
, being
v_{\rm CJ}
the Chapman-Jouget speed,
which takes a value
v_{\rm CJ}\simeq 0.78
for
\alpha=0.1
[see Eq. (
2.6
)].

2.3.2
Small-scales limit

In this section, we investigate
the small-scales limit

z\to\infty
of
f^{\prime}(z)
.
This limit depends on the
discontinuities of the velocity self-similar profiles.
As we have seen,
they have compact support on
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
, and present either one or two
discontinuities: one at the bubble wall and another one due to the formation of a shock
at the front of the fluid shell in deflagrations (see discussion in Sec.
2.2
and
more details in App.
A
).
Integrating by parts Eq. (
2.9
),
we can write

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z)=\frac{4\pi}{z}\,\Biggl[\sum_{\xi_{0}}\xi_{0}^{2}\,j_{2}(z\xi_{0})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{0})+\int_{\mathcal{C}}j_{2}(z\xi)\,\xi^{3}\,\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{\xi}\right){\rm\,d}\xi\Biggr]\,,

(2.13)

where
\mathcal{C}\equiv(\xi_{b},\xi_{w})\cup(\xi_{w},\xi_{f})
is the manifold where the velocity profile
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
is smooth,

\xi_{0}=\{\xi_{w},\,\xi_{\text{sh}}\}
is the potential set of discontinuities in the velocity,

\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{0})\equiv v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{0}^{+})-v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{0}^{-})

is the set of
jumps at the discontinuities,
and
j_{2}(x)\equiv(3-x^{2})\sin x/x^{3}-3\cos x/x^{2}
is the
second-order spherical Bessel function of the first kind.
Integrating by parts again, the second term in Eq. (
2.13
) yields higher powers of
z
in the denominator,
such that the leading contribution in the small-scales limit is

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime}_{\infty}(z)}{z^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-3})\quad\text{for }z\to\infty\,,

(2.14a)

where
we have kept the leading-order term
of the spherical Bessel function
j_{2}(x)=-\sin x/x+{\cal O}(x^{-2})

and we have defined
the oscillatory amplitude

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\infty}(z)\equiv

\displaystyle\,-4\pi\sum_{\xi_{0}}\xi_{0}\sin(z\xi_{0})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-4\pi\bigl[\xi_{\text{sh}}\,\sin(z\xi_{\text{sh}})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})+\xi_{w}\,\sin(z\xi_{w})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})\bigr]\,.

(2.14b)

The jump of the velocity at
\xi_{\text{sh}}

is
\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})=-v_{\text{sh}}^{-}=-v_{\text{sh}}
[see Eq. (
A.22
)]
when a shock forms for deflagrations.
For subsonic deflagrations,
the jump in the velocity profile
at
c_{\rm s}
decays with
\xi_{w}
, so its contribution to the asymptotic limit
becomes negligible for small
\xi_{w}
.
The jump at
\xi_{w}
,
\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})=v_{+}-v_{-}
, is
positive for deflagrations and negative for detonations.
We summarize in Table
1
the discontinuities present for each type of solution.
In Secs.
A.2
and
A.3
of App.
A
, we describe how
to compute
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
for each type of solution
and how to determine the position (
\xi_{\text{sh}}
) and amplitude of the shock,
v_{\text{sh}}
[see Eq. (
A.22
)].

Equation (
2.14a
) justifies
the
z^{-4}
asymptotic behavior
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
at small scales, shown in Fig.
3
.
Furthermore, Eq. (
2.14b
) allows us to predict the amplitude of the asymptotic branch
z\to\infty
as a function of the discontinuity jumps in the velocity profiles.
The envelope of this asymptotic branch is

{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}(z)=\frac{{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}}{z^{4}}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }f^{\prime}_{\text{env}}\equiv 4\pi\,(\xi_{\text{sh}}\,v_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}\,|v_{+}-v_{-}|)\,.

(2.15)

We infer numerically that the onset of the
z^{-4}
regime, indicated by vertical dotted lines in Fig.
3
, occurs at
z_{2}=2\pi/\xi_{2}
with

\displaystyle\xi_{2}\equiv\frac{2\pi}{z_{2}}\simeq\begin{cases}\,2\,(\xi_{\text{sh}}-\xi_{w})\ &\text{for deflagrations}\,,\\
\,\tfrac{4}{3}\,(\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s})\ &\rm{for\ detonations}\,.\end{cases}

(2.16)

For deflagrations,

\xi_{2}\propto\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\xi_{\text{sh}}-\xi_{w}

is determined by
the distance between the two velocity field discontinuities.
We note that for subsonic
deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}v_{\rm CJ}
,

z_{2}
approaches
z_{1}

and, hence, they are not clearly
distinguishable
in
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, as it can
be seen in Fig.
3
.
For detonations,
there is only one discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
, and we find
that the second scale
z_{2}
depends on
the fluid shell thickness
\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\Delta\xi=\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s}
.

After averaging over bubble nucleation locations and times
(see Secs.
3
–
5
),
z_{2}
determines the second break in the velocity power spectrum.
Ultimately, this then enters in the GW power spectrum.
We therefore find that,
contrary to what
proposed in previous literature, where the sound shell thickness has been identified as the relevant scale entering the GW spectrum

[
61
,
64
,
60
]
,
it is the
difference between the discontinuities for deflagrations and the
fluid shell thickness for detonations the relevant scale.
This difference is particularly relevant for hybrids.
As an illustrative example, the confined
hybrid with
\xi_{w}=0.7
shown in Fig.
3

has
\tilde{\Delta}\xi\simeq 0.01
and
\Delta\xi\simeq 0.14
, hence larger
\Delta\xi
but
smaller
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
than the hybrid with
\xi_{w}=0.6
,
for which
\tilde{\Delta}\xi\simeq 0.05
and
\Delta\xi\simeq 0.07

(see Fig.
1
and Table
1
).
Indeed, from Fig.
3
, it appears that the position of the second break is clearly found at larger
z
for
\xi_{w}=0.7
.
The fact that the second scale for the hybrids has to be computed from
\tilde{\Delta}\xi

is even more clear
in Fig.
4
, where
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
is shown for three
values of
\xi_{w}
that are approaching
v_{\rm CJ}
from below.
It can be seen that as
\xi_{w}\to v_{\rm CJ}^{-}
,
z_{2}
increases and, hence, it is not well
characterized by
\Delta\xi
.

Figure 4
:

Same as Fig.
3
for hybrids
with
\alpha=0.1
and a bubble wall
velocity approaching
v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)\simeq 0.78
.
We find that, as we approach
\xi_{w}\to v_{\rm CJ}^{-}
, the
second scale
z_{2}
becomes progressively larger:
this is due to the fact that
it is characterized by the inverse distance between
discontinuities,
\tilde{\Delta}\xi^{-1}
(see Eq. (
2.16
)
and the right panel in
Fig.
5

for a comparison between
\Delta\xi^{-1}
and
\tilde{\Delta}\xi^{-1}
).
A second scale determined by the inverse fluid shell thickness,

\Delta\xi^{-1}
, would not capture well the
z
dependence of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.

type

Table 2
:

Relevant set of parameters to reconstruct the single and double broken power law
fits in Eqs. (
2.17
) and (
2.18
)
of the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
for
\alpha=0.1
and different
values of
\xi_{w}
.

{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
provides the amplitude of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in the

z\to 0
limit.
Together with the envelope in the
z\to\infty
limit,
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
, the crossing
scale
z_{\rm cross}
and
the intermediate slope
\gamma
can be reconstructed
using Eq. (
2.19
).
The two scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are summarized in Table
1

and depend either
on the positions of the front and the back (detonations) or the positions
of the discontinuities (deflagrations):
z_{1}
depends
on their sum (
\xi_{+}
), while
z_{2}
depends on their difference (
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
).
For comparison, we give
the value of
\Delta\xi\equiv\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b}
when it is different
than
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
, i.e.,
for hybrids.
The amplitude
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
can be reconstructed from the values
of the velocity
jumps at
\xi_{w}
and at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
, which can be computed
from
\alpha_{+}
and

\xi_{\text{sh}}

following the procedure described in App.
A
.

We summarize in Table
2
the values
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
and
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
that
determine the asymptotic limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
,
and the values
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
that characterize the two scales
(
\xi_{+}
and
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
)
for benchmark phase transitions with
\alpha=0.1
and different
wall velocities.
We also provide
\xi_{\text{sh}}

and
\alpha_{+}\leq\alpha
,
where

\alpha_{+}
can be
used to determine the values of the velocity jump at
\xi_{w}
(see Sec.
A.2
)
and, hence, to determine
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
together with
\xi_{\text{sh}}

and
v_{\text{sh}}
, using Eq. (
2.15
).

Figure 5
:

Left panel:
Dependence on the bubble wall velocity,
\xi_{w}
, of the parameters
z_{1}
(blue) and
z_{2}
(red),
given in Eqs. (

) and (
2.16
), that characterize the shape of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
The double broken power law structure in the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

arises when
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
,
corresponding to the value at which
z_{\text{cross}}
(green),
given in Eq. (

),
starts to deviate from
z_{1}
.
The Chapman-Jouget speed,
v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
,
the speed of sound,
c_{\rm s}
, and
\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
,
are indicated by vertical gray lines.
We show

,

, and

for
\alpha=0.01
(dotted),

0.05
(dashed), and
0.1
(solid).

Comparison of
\tilde{\Delta}\xi^{-1}
(blue),

\Delta\xi^{-1}
(red), and
|\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s}|^{-1}
(green) for
\alpha=0.1
.
The choices
\Delta\xi^{-1}

[
61
,

,
60
,
70
,
69
,

,
29
,
32
]
and

|\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s}|^{-1}

[
30
,
31
,
98
]

have been previously considered in the literature to determine the
position of the second
spectral break.
We show how previous choices deviate from
\tilde{\Delta}\xi^{-1}
for
hybrids.
The largest separation between the two scales occurs
when
\xi_{w}\lesssim v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
, differing from the usual
assumption that it occurs when the fluid shell thickness
reaches its minimum at
\xi_{w}\approx c_{\rm s}

[
31
,
60
,
59
,
98
]
.

2.4
Single and double broken power law templates for
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

As we have seen in Sec.
2.3
, the asymptotic limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

for large and small scales are respectively reached at
z_{1}=2\pi/\xi_{1}
and
z_{2}=2\pi/\xi_{2}
,
summarized in Table
1
.

The dependence of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
on
\xi_{w}
for various values of
\alpha
is shown in Fig.
5
. From the numerical results, shown in Fig.
3
, we find that the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
takes the form of a multiple
broken power law modulated by oscillations.
When
\xi_{w}\simeq\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}
, we see that the scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
become very
close to each other and,
for
\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}v_{\rm CJ}
,
the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
takes the form of a single broken power law (SBPL) given by

{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\rm SBPL}(z)={f_{0}^{\prime}}^{2}\,z^{2}\,\biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{z}{z_{1}}\biggr)^{a_{1}}\biggr]^{-\frac{6}{a_{1}}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\lesssim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)\,,

(2.17)

where the value of
a_{1}
determines the smoothness of the transition of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
around
z_{1}
and has been set to
4
in Fig.
3
.
Even though we only show results for
\alpha=0.1
in Fig.
3
, we have
confirmed that the presented fits are also valid for other values of
\alpha
.

Figure 6
:

Intermediate slope
\gamma
, defined in Eq. (
2.19
), as a function of
\xi_{w}
for different values of
\alpha=0.01
(green), 0.05 (blue), and 0.1 (red).
For each value of
\alpha
,
we plot
\gamma

in the range
\xi_{w}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
(indicated
by vertical, colored dashed lines),
in which we fit the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

with a double broken power law [see Eq. (
2.18
)].
The Chapman-Jouget speed,
v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
,
and the speed of sound,
c_{\rm s}
, are indicated with
vertical solid lines.

On the other hand, when
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}
, the second scale
z_{2}
becomes
clearly separated from
z_{1}
.
This can be interpreted as the appearance of a second discontinuity,
since it is roughly for
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}
that the velocity jump at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
starts to be non-negligible.
Therefore,
we see a clear intermediate slope in the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
,
which can be fit with a double broken power law (DBPL),

{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\rm DBPL}(z)={f_{0}^{\prime}}^{2}\,z^{2}\,\biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{z}{z_{1}}\biggr)^{a_{1}}\biggr]^{\frac{\gamma-2}{a_{1}}}\,\biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{z}{z_{2}}\biggr)^{a_{2}}\biggr]^{\frac{-\gamma-4}{a_{2}}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)\,,

(2.18)

where
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
respectively
determine the smoothness
of the transitions around
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
Both parameters are set to
4
in Fig.
3
.
The intermediate slope
can be computed as

\gamma=2\left[1-3\,\frac{\log(z_{2}/z_{\text{cross}})}{\log(z_{2}/z_{1})}\right]\in[-4,2]\,,\qquad\text{with \ }z_{\text{cross}}=\left|\frac{f_{\text{env}}^{\prime}}{f^{\prime}_{0}}\right|^{1/3}\,.

(2.19)

We have defined
z_{\text{cross}}
(see Fig.
5
) as the scale at which the two asymptotic
branches of the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
cross.
We show the intermediate slope
\gamma
for different values of
\xi_{w}

and
\alpha
in Fig.
6
.
Notice that, when all scales collapse to one
z_{2}\simeq z_{1}\simeq z_{\text{cross}}
,
then
\gamma\to-4
,
and we recover the single broken power law fit, as in Eq. (
2.17
).
The expressions for
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
in Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.16
) have been tested for values of
\xi_{w}\gtrsim 0.1
.
For smaller values of
\xi_{w}
, we propose to use the single broken power law fit in Eq. (
2.17
) with
z_{1}
substituted with
z_{\text{cross}}
as the latter is more accurate.
The necessary quantities to reconstruct the
values of the fitting parameters
z_{1}
,
z_{2},
and
\gamma

are reported in Table
2
for a range of wall velocities
and the benchmark value
\alpha=0.1
.
As mentioned above, the deviations of
z_{\rm cross}
from
z_{1}
happen
for
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}
(see Fig.
5

for
\alpha=0.1
) and
justify the need for a second scale
z_{2}

and a double broken power law fit for
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}
.
For these deflagrations, the jump of the velocity profile at

\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
is not negligible, as can be seen in Fig.
1
,
while for subsonic deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\lesssim\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}
the velocity jump is close to zero.

2.5
Toy models for the velocity spectral shape

In this section, we propose simplified analytical toy
models that can be used to mimic
the velocity profiles.
These toy models are constructed using the minimal amount of information from the velocity profiles that is relevant to provide an accurate velocity power spectrum and, hence,
the resulting GW spectrum.
In particular, to mimic
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
for
subsonic deflagrations, we consider
a quadratic velocity profile with a discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm b}=\xi_{w}
.
When a shock is formed for
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}
,
we
add a second discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
For the case of hybrids, we consider a piecewise
linear-constant profile that continuously goes to zero at
c_{\rm s}
and
presents discontinuities at
\xi_{w}
and at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\rm sh}
.
Finally, to mimic detonations,
we use again a quadratic profile with a
single discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
.
The parameters of our toy models
are fixed by reproducing the
constants
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
and
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}

using Eqs. (
2.10
) and (
2.15
), which
determine the asymptotic limits
of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
These toy models are illustrated in Fig.
7

and described in Secs.
2.5.1
and
2.5.2
.
In App.
B
, we compare the proposed quadratic and linear-constant toy models with even simpler
ones (e.g., constant
and linear toy models), which allow us to exactly reproduce the locations
z_{1}

and
z_{2}
and one of the two asymptotic limits (e.g.,
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
)
but they fail to reproduce
the opposite asymptotic limit (e.g.,
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
) and, hence, the intermediate slope
\gamma
for cases that follow a double broken power law.
Therefore, the quadratic
and linear-constant toy models provide the simplest analytical
profiles to reproduce both asymptotic limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
,
as we illustrate in Figs.
3
and
4
.
Throughout the rest of the paper, we study the deviation of the results computed
with the full numerical profiles
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
(see Fig.
1
)
with respect to the results obtained using the toy models.
In particular, Fig.
3
compares the results for the function

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)/(zf_{0}^{\prime})^{2}
.
We find great agreement between the full profiles and the
toy models of Fig.
7
.

Figure 7
:

Numerical fluid profiles (solid lines) compared to the analytical toy models (dashed lines).
The quadratic profiles of Eq. (
2.20
) with one
or two discontinuities
are used as toy models
for subsonic deflagrations (see blue and green lines)
and detonations (see orange lines).
For hybrids, the linear-constant profile of Eq. (
2.27
) is used (see brown lines).

2.5.1
Quadratic toy model for subsonic deflagrations and detonations

Let us consider a generic quadratic velocity profile,
continuous in the range
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
,

\displaystyle v_{\rm quad}(\xi)=(C_{2}\,\xi^{2}+C_{1}\,\xi+C_{0})\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,

(2.20)

with
\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\equiv\Theta(\xi-\xi_{\rm b})-\Theta(\xi-\xi_{\rm f})

being the characteristic function of the interval
(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
, and where
C_{i}\,(i=0,1,2)
are free parameters.
Note that constant and linear velocity profiles can be recovered by taking
C_{2}=0

and further taking
C_{1}=0
for the constant case.
The corresponding
f^{\prime}(z)
has an analytical expression that can be obtained
from Eq. (
2.9
) as

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}_{\rm quad}(z)}{4\pi}=

\displaystyle\,C_{2}\biggl[\frac{\xi^{3}\sin(z\xi)}{z^{2}}+\frac{4\xi^{2}\cos(z\xi)}{z^{3}}-\frac{8\xi\sin(z\xi)}{z^{4}}-\frac{8\cos(z\xi)}{z^{5}}\biggr]_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}

\displaystyle\,+C_{1}\biggl[\frac{\xi^{2}\sin(z\xi)}{z^{2}}+\frac{3\xi\cos(z\xi)}{z^{3}}-\frac{3\sin(z\xi)}{z^{4}}\biggr]_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}+C_{0}\biggl[\frac{\xi\sin(z\xi)}{z^{2}}+\frac{2\cos(z\xi)}{z^{3}}\biggr]_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\,,

(2.21)

where each term in brackets corresponds to subtracting its value evaluated
at
\xi_{\rm b}
from its value evaluated at
\xi_{\rm f}
.
From Eq. (
2.21
), we
have the following limits for
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\rm quad}(z)

\displaystyle=-4\pi\,\biggl[\frac{\xi^{4}}{3}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{6}C_{2}\,\xi^{2}+\tfrac{1}{5}C_{1}\,\xi+\tfrac{1}{4}C_{0}\bigr)\biggr]_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}z+\mathcal{O}(z^{3})\quad\text{for }z\to 0\,,

(2.22a)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\rm quad}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi}{z^{2}}\Bigl[-\xi\sin(z\xi)\,v_{\rm quad}(\xi)\Bigr]_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-3})\quad\text{for }z\to\infty\,,

(2.22b)

which, as expected, follow the same power laws as the ones of
f^{\prime}(z)

[see Eqs. (
2.10
) and (
2.14
)].
Note that in the expansion around
z\to 0
of Eq. (
2.22a
),
all divergent terms in Eq. (
2.21
)
of order
z^{-n}
with
n>0
cancel out.

Since we want to match the
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
limits
to the numerical
velocity profile
f^{\prime}(z)
, using Eqs. (
2.10
) and (
2.14b
),
we require the following conditions,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{6}C_{2}\,(\xi_{\rm f}^{6}-\xi_{\rm b}^{6})+\tfrac{1}{5}C_{1}\,(\xi_{\rm f}^{5}-\xi_{\rm b}^{5})+\tfrac{1}{4}C_{0}(\xi_{\rm f}^{4}-\xi_{\rm b}^{4})=\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(2.23a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\rm b}\sin(z\xi_{\rm b})\,v_{\rm quad}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})-\xi_{\rm f}\sin(z\xi_{\rm f})\,v_{\rm quad}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt=\xi_{w}\sin(z\xi_{w})\Delta v_{\rm ip}(\xi_{w})+\xi_{\rm sh}\sin(z\xi_{\rm sh})\Delta v_{\rm ip}(\xi_{\rm sh})\,.

(2.23b)

A quadratic toy model, characterized by
the constants
C_{i}

and by the positions of
\xi_{\rm b}
and
\xi_{\rm f}

can be defined to meet the conditions in Eqs. (
2.23
) for a
generic profile that is continuous in
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
(i.e.,
subsonic deflagrations and detonations).
In particular, we can determine two of the three constants by imposing
that
v_{\rm quad}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})=v_{\rm b}
and

v_{\rm quad}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{\rm f}
,
where
v_{\rm b}
and
v_{\rm f}
are the
values of the velocity profile at

\xi_{\rm b}
and
\xi_{\rm f}
, respectively
(see App.
A
).
One possible choice respecting such conditions is

\displaystyle v_{\rm quad}(\xi)=\biggl[v_{\rm f}-(v_{\rm f}-v_{\rm b})\frac{(\xi_{\rm f}-\xi)(\xi-\xi_{\rm A})}{(\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b})(\xi_{\rm b}-\xi_{\rm A})}\biggr]\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,

(2.24)

where we have introduced a third constant
\xi_{\rm A}
as one of the two zeros
(together with
\xi_{\rm f}
) of the polynomial
v_{\rm quad}(\xi)-v_{f}
.
Equation (
2.24
) corresponds to Eq. (
2.20
) with the choice

C_{2}=\frac{v_{\rm f}-v_{\rm b}}{(\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b})(\xi_{\rm b}-\xi_{\rm A})}\,,\qquad C_{1}=-(\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm A})\,C_{2}\,,\qquad C_{0}=v_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm f}\xi_{\rm A}\,C_{2}\,.

(2.25)

This automatically satisfies Eq. (
2.23b
).
Then, we
use Eq. (
2.23a
) to determine the remaining constant,
\xi_{\rm A}
,

\displaystyle\xi_{\rm A}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi_{\rm b}\,\Delta\xi\biggl[\displaystyle\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi-\tfrac{1}{4}(\xi_{\rm f}^{4}-\xi_{\rm b}^{4})v_{\rm f}\biggr]+(v_{\rm b}-v_{\rm f})\bigl[\tfrac{1}{6}(\xi_{\rm f}^{6}-\xi_{\rm b}^{6})-\tfrac{1}{5}\xi_{\rm f}(\xi_{\rm f}^{5}-\xi_{\rm b}^{5})\bigr]}{\Delta\xi\biggl[\displaystyle\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi-\tfrac{1}{4}(\xi_{\rm f}^{4}-\xi_{\rm b}^{4})v_{\rm f}\biggr]+(v_{\rm b}-v_{\rm f})\bigl[\tfrac{1}{5}(\xi_{\rm f}^{5}-\xi_{\rm b}^{5})-\tfrac{1}{4}\xi_{\rm f}(\xi_{\rm f}^{4}-\xi_{\rm b}^{4})\bigr]}\,.

(2.26)

The toy model of Eq. (
2.24
) applies to subsonic deflagrations, which have in general
two discontinuities at
\xi_{\rm b}
and
\xi_{\rm f}
.
For
\xi_{w}\lesssim\frac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}
, the velocity profile
continuously goes to zero at
\xi_{\rm f}
, hence

v_{\rm f}\to 0
as
\xi_{\rm f}\to c_{\rm s}
, while still presenting
a discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm b}
.
This model also applies to detonations, which take a value
v_{\rm f}
at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
,
and continuously go to zero at
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
.
In Figs.
3
and
4
, we show that the quadratic profiles provide an accurate modeling
of the numerical results for the
asymptotic limits and also
captures the oscillations
in
z
.
This is true for all detonations and subsonic deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{4}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
.
For smaller values of
\xi_{w}
(e.g.,
see
\xi_{w}=0.1
in Fig.
3
), we find slight
discrepancies around
z_{1}
between the numerical and the toy model results.
This is potentially due to
the fact that the
quadratic profile becomes
negative in this range of
\xi_{w}
.
This could be improved using a cubic profile.
However, this does not seem to considerably affect our results, so we restrict
our analysis to the use of quadratic toy models for simplicity.

2.5.2
Linear-constant toy model for hybrids

For hybrids, the velocity profile has two discontinuities: one
at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
and another one at
\xi_{w}\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
,
where
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}<\xi_{w}
.
Hence, hybrids are discontinuous within the fluid shell and
we cannot use the profile in Eq. (
2.20
).
As we show in App.
B
,
the simplest alternative is
a linear-constant (lc) toy model,

\displaystyle v_{\rm lc}(\xi)=v_{\rm A}\frac{\xi-\xi_{\rm b}}{\xi_{w}-\xi_{\rm b}}\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{w})}(\xi)+v_{\rm B}\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{w},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,

(2.27)

where
\xi_{\rm b}
and
\xi_{\rm f}
are now chosen to be
c_{\rm s}
and
\xi_{\rm sh}
of the full profile.
This profile corresponds to the sum of
a linear profile defined in
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{w})

with
C_{2}=0
,
C_{1}=v_{\rm A}/(\xi_{w}-\xi_{\rm b})
, and
C_{0}=-\xi_{\rm b}C_{1}
, and
a constant profile with
C_{0}=v_{\rm B}
in
\xi\in(\xi_{w},\xi_{\rm f})
,
presenting hence a discontinuity at
\xi_{w}
.
Using Eq. (
2.21
),
we find the following function
f^{\prime}(z)
for the linear-constant profile,

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}_{\rm lc}(z)}{4\pi}=

\displaystyle\,v_{\rm A}\biggl[\frac{\xi_{w}\,\sin(z\xi_{w})}{z^{2}}+\frac{(3\xi_{w}-2\xi_{\rm b})\cos(z\xi_{w})-\xi_{\rm b}\cos(z\xi_{\rm b})}{z^{3}(\xi_{w}-\xi_{\rm b})}+3\,\frac{\sin(z\xi_{\rm b})-\sin(z\xi_{w})}{z^{4}(\xi_{w}-\xi_{\rm b})}\biggr]

\displaystyle\,+v_{\rm B}\biggl[\frac{\xi_{\rm sh}\sin(z\xi_{\rm sh})-\xi_{w}\sin(z\xi_{w})}{z^{2}}+\frac{2}{z^{3}}\bigl[\cos(z\xi_{\rm sh})-\cos(z\xi_{w})\bigr]\biggr]\,.

(2.28)

In the
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
limits, it becomes

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\rm lc}(z)=

\displaystyle\,\pi\biggl[\frac{v_{\rm A}}{15}\,(\xi_{\rm b}^{4}+\xi_{\rm b}^{3}\xi_{w}+\xi_{\rm b}^{2}\xi_{w}^{2}+\xi_{w}^{3}\xi_{\rm b}-4\xi_{w}^{4})-\frac{v_{\rm B}}{3}(\xi_{\rm sh}^{4}-\xi_{w}^{4})\biggr]\,z+\mathcal{O}(z^{3})\quad\text{for $z\to 0$}\,,

(2.29a)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\rm lc}(z)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{4\pi}{z^{2}}\,\bigl(v_{\rm A}\,\xi_{w}\sin(z\xi_{w})+v_{\rm B}[\xi_{\rm sh}\sin(z\xi_{\rm sh})-\xi_{w}\sin(z\xi_{w})]\bigr)+\mathcal{O}(z^{-3})\qquad\,\text{for $z\to\infty$}\,.

(2.29b)

Hence, in order to reproduce the
z\to 0
limit in Eq. (
2.10
)
and the
z\to\infty
limit in Eq. (
2.14b
),
we have to simultaneously satisfy the conditions

\displaystyle v_{\rm B}(\xi_{\rm sh}^{4}-\xi_{w}^{4})-\frac{v_{\rm A}}{5}\,(\xi_{\rm b}^{4}+\xi_{\rm b}^{3}\xi_{w}+\xi_{\rm b}^{2}\xi_{w}^{2}+\xi_{w}^{3}\xi_{\rm b}-4\xi_{w}^{4})=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(2.30a)

\displaystyle(v_{\rm B}-v_{\rm A})\,\xi_{w}\sin(z\xi_{w})-v_{\rm B}\xi_{\text{sh}}\sin(z\xi_{\text{sh}})=\xi_{w}\,\sin(z\xi_{w})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})+\xi_{\text{sh}}\,\sin(z\xi_{\text{sh}})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})\,.

(2.30b)

If we want to reproduce the full oscillations
of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, we have to choose
v_{\rm B}=-\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})=v_{\text{sh}}

and
v_{\rm A}=-\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})-\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})=v_{\text{sh}}-(v_{+}-v_{-})
.
However, this choice does not allow to reproduce the
z\to 0
limit of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

since Eq. (
2.30a
) is then not generally satisfied.
Hence, we choose to reproduce only the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in the
z\to\infty
limit, for which, using Eq. (
2.15
),
the condition in Eq. (
2.30b
) becomes

\displaystyle|v_{\rm B}-v_{\rm A}|\xi_{w}+v_{\rm B}\xi_{\rm sh}=\xi_{\rm sh}|\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})|+\xi_{w}\,|\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})|\,.

(2.31)

We then find the following choice for
v_{\rm B}
and
v_{\rm A}
(assuming
that
v_{\rm B}>v_{\rm A}
),

\displaystyle v_{\rm A}

\displaystyle=\frac{5(\xi_{\rm sh}-\xi_{w})(\xi_{\rm sh}^{2}+\xi_{w}^{2})[\xi_{\rm sh}v_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}\,(v_{+}-v_{-})]-20\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi^{3}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi}{\xi_{\rm b}^{4}+\xi_{\rm b}^{3}\xi_{w}+\xi_{\rm b}^{2}\xi_{w}^{2}+\xi_{w}^{3}\xi_{\rm b}-4\xi_{w}^{4}-\xi_{w}\xi_{\text{sh}}^{3}-\xi_{w}^{3}\xi_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}^{2}\xi_{\text{sh}}^{2}+\xi_{w}^{4}}\,,

(2.32a)

\displaystyle v_{\rm B}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi_{\rm sh}v_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}\,(v_{+}-v_{-})+v_{\rm A}\xi_{w}}{\xi_{\rm sh}+\xi_{w}}\,,

(2.32b)

which satisfies both Eqs. (
2.30a
) and (
2.31
)
at the same time.
Adding a linear profile in the range
\xi\in(\xi_{w},\xi_{\rm f})
would
allow us to recover the same oscillations at large
z
than the
original profile if we set the same values of the velocity jumps
at the discontinuities.
In this case, we would have
three free parameters:
v_{\rm A}=v(\xi_{w}^{-})
,
v_{\rm B}=v(\xi_{w}^{+})
,
and
v_{\rm C}=v(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})
.
Hence, to reproduce the full
z\to\infty
limit,
we would need to impose
v_{\rm C}=v_{\rm ip}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})

and
v_{\rm B}-v_{\rm A}=v_{+}-v_{-}
.
We would then still have
the freedom to choose
v_{\rm B}+v_{\rm A}
such that the
z\to 0
limit is reproduced.
This is possible, but it would involve a more complicated determination of the
toy model parameters.
Since for the final results in Sec.
5
, recovering the exact
oscillations at large
z
is not essential
to reproduce the slopes and the breaks
of the velocity spectrum, we use the simpler linear-constant toy model.

3
Two-point correlation of the velocity field before collisions

3.1
In coordinate space

The total velocity field is the linear superposition of the field induced by each of the
N_{b}
expanding bubbles in the volume
V
, as described in Eq. (
2.5
).
Its two-point correlation function

B_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})=\langle v_{i}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,v_{j}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})\rangle

can thus be written as,

\displaystyle B_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})\equiv\Biggl\langle\,\sum_{n,m=1}^{N_{b}}v_{i}^{(n)}\Bigl(t-t_{0}^{(n)},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}\Bigr)\,v_{j}^{(m)}\Bigl(t-t_{0}^{(m)},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}_{0}^{(m)}\Bigr)\!\Biggr\rangle\,,

(3.1)

where the brackets indicate an ensemble average over the nucleation locations

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}_{0}^{(m)}
, and the nucleation times
t_{0}^{(n,m)}
.
Since we
assume a homogeneous statistical distribution of the nucleation locations,
the resultant velocity field at any time follows a
homogeneous and isotropic distribution.
Indeed, we note that, even though each realization of
the velocity field produced by a distribution of bubbles is localized and has a preferred direction at any given point,
it becomes homogeneous and isotropic
after averaging over all possible nucleation locations
when they are homogeneously distributed in space.
We present general properties of statistically homogeneous and isotropic fields in App.
C
.

The velocity fields induced by bubbles nucleated
at different
locations are uncorrelated, and the ensemble average over
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}_{0}^{(m)}

is non-trivial only if
n=m
.
Therefore, in the rest of the paper, we use the notation

B_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})=n_{b}(t)\,\langle V\tilde{B}_{ij}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})\rangle_{t_{0}}\,,

(3.2)

where the sum in Eq. (
3.1
) is replaced by the average number density of
bubbles,
n_{b}(t)
, defined in Eq. (
D.3
), and

\tilde{B}_{ij}
corresponds to the integral over nucleation locations in
Eq. (
3.1
) divided by the volume
V
where bubbles can be nucleated,

\tilde{B}_{ij}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})=\frac{1}{V}\int v_{i}^{(n)}\Bigl(t-t_{0}^{(n)},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}\Bigr)\,v_{j}^{(n)}\Bigl(t-t_{0}^{(n)},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}\Bigr){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}\,.

(3.3)

Since
\tilde{B}_{ij}
is the same for all bubbles, we omit from now on the superscript
(n)
in the velocity
\textstyle v
,
and in the nucleation location and time,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}
and
t_{0}
.
The average over nucleation times
t_{0}

is studied in Sec.
3.3
, while in App.
D

we review the basic ingredients of the
exponential and simultaneous
distributions
of nucleation times that are
commonly chosen in the literature.

Tensor structure

Due to statistical homogeneity and isotropy,
the two-point correlation function of the velocity field at
the points
\textstyle x
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}\equiv\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}
,
introduced in Eq. (
3.2
),
has the following tensor structure,

B_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})=(\delta_{ij}-\hat{r}_{i}\hat{r}_{j})B_{\rm N}(t,r)+\hat{r}_{i}\hat{r}_{j}B_{\rm L}(t,r)\,,

(3.4)

where
B_{\rm L}(r)
and
B_{\rm N}(r)
are the longitudinal (L) and normal (N) components of the correlation function [see Eq. (
C.1
)],
which depend uniquely on the radial distance
r=|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}|=|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}|
.
We note that the decomposition of any homogeneous
and isotropic field is identical, e.g.,
\tilde{B}_{ij}
can also be decomposed
into
\tilde{B}_{\rm L}(r)
and
\tilde{B}_{\rm N}(r)
using Eq. (
3.4
).

Normal and longitudinal components

We now compute the correlation function
\tilde{B}_{ij}
explicitly for a generic velocity profile by averaging over all possible nucleation locations
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}
.
The radial velocity field induced by an expanding bubble is

v_{i}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\,\hat{\xi}_{x,i}

with

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x}=(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})/(t-t_{0})
,
\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}}{}_{x}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x}/\xi_{x}
, and
\xi_{x}=|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x}|
,
where the self-similar velocity profile induced by an expanding bubble is
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
.
For a generic profile,
\tilde{B}_{ij}
, given in Eq. (
3.3
),
can be expressed as

\tilde{b}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r})\equiv\frac{V\tilde{B}_{ij}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})}{(t-t_{0})^{3}}=\int v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})\,\hat{\xi}_{x,i}\,\hat{\xi}_{y,j}{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}\,,

(3.5)

where we have defined the additional self-similar variables
