\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{y}\equiv(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})/(t-t_{0})
,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r}\equiv\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}/(t-t_{0})
, and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}\equiv\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}/(t-t_{0})
.
A similar calculation was carried out in ref.
[
24
]
for
a linear velocity profile.
We extend their analysis to a generic radial profile
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
.

To identify the relevant integration domain, we observe that the correlation
between the velocity fields at the points
\textstyle x
and
\textstyle y
is non-zero only if, at the time
t
, both
past lightcones of
\textstyle x
and
\textstyle y
intersect with the compact support of the
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}

profile sourced by the bubble nucleated at
(t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})
.
Hence, the integral
over nucleation locations is computed
over this intersection volume,
V_{i}
, and normalized by the total volume
V\supset V_{i}
.
The volume
V
in the normalization does not affect
the final result,

B_{ij}
, given that

n_{b}(t)=N_{b}(t)/V
in Eq. (
3.2
).
Reference
[
24
]
normalized the integral in Eq. (
3.5
) by the intersection volume
V_{i}
instead of the total volume.
However, as we show below, if the normalizing volume depends on the distance
r
, the normal spectral density
\tilde{F}_{\rm N}
before the average over nucleation times is non-zero,
which is
in contradiction with the result expected for irrotational fields (see Sec.
C.3
and ref.
[
91
]
).
This is resolved if one would then correct the volume
V\to V_{i}(r)

in Eq. (
3.2
) used to define
n_{b}(t)
, getting rid of the additional

r
dependence such that the final spectral density
F_{\rm N}

would become zero even if
\tilde{F}_{\rm N}
is not (see discussion in Sec.
3.2
).

Figure 8
:

Example of contributing intersection of two light-cones, centered respectively at

\textstyle x
and
\textstyle y
, for the evaluation of the integral in Eq. (
3.5
).
We use self-similar variables

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x}\equiv(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})/(t-t_{0})
,

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{y}\equiv(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0})/(t-t_{0})
,

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r}\equiv\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}/(t-t_{0})
,
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}/(t-t_{0})
.
We choose a cylindrical coordinate system
such that a point
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}

can be identified with coordinates

(x\equiv\xi_{\rho}\cos\theta,y\equiv\xi_{z},z\equiv\xi_{\rho}\sin\theta)
.
The intersection
volume
V_{i}
is found by rotating the surface
of revolution
S_{i}
(gray shaded area)
an angle
\theta\in(0,2\pi)
around the
\xi_{z}
axis.
The distance of
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}
from the origin is
\sqrt{\xi_{\rho}^{2}+\xi_{z}^{2}}
.

The system of coordinates used to parametrize
V_{i}
and integrate Eq. (
3.5
)
analytically is sketched in Fig.
8
.
Specifically, we introduce self-similar cylindrical coordinates

(\xi_{\rho},\xi_{z},\theta)
and set the origin
at the intermediate point between
\textstyle x
and
\textstyle y
,
being
\xi_{\rho}
and
\xi_{z}
the distance to the origin
projected, respectively, in the perpendicular and parallel
directions to the vector
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r}
, as indicated in Fig.
8
,
while
\theta\in(0,2\pi)

is the azimuthal angle
in the plane perpendicular to
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{r}
.
In this system of coordinates, we write

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}=(\xi_{\rho}\cos\theta,\xi_{z},\xi_{\rho}\sin\theta)\,,

\displaystyle\quad{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}=\,\xi_{\rho}{\rm\,d}\xi_{\rho}{\rm\,d}\xi_{z}{\rm\,d}\theta\,,

(3.6a)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{x}=-(\xi_{\rho}\cos\theta,\xi_{z}+{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{r},\xi_{\rho}\sin\theta)\,,

\displaystyle\quad{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{y}=-(\xi_{\rho}\cos\theta,\xi_{z}-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{r},\xi_{\rho}\sin\theta)\,.

(3.6b)

Since the volume
V_{i}
is axisymmetric, we can take the integral over
\theta\in(0,2\pi)
,
and obtain

\tilde{b}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r})=2\pi\int_{S_{i}}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{\rho}{\rm\,d}\xi_{z}\,\xi_{\rho}\frac{v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})}{\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{\rho}^{2}&0&0\\
0&\xi_{z}^{2}-{\textstyle{1\over 4}}\xi_{r}^{2}&0\\
0&0&{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{\rho}^{2}\end{array}\right)\,,

(3.7)

where
S_{i}
is the surface of revolution, corresponding to the gray shaded area
in Fig.
8
,
that produces the volume
V_{i}
by a full rotation in the azimuthal direction.
The contributing matrix components of
\tilde{b}_{ij}

are only diagonals because
the off-diagonal terms drop
thanks to the integral over
\theta
. They can be identified as

\tilde{b}_{11}=\tilde{b}_{33}=\tilde{b}_{\rm N}

and

\tilde{b}_{22}=\tilde{b}_{\rm L}
.
The symmetry of the integrand for
\xi_{z}\to-\xi_{z}

allows us to express it as
twice the integral over the region
\xi_{z}\in[0,1-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}]
,

\xi_{\rho}^{2}\in[0,1-({\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}+\xi_{z})^{2}]
.

Finally, we change integration variables to
\xi_{x}
and
\xi_{y}
using
the following relations,

\xi_{\rho}^{2}(\xi_{x},\xi_{y})={\textstyle{1\over 2}}(\xi_{x}^{2}+\xi_{y}^{2}-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{r}^{2}-2\xi_{z}^{2})\,,\qquad\xi_{z}(\xi_{x},\xi_{y})=\frac{\xi_{x}^{2}-\xi_{y}^{2}}{2{\xi_{r}}}\,,

(3.8)

such that

{\xi_{r}}\,\xi_{\rho}{\rm\,d}\xi_{\rho}{\rm\,d}\xi_{z}=\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}
.
Then,
the region of integration corresponds to

\xi_{x}\in\bigl[{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}},1\bigr]
,
\xi_{y}\in[|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|,\xi_{x}]
,
where the lower bound of
\xi_{y}
can be understood
from the triangular inequality applied to the triangle
formed by the vectors
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x}
,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{y}
, and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{r}

(see Fig.
8
),
and Eq. (
3.7
) can be written, using the decomposition in Eq. (
3.4
), as

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{\rm N,L}({\xi_{r}})=\frac{2\pi}{{\xi_{r}}}\int_{{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}}^{1}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\int_{|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|}^{\xi_{x}}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})\,I_{\rm N,L}(\xi_{x},\xi_{y})\,,

(3.9)

with
I_{\rm N}({\xi_{x}},{\xi_{y}})=\xi_{\rho}^{2}
and
I_{\rm L}({\xi_{x}},{\xi_{y}})=2\xi_{z}^{2}-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{r}^{2}
.
Since the change of variables is applicable under the condition
\xi_{r}\neq 0
,
we stress that Eq. (
3.9
) is not
valid in the limit
\xi_{r}\to 0
, where
instead we find

\tilde{b}_{\rm N}(0)=\tilde{b}_{\rm L}(0)={\textstyle{1\over 3}}\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
[see Eq. (
C.15
)].
We note that the results of App.
C
apply to any
statistically homogeneous and isotropic field.
Figure
9
shows the normalized

\tilde{b}_{\rm N,L}(\xi_{r})
for benchmark phase transitions with

\alpha=0.1
, compared to the correlation functions
found using the toy models of Sec.
2.5
.
The causal decay to zero at distances
{\xi_{r}}\geq 2\,\xi_{\rm f}
is clearly visible.

Figure 9
:

Longitudinal (left) and normal (right) components of the correlation
function
\tilde{b}_{\rm N,L}(\xi_{r})
normalized by their values at
\xi_{r}=0
,

{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
.
As a benchmark,
we have used
\alpha=0.1
.
The normal component,

\tilde{b}_{\rm N}
, is always positive,
but the longitudinal one,
\tilde{b}_{\rm L}
,
can become negative.
Both correlation functions
vanish at the distance
\xi_{r}=2\,\xi_{\rm f}\leq 2
(vertical dotted lines),
as expected from causality.
The correlation functions found
from
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
(solid lines) are compared to those found
using the toy models (dashed lines) presented in Sec.
2.5

(i.e., quadratic for subsonic deflagrations and detonations,
and linear-constant for hybrids).

Components relation for irrotational fields

If the velocity field is purely irrotational, i.e.,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}={\boldsymbol{0}}
,
the components of the corresponding correlation function satisfy
the following differential equation (see Sec.
C.3
and ref.
[
91
]
),

\tilde{b}_{\rm N}(\xi_{r})+\xi_{r}\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}\tilde{b}_{\rm N}(\xi_{r})=\tilde{b}_{\rm L}(\xi_{r})\,.

(3.10)

With Eq. (
3.9
) at hand, we can verify if this is the case computing

{\xi_{r}}\,\partial_{{\xi_{r}}}\tilde{b}_{\rm N}({\xi_{r}})=2\pi\,\partial_{{\xi_{r}}}\Biggl[\int_{{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}}^{1}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\int_{|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|}^{\xi_{x}}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})\,I_{\rm N}({\xi_{x}},{\xi_{y}})\Biggr]-\tilde{b}_{\rm N}({\xi_{r}})\,.

(3.11)

Equation (
3.11
) can be rearranged into

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{\rm N}({\xi_{r}})+{\xi_{r}}\,\partial_{{\xi_{r}}}\tilde{b}_{\rm N}({\xi_{r}})=-2\pi\int_{{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}}^{1}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\Biggl[

\displaystyle v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(|{\xi_{r}}-\xi_{x}|)\,I_{\rm N}(\xi_{x},|{\xi_{r}}-\xi_{x}|)\,{\rm sgn}({\xi_{r}}-\xi_{x})

\displaystyle-\int_{|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|}^{\xi_{x}}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}I_{\rm N}({\xi_{x}},{\xi_{y}})\Biggr]\,.

(3.12)

Setting
I_{\rm N}=\xi_{\rho}^{2}
we find that the boundary term vanishes
since
\xi_{y}=|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|
implies
\xi_{\rho}=0
,
while for the
second term we can use
{\xi_{r}}\partial_{\xi_{r}}I_{\rm N}=2\xi_{z}^{2}-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\xi_{r}^{2}=I_{\rm L}
.
Hence, using these results in Eq. (
3.1
), we find that indeed
Eq. (
3.10
) is satisfied.
Equations (
3.11
) and (
3.1
) hold also after changing N
\,\leftrightarrow\,
L.

As discussed in the paragraph after Eq. (
3.5
),
using
V_{i}(\xi_{r})
as the normalization volume in Eq. (
3.5
)
would spoil this result, since then

{\xi_{r}}\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}\tilde{b}_{\rm N}\to V_{i}^{-1}\,{\xi_{r}}\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}\tilde{b}_{\rm N}-V_{i}^{-2}\,{\xi_{r}}\,\tilde{b}_{\rm N}\,\partial_{\xi_{r}}V_{i}
.
This is what is found in ref.
[
24
]
,
where Eq. (
3.10
) is not satisfied.
However, it would then be required to accordingly define

n_{b}(t)=N_{b}(t)/V_{i}(\xi_{r})
in Eq. (
3.2
),
such that
the resulting correlation function
B_{ij}

is independent of

V_{i}
and the analogous of Eq. (
3.10
)
is satisfied for the
B_{\rm N}(r)
and
B_{\rm L}(r)
components.
Here, we rather we use the generic volume
V\supset V_{i}
, independent
of
\xi_{r}
, leading to the expected relation between the longitudinal and
normal components of
\tilde{b}_{ij}
as given in Eq. (
3.10
).

3.2
In momentum space

Because of the statistical homogeneity and isotropy
of the velocity field,
its spectral density

{\cal F}_{ij}
, defined from the correlation function
in Fourier space as

\displaystyle\bigl\langle v_{i}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v_{j}^{*}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,{\cal F}_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,,

(3.13)

has the following tensor structure
[
91
]

{\cal F}_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=(\delta_{ij}-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j})F_{\rm N}(t,k)+\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}F_{\rm L}(t,k)\,,

(3.14)

where the spectral density is decomposed into longitudinal (
F_{\rm L}
)
and normal (
F_{\rm N}
) components [see Eq. (
C.3
)].
In analogy to Eq. (
3.5
), we denote the two-point function averaged
over nucleation locations with a tilde,

\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}
,

{\cal F}_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=n_{b}(t)\bigl\langle V\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\bigr\rangle_{t_{0}}\,.

(3.15)

The decomposition of
\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}

into
\tilde{F}_{\rm L}
and
\tilde{F}_{\rm N}

follows again directly from Eq. (
3.14
).
As we consider
statistically homogeneous and isotropic irrotational
fields, the normal component of the spectral density vanishes
[
91
]
.
Indeed, re-expressing
\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}
using Eq. (
2.8
), we find

\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=\frac{1}{V}\int v_{i}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v_{j}^{\ast}(t-t_{0},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime}){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}=\frac{(2\pi)^{3}}{V}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,\hat{k}_{i}\,\hat{k}_{j}(t-t_{0})^{6}{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\,,

(3.16)

where
z=k(t-t_{0})
.
Comparing Eq. (
3.16
) with the general decomposition in Eq. (
3.14
) applied to
\tilde{\cal F}_{ij}
, we directly
find
\tilde{f}_{\rm N}(z)=0
and

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)\equiv\frac{V\,\tilde{F}_{\rm L}(t-t_{0},k)}{(t-t_{0})^{6}}={f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\,.

(3.17)

This longitudinal spectral density was directly computed in momentum
space in refs.
[
64
,
60
]
.
Alternatively, it can be computed from
\tilde{b}_{\rm N}
and
\tilde{b}_{\rm L}
using Eq. (
C.13
),

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!{\rm\,d}{\xi_{r}}\,\xi_{r}^{2}\,j_{0}(z{\xi_{r}})\bigl[2\,\tilde{b}_{\rm N}({\xi_{r}})+\tilde{b}_{\rm L}({\xi_{r}})\bigr]\,.

(3.18)

Using Eq. (
3.9
),

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)=\frac{8\pi^{2}}{z}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!{\rm\,d}{\xi_{r}}\,{\xi_{r}}\sin(z{\xi_{r}})\int_{{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}}}^{1}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\int_{|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|}^{\xi_{x}}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})(\xi_{x}^{2}+\xi_{y}^{2}-\xi_{r}^{2})\,.

(3.19)

The region of integration
\xi_{r}\in(0,\infty)
,
\xi_{x}\in\bigl[{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\xi_{r}},1\bigr]
,

\xi_{y}\in[|\xi_{r}-\xi_{x}|,\xi_{x}]
, extended back to the region of points with
\xi_{z}<0
,
is equivalent to

\xi_{x}
,
\xi_{y}\in(0,\infty)
,
{\xi_{r}}\in[|\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}|,\xi_{x}+\xi_{y}]
,

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{z}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x})\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!{\rm\,d}\xi_{y}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y})\int_{|\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}|}^{\xi_{x}+\xi_{y}}\!\!{\rm\,d}{\xi_{r}}\,(\xi_{x}^{2}+\xi_{y}^{2}-\xi_{r}^{2})\sin(z{\xi_{r}})\,.

(3.20)

The integral over
{\xi_{r}}
yields

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}|}^{\xi_{x}+\xi_{y}}\!\!{\rm\,d}{\xi_{r}}\,\sin(z{\xi_{r}})(\xi_{x}^{2}+\xi_{y}^{2}-\xi_{r}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{z^{3}}\Bigl[\sin(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})+z^{2}\,\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}\cos(z\xi_{x})\cos(z\xi_{y})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=
\frac{4}{z^{3}} \Bigl[}-z\,\xi_{x}\cos(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})-z\,\xi_{y}\sin(z\xi_{x})\cos(z\xi_{y})\Bigr]\,.

(3.21)

Substituting Eq. (
3.21
) into Eq. (
3.20
), we find that the integral
becomes symmetric in
\xi_{x}
and
\xi_{y}
, and
separable, leading to

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)={f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
[see Eq. (
2.9
)].
Therefore, after correcting the normalization volume used for the average in physical space, the approach of ref.
[
24
]

leads to a correlation function that is compatible with the
longitudinal spectral density
\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)={f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
found
in ref.
[
64
]
, as expected.

The exact
k
dependence of

F_{\rm L}(t,k)=n_{b}(t)\langle V\tilde{F}_{\rm L}(t-t_{0},k)\rangle_{t_{0}}

requires a description of the ensemble average over nucleation times
t_{0}
,
which is addressed in Sec.
3.3
.
However, the asymptotic limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
become simple power laws in
z=k(t-t_{0})
[see Eqs. (
2.10
) and (
2.14a
)].
Therefore, after performing the average over nucleation times
t_{0}
at fixed
t
, one finds again the same power laws for the asymptotic
k\to 0
and
k\to\infty
of
F_{\rm L}(k)
and

\tilde{F}_{\rm L}(k)
: they
scale proportionally to
k^{2}
and
k^{-4}
, respectively.

The non-zero component of the spectral density,

F_{\rm L}(t,k)
, decays as
k^{2}
or steeper in the
k\to 0
limit,
due to the velocity field
being irrotational and causal
[
91
,
60
]
(see
discussion in Sec.
C.3
).
In our case, the causality condition
is verified (see Fig.
9
) and, hence, Eq. (
2.10
) yields

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)={f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}\,z^{2}+\mathcal{O}(z^{4})\quad\text{for }z\to 0\,,

(3.22)

as shown in Fig.
3
.
As
z\to\infty
, we find
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\sim z^{-4}
numerically (see Fig.
3
),
and analytically for a generic velocity profile
[see Eqs. (
2.14
)].
Hence,
F_{\rm L}(k)\to k^{-4}
in the
k\to\infty
limit.
This is a direct consequence of squaring the function
f^{\prime}(z)
,
whose asymptotic limit
z\to\infty
is determined by the
discontinuities
present in the velocity profile
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
.
Additionally, since
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)=\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)
is related
to the correlation functions
\tilde{b}_{\rm N,L}({\xi_{r}})

via Eq. (
3.18
),
this asymptotic limit should
be a consequence of the smoothness of
\tilde{b}_{\rm L}
.
Based on preliminary work,
we expect that
\tilde{f}_{\rm L}\sim z^{-4}
is a consequence
of
\tilde{b}_{\rm L}
(
\tilde{b}_{\rm N}
) being a function of class

{\cal C}^{1}
(
{\cal C}^{2}
), which
presents discontinuities in its second (third) derivative
with respect to
\xi_{r}
.

3.3
Ensemble average over nucleation times

Physical observables for the bubble ensemble,
\mathcal{O}
, are obtained after averaging
over the bubble nucleation history,

\mathcal{O}(t)=\langle\mathcal{\tilde{O}}(t,t_{0})\rangle_{t_{0}}\equiv\frac{1}{n_{b}(t)}\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})\,\mathcal{\tilde{O}}(t,t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }n_{b}(t)\equiv\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\,\Gamma(t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}\,,

(3.23)

where the time
t_{c}
marks the moment at which the critical temperature is reached,
while

\Gamma(t)=p(t)\,h(t)\,,

(3.24)

is the probability distribution in time, per unit volume, of nucleating a bubble,
with
p(t)
the probability per unit volume in the
symmetric phase
and
h(t)\in[0,1]
the volume fraction in
the symmetric phase,
which can be computed from
p(t)
as described in App.
D
using Eq. (
D.2
).

Hence, the correlation function in physical space becomes
[see Eqs. (
3.2
) and (
3.5
)]

B_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})=\int_{t_{c}}^{t}V\tilde{B}_{ij}\,\Gamma(t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}=\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}t_{0}\int v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{x})\,v_{j}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{y}){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}\,.

(3.25)

Similarly, in momentum space the components of the velocity
correlator

{\cal F}_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}F_{\rm L}(t,k)
,
given in
Eqs. (
3.14
–
3.17
), can be evaluated from
\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)={f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

by computing an average of single-bubble profiles over different nucleation times,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}(t,k)=n_{b}(t)\,\bigl\langle V\tilde{F}_{\rm L}(t-t_{0},k)\bigr\rangle_{t_{0}}=\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{6}\,\tilde{f}_{\rm L}\bigl[(t-t_{0})\,k\bigr]{\rm\,d}t_{0}\,.

(3.26)

We describe in App.
D
how
p(t)
and
h(t)
are obtained in
two particular choices for expanding
the Euclidean action,
S(t)
,
around a characteristic time
t_{*}
.
In particular,
when the action is a decreasing function of time,
a linear expansion around
the characteristic time
t_{\ast}

yields an exponentially increasing nucleation probability
distribution per unit volume,

p_{\rm exp}(t>t_{c})=p_{\ast}\exp[\beta(t-t_{\ast})]\,,

(3.27)

where
p_{*}
is given in Eq. (
D.12
) and
\beta\equiv-S^{\prime}(t_{\ast})

is the rate
that characterizes the inverse
time scale of the phase transition
(see Sec.
D.1
).
On the other hand,
a quadratic expansion
around a minimum of the Euclidean action
leads to a Gaussian probability distribution
in time (see Sec.
D.2
),

p_{\rm Gauss}(t>t_{c})=p_{\ast}\exp\bigl[-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\beta_{2}^{2}(t-t_{\ast})^{2}\bigr]\,,

(3.28)

where
p_{*}
depends on
\beta_{2}\equiv\sqrt{S^{\prime\prime}(t_{\ast})}

[see Eq. (
D.25
)].
In the limit of simultaneous nucleation,

p_{\rm Gauss}
can be approximated with a delta distribution,
as described in Sec.
D.2
,

p_{\rm sim}(t)=\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}p_{\rm Gauss}(t)=\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm sim}\delta(t-t_{\ast})\,.

(3.29)

Note that we can define a finite

\beta_{\rm eff}
, such that the asymptotic number of bubbles
\bar{n}_{b}

takes the same expression for both exponential [see Eq. (
D.16
)]
and simultaneous [see Eq. (
D.30
)] nucleations,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm exp}(t)=\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm exp}=\frac{\beta^{3}}{8\pi\xi_{w}^{3}}\,,\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm sim}(t)=\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm sim}=\frac{\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}}{8\pi\xi_{w}^{3}}\,.

(3.30)

A bar over

n_{b}
indicates that it is computed in the limit

\beta(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
or
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
, which imply that nucleation is assumed to occur far from the critical time.

The two cases presented above are commonly known as exponential and simultaneous nucleation,
and the timescale associated with the
phase transition is parametrized by the inverse of
\beta
and
\beta_{\rm eff}
,
respectively.
To streamline the notation, in the following we only use

\beta
for both cases.
Since the dependence on the time of nucleation
in
p(t)
and, hence,
\Gamma(t)
,
appears only in the dimensionless combination
\tilde{t}=\beta\,t
,
the result of the integral in Eq. (
3.26
)
is a function of
\tilde{t}
and
\tilde{k}=k/\beta
.
The dependence of
p_{\rm sim}(t)

on
\tilde{t}=\beta_{\rm eff}\,t
in Eq. (
3.29
) becomes apparent taking into
account that
\beta_{2}/\beta_{\rm eff}
is a constant, given in Eq. (
D.33
),
and that
\delta(\tilde{t})=\delta(t)/\beta_{\rm eff}
.

For the exponential and Gaussian models,
the function
\Gamma(\tilde{t})

depends thus on the choice of the characteristic time scale
\tilde{t}_{\ast}
,
which can be fixed by choosing the volume of the symmetric phase at this time,

h(\tilde{t}_{*})=h_{\ast}
, and
on the time interval
\tilde{t}_{*}-\tilde{t}_{c}
.
Figures
23
and
25
in App.
D

show numerical results for
\gamma
,
which is the normalized nucleation rate,

\gamma(\tilde{t})\equiv\frac{\Gamma({t})}{\beta\,n_{b}}\,,\qquad\text{ with \ }\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\tilde{t}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}=1\,,

(3.31)

for different values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
and
h_{\ast}
.
The distribution function
\gamma
is normalized with the asymptotic number density of bubbles,

n_{b}=n_{b}({\tilde{t}\to\infty})
.

Figure 10
:

Normalized probability distribution for exponential nucleation,

\gamma_{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})
, defined in Eq. (
3.31
),
as a function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
, with
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}+C_{\rm exp}
and
C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})
.
The plot shows the asymptotic
limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,
for which the functional dependence of
\gamma_{\rm exp}
with
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}

does not depend on the choice of
h_{\ast}

(see Sec.
D.1
for details).

In the following, we will restrict our
analysis to the case where
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,
such that we can effectively set
\tilde{t}_{c}\to-\infty

[
67
,
60
]
,
and, hence, we will omit the bar on
\bar{n}_{b}
from now on.
We will consider two choices of the characteristic time for
exponential nucleation,
h_{\ast}=1/e
and
h_{\ast}\simeq 0.71
.
The former choice takes
t_{\ast}
as the time when the rate of change
of the symmetric volume reaches its maximum
[
47
,
60
,
59
]
,
while the latter choice
takes
t_{\ast}
as the percolation time

[
10
,
32
]
(see details in Sec.
D.1
).
However, taking the limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,
the function
\gamma_{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})
only depends on
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
,
where
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}+C_{\rm exp}
indicates the time at
which
\gamma_{\rm exp}
reaches its peak value, with
C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})
,
as we show in App.
D
and in Fig.
10
.
Therefore, our results, when described
as functions of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
, apply to any generic choice of
h_{\ast}
.
For Gaussian nucleation, we will restrict our analysis to the simultaneous case,
which effectively assumes
h_{\ast}\to 1
and
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
(see details in Sec.
D.2
).

To single out the effects of the time dependence introduced by

\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)={f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
from the specific choice of the nucleation history in Eq. (
3.26
),
we introduce

\displaystyle A(\tilde{t})\equiv\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{6}{\rm\,d}t_{0}=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{6}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}\,,

(3.32)

and use it to define a normalized
{\tt F}_{\rm L}
from
F_{\rm L}
,

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})=A(\tilde{t})\,{\tt F}_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})\,.

(3.33)

The time evolution of the normalized power spectral density

{\tt F}_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})
is shown in Fig.
11
, while in Fig.
12
,
we provide the time evolution of the
normalization amplitude
A(\tilde{t})
(left panel) and the position
of the spectral peak

\tilde{k}_{*}(\tilde{t})
(right panel).
Since we consider the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
limit,
the resulting
functions depend solely on the time difference
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
.
We find that for both exponential
and simultaneous nucleation,
A(\tilde{t})
accurately captures the growth
of
F_{\rm L}
with time, such that the amplitude of
{\tt F}_{\rm L}\equiv F_{\rm L}/A

remains roughly constant
in all panels of Fig.
11
.
For the simultaneous case, when we compensate
\tilde{k}
with
\tilde{k}_{\ast}
, we find
that the spectrum collapses exactly into
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z/z_{\ast})
at all times,
meaning that the remaining time dependence is only due to the shift of
\tilde{k}=z/(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{*})
with time.
On the other hand,
for the exponential case, we observe some residual time dependence
on the normalized spectral shape
{\tt F}_{\rm L}(k/k_{\ast})
, which also eventually
reaches
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z/z_{\ast})
at late times.
In general, the ensemble average over nucleation times introduces an additional
scale
1/\beta
, embedded in our normalization
\tilde{k}\equiv k/\beta
, which
enters in determining the resulting length scales of the velocity spectrum, as we study in Sec.
5
.

Figure 11
:

Normalized power spectral density of the velocity field
in the bubble expansion phase,
{\tt F}_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})\equiv F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})/A(\tilde{t})
,
as a function of time
\tilde{t}
for the cases of
exponential (EXP, top panels) and simultaneous (SIM, bottom panels)
nucleation.
The power spectral density is shown as a function of
\tilde{k}
(left panels)
and the normalized
\tilde{k}/\tilde{k}_{\ast}
(right panels).
The evolution with time of the normalizing
function
A(\tilde{t})
[see Eq. (
3.32
)] and the position of the
peak
k_{\ast}(\tilde{t})
are shown in Fig.
12
.
For illustration, we take a benchmark phase transition with

\xi_{w}=0.4
and
\alpha=0.1
.
Green dotted lines in the right panels show
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z/z_{\ast})
,
with
z_{*}\simeq z_{1}
being the peak position of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
We see that in the exponential case,
the function
{\tt F}_{\rm L}(\tilde{k}/\tilde{k}_{\ast})

tends to
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z/z_{\ast})
in the
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}\gg 1
limit
and at all times in the simultaneous case.
The black solid lines indicate

\tilde{t}=\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{*}+C_{\rm exp}
in the case of exponential nucleation.

Figure 12
:

Time evolution of the function
A(\tilde{t})
[see Eq. (
3.32
)] (left panel)
and the spectral peak
\tilde{k}_{\ast}
(right panel) that characterize
the velocity power spectral density,
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})
,
presented in Fig.
11
,
for exponential nucleation with
h_{\ast}=1/e
(red lines) and

h_{\ast}=0.71
(orange lines),
and for simultaneous nucleation
(blue lines).
Both
A
and
\tilde{k}_{\ast}

are shown as a function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
, where

\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}+C
with
C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})

and
C_{\rm sim}=0
.
In the simultaneous case, we find

A=n_{b}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})^{6}\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})
and
\tilde{k}_{\ast}=z_{\ast}/(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})
, (note that
\tilde{k}_{*}
is defined only for
\tilde{t}\geq\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
).
In the exponential case, instead, the aforementioned behavior with
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
is only reached asymptotically at
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gtrsim 15
. The spectral peak of the
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
function
z_{\ast}
is approximately
the value of
z_{1}
investigated in Sec.
2.3
.

In the simultaneous case,
the normalized distribution function becomes
\gamma(\tilde{t})=\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})
,
hence,

\displaystyle A_{\rm sim}(t)=n_{b}(t-t_{\ast})^{6}\,\Theta(t-t_{\ast})\,.

(3.34)

Moreover,
we find the following analytical solution for

F_{\rm L}
(shown in the bottom panels of Fig.
11
),

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}^{\rm sim}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})=A_{\rm sim}(t)\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\bigl[\tilde{k}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{*})\bigr]\,.

(3.35)

Hence,
the evolution of the spectral peak of
F_{\rm L}^{\rm sim}
is

\tilde{k}_{*}^{\rm sim}(\tilde{t})=z_{*}/(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})
,
where
z_{*}
is the spectral peak of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
The value of
z_{\ast}
is approximately
z_{1}
, which has been computed
for all phase transitions in Sec.
2.3
.
In particular, for the single broken power law used in Eq. (
2.17
),
z_{\ast}/z_{1}=2^{-a_{1}^{-1}}\simeq 0.84
.
The appearance of a second scale
z_{2}
in the double broken power law
can slightly modify the relation between
z_{1}
and
z_{*}
,
but in general we find
z_{\ast}/z_{1}\approx(0.85-1.05)
.
Both
A_{\rm sim}(t)/n_{b}=(t-t_{\ast})^{6}\,\Theta(t-t_{\ast})

and
\tilde{k}_{\ast}^{\rm sim}

are shown in Fig.
12
, where
C_{\rm sim}=0
indicates
that the position of the peak of
\gamma_{\rm sim}
is
at
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}
.

For the case of exponential nucleation instead, these results
are only approximately valid
in the asymptotic limit
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gg 1
.
In this limit,
since
\gamma_{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})
is
peaked at
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{*}+C_{\rm exp}
,
with

C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})
,
as shown in Fig.
10
,
we approximately have

\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gg 1}A_{\rm exp}(t)\simeq n_{b}(t-t_{\rm peak})^{6}\,.

(3.36)

Again in this asymptotic limit,
a crude estimate of the longitudinal correlator
is therefore found by approximating

\gamma_{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})\approx\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})
,
such that
F_{\rm L}
becomes
a function only of the time difference

\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
,

\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gg 1}F_{\rm L}^{\rm exp}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})\simeq A_{\rm exp}(t)\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\bigl[\tilde{k}\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})\bigr]\,.

(3.37)

Indeed,
we observe in Figs.
11
and
12

that the functional form at late times becomes the same
for both exponential and simultaneous nucleation
(for which
C_{\rm sim}=0
and
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}
),

\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gg 1}F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})=n_{b}\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})^{6}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\,\bigl[\tilde{k}\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})\bigr]\,.

(3.38)

The main consequence of

\gamma_{\rm exp}
being highly peaked
around
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
is that
the oscillations of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
are not smoothed out at late time,
since the shape of
F_{\rm L}
eventually inherits the shape of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
For the cases of exponential and Gaussian nucleation,
the phase transition might complete before this
limit is reached.
Therefore, in general, we shall use Eq. (
3.26
) for numerical evaluation
of
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})
.

In general, we show in Sec.
D.1
that for any choice of
h_{\ast}

in the exponential case,
the distribution function
\gamma_{\rm exp}
can be uniquely expressed as a function
of

\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
(see also Fig.
10
).
We indeed observe in Fig.
12
that both
A(\tilde{t})

and
\tilde{k}_{\ast}(\tilde{t})
collapse at all times when shown as a
function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}

for the two choices of
h_{\ast}=1/e
and 0.71.
At early times, we find that
the ratio
\tilde{k}_{\ast}/z_{\ast}\simeq 0.13

remains roughly constant for
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\lesssim 5
.
In the following sections, we will be interested in computing

F_{\rm L}
at the beginning of the sound-wave regime,
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
,
which we model in Sec.
4
.
Since for exponential nucleation

\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
might occur before the
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}\gg 1

asymptotic limit is reached, we will have
some dependence on this time of the resulting spectral shape and the position
of the spectral scales, as we investigate in Sec.
5
.

3.4
Anisotropic stresses in the phase
of expanding bubbles

To conclude the treatment of the bubble expansion phase,
in this section we show that the transverse
and traceless (TT) projection of
the energy-momentum tensor of a superposition of expanding bubbles
is identically zero and,
hence, does not give rise to GWs.
This is the extension to momentum space of the calculation
given in Appendix A of ref.
[
24
]
.
The TT-projected energy-momentum tensor in momentum space is

\Pi_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=\Lambda_{ijkl}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})\,T_{kl}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})
,
where
\Lambda_{ijkl}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})
is
the projection tensor,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{ijkl}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})\equiv{\textstyle{1\over 2}}(P_{ik}P_{jl}+P_{il}P_{jk}-P_{ij}P_{kl})\,,\qquad\text{with \ }P_{ik}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})=\delta_{ij}-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}\,.

(3.39)

The energy-momentum tensor, given in Eq. (
2.1
) for fluid
perturbations, can be expressed as

T_{ij}(t,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}})=X_{i}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})X_{j}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})+p(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,\delta_{ij}
,
where we have defined

X_{i}\equiv\sqrt{w}\gamma v_{i}

[
50
]
.
The TT projection of the diagonal terms
vanishes.
Using the Fourier transform of the irrotational
velocity field induced by a single bubble, given in Eq. (
2.8
),
generalized to

X_{i}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{})=\hat{p}_{i}X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(t,p)\,e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}}
,
the anisotropic stress tensor in momentum space becomes

\displaystyle\Pi_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=

\displaystyle\,e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}}\int\frac{{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\Lambda_{ijkl}(\hat{k})\,\hat{p}_{k}\,\hat{\tilde{p}}_{l}\,X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(t,p)\,X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(t,\tilde{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}}\int_{-1}^{1}{\rm\,d}\vartheta\int_{0}^{\infty}X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(t,p)\,X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(t,\tilde{p})\,\frac{p^{3}}{\tilde{p}}{\rm\,d}p

\displaystyle\quad\times\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\bigl[\delta_{ij}(1-\vartheta^{2})-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}(1+\vartheta^{2})\bigr]-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\frac{{\rm\,d}\phi}{2\pi}\,\bigl[\hat{p}_{i}\hat{p}_{j}-(\hat{p}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}+\hat{p}_{j}\hat{k}_{i})\vartheta\bigr]\biggr\}\,,

(3.40)

where we have defined
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}

and expressed the integral over
\textstyle p
in
spherical coordinates
(p,\theta,\phi)
, such that
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}=pk\vartheta

and
\tilde{p}^{2}=p^{2}+k^{2}-2pk\vartheta
, being
\vartheta\equiv\cos\theta
.
Since the integral over
\phi
cancels out with
the first term in brackets,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{{\rm\,d}\phi}{2\pi}\bigl[\hat{p}_{i}\hat{p}_{j}-(\hat{p}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}+\hat{p}_{j}\hat{k}_{i})\vartheta\bigr]=\frac{1}{2}\bigl[\delta_{ij}(1-\vartheta^{2})-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}(1+\vartheta^{2})\bigr]\,,

(3.41)

one finds indeed
\Pi_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=0
.

This result shows that in general the energy-momentum tensor of
a radial velocity profile, i.e., induced by the same bubble
n

[
24
]
, has identically zero traceless and transverse projection,
\Pi_{ij}=0
.
The velocity field resultant from many expanding and uncollided bubbles
also does not produce anisotropic stresses
\Pi_{ij}
.
Therefore, there is no GW production from the bubble expansion phase.
GWs start to be sourced by the collisions of fluid
shells, when the energy-momentum tensor
T_{ij}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})

contains also the velocity field arising from the collided bubbles.
Indeed, the product of velocities induced by different
bubbles
n
and
m

in Eq. (
3.40
) adds the
term
e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}}e^{i\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(m)}}=e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle p$}}{}\cdot(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(n)}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(m)})}e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{0}^{(m)}}
, leading to a non-vanishing
integral
[
71
,
72
,
21
]
.
Furthermore, at the end of the phase transition, the fluid perturbations
in the linear regime
can be described as a superposition of sound
waves, induced by different bubbles, leading to the
production of GWs as described in the sound shell model

[
64
,
60
,
106
,
113
,
104
]
.

In general, one aims at calculating the normalized GW energy density power
spectrum
\Omega_{\rm GW}(t,k)
.
Solving the GW equation
with the Wronskian method, one finds

[
27
,
106
,
32
]

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW}(t,k)=\frac{3k^{3}}{4\pi^{2}}F_{\rm GW}^{0}\int_{t_{\ast}}^{t}\!\frac{{\rm\,d}t_{1}}{t_{1}}\int_{t_{\ast}}^{t}\!\!\frac{{\rm\,d}t_{2}}{t_{2}}\,F_{\Pi}(t_{1},t_{2},k)\cos k(t-t_{1})\cos k(t-t_{2})\,,

(3.42)

where
F_{\rm GW}^{0}\simeq 1.6\times 10^{-5}
is the redshift of the GW energy
density to present time, assuming all Standard Model particles
are relativistic at the time of GW formation

[
31
,
106
,
29
]
,
and
F_{\Pi}
is the unequal-time correlator (UETC) of
the anisotropic stresses,

\langle\Pi_{ij}(t_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}})\,\Pi^{*}_{ij}(t_{2},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\rangle\equiv(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,F_{\Pi}(t_{1},t_{2},k)\,.

(3.43)

The two-point function of the anisotropic stress in the left-hand
side of the above equation is a four-point function of the field

X_{i}(t,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})
that is in general unknown.
The calculation is then tackled by assuming that
the fields entering the four-point function in Eq. (
3.43
) are normally
distributed.
Wick’s (Isserlis’) theorem then applies, and the correlator can be decomposed in terms of
two-point functions of the velocity field
[
68
]
.

However, in the present case, applying Wick’s theorem
leads to a non-vanishing

F_{\Pi}(t_{1},t_{2},k)
and, therefore, to GW production
[see Eq. (
3.42
)], despite the fact that the velocity field before collisions
is spherically symmetric.
To clarify this point, let us
apply Wick’s theorem to the two-point correlator of
\Pi_{ij}
, similarly
to the calculation of ref.
[
24
]
.
Assuming, as is usually done in the literature, that the velocity and enthalpy perturbations
are small,
we can express
X_{i}\simeq\bar{w}\,v_{i}
,
where
\bar{w}
is the average enthalpy.
Then, one obtains

[
24
,
106
,
32
]

\displaystyle F_{\Pi}(t_{1},t_{2},k)

\displaystyle\,=\frac{\bar{w}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{\rm L}(t_{1},t_{2},p)\,p^{4}{\rm\,d}p\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{F_{\rm L}(t_{1},t_{2},\tilde{p})}{\tilde{p}^{2}}(1-\vartheta^{2})^{2}{\rm\,d}\vartheta\neq 0\,,

(3.44)

where
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}{}
.
The spectral densities
F_{\rm L}
in Eq. (
3.44
)
are non-zero even when the velocity field from the same bubble
is considered (see Sec.
3
).
The angular integral on
\vartheta
is also not trivial, yielding a
non-vanishing
F_{\Pi}
.

Applying Wick’s theorem leads to a non-vanishing GW signal,
in contradiction with
\Pi_{ij}=0
demonstrated previously.
This indicates that
the velocity field before collisions is non-Gaussian.
Indeed, the statistic of the velocity field given by the presence of many uncollided bubbles
is far from Gaussian, and the velocity field distribution becomes
normal only in the sound-wave regime, once it corresponds to the superposition of
velocity shells from many collided bubbles, characterized as sound waves.
Therefore, applying Wick’s theorem in the uncollided regime is not justified, and a
non-zero

F_{\Pi}(t_{1},t_{2},k)

cannot describe
the bubble expansion phase.
However, it might be
regarded as an estimate of the anisotropic stresses induced by the collisions of the
fluid shells surrounding the bubbles.
This was indeed
used in ref.
[
24
]
to estimate the GW signal.
Simplifying the four-point correlation function
of the velocity field with Wick’s theorem renders it non-zero also for points

\textstyle x
and
\textstyle y
belonging to different bubbles,
as it contains the product of the velocity field two-point correlation
functions of the two bubbles, which is non-zero.
The approach could therefore be justified
if one further assumes that the velocity
field is not too much perturbed during the collision and sound-wave phases.
However, although the UETC of Eq. (
3.44
) describes it at the initial time
of the sound-wave phase, the velocity
field evolves following the sound-wave
solution during the sound-wave regime.
We will compare this result with the one obtained following
the sound shell model in ref.
[
104
]
.

4
Fluid perturbations across collisions

As the bubbles grow, the perturbed fluid shells start to collide.
Within the sound shell model, the velocity perturbations
at this moment provide the initial conditions to
study the perturbative dynamics after the collisions.
The regime after the period of collisions
corresponds to the acoustic or sound-wave
regime and is commonly described following
the sound shell model
[
64
,
60
]
.
In the present section,
we study the evolution of the kinetic energy with time.
In particular, in Sec.
4.1
we review the
energy conservation argument to estimate the conversion
of the false vacuum energy
into thermal and kinetic energy for a single
bubble

[
48
,
60
]
.
We then extend this argument to the phase of
bubble expansion, where the velocity field
is described by the superposition of
N_{b}
bubbles.
Since we consider the linear regime of fluid
perturbations, we assume that the average
kinetic energy density is
\langle e_{\rm kin}\rangle=\langle w\gamma^{2}v^{2}\rangle\simeq\bar{w}\langle v^{2}\rangle=\bar{w}\,v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
, where
\bar{w}
is the average enthalpy.
Hence, we focus on studying the evolution of the rms
velocity
v_{\text{rms}}=\sqrt{\langle v^{2}\rangle}
.
In Sec.
4.2
, we evaluate the time evolution
of
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
during the expansion phase of multiple uncollided bubbles.
We then point out in Sec.
4.3
that
an implicit assumption of the sound shell model is that the
kinetic energy density is conserved across collisions.
Then, imposing this conservation of kinetic
energy, we estimate the
effective deterministic time
t_{\rm sw}

as the time at which the kinetic energy density
around expanding bubbles matches the
result at the beginning of the sound-wave regime.

4.1
Velocity and energy conservation for a single bubble

Let us first estimate the macroscopic energy density conversion that sources perturbations
in the fluid velocity after
a broken-phase bubble has nucleated

[
48
,
60
]
.
Initially, the energy density of the primordial plasma is homogeneous and takes the value

e_{n}
, evaluated at the nucleation time
t_{n}
, defined in Eq. (
D.5
).
In the bag equation of state,
the false vacuum contribution in the symmetric phase is
treated as a constant
\epsilon
that only depends on
T_{n}
[see Eq. (
2.3
)], such that the total energy
density just before the
nucleation of the first bubble,
is

T^{00}=e_{n}+\epsilon\,.

(4.1)

As bubbles of the broken phase are nucleated and expand,
the vacuum energy density is released into the plasma, producing fluctuations in the fluid
velocity,
v
, and enthalpy,
w=w_{n}+\delta w
.
Once the transition is completed, the radiation energy takes the perturbed value

e=e_{n}+\delta e
, and the total energy density is

T^{00}=w\gamma^{2}-p=e_{\rm kin}+e\,,

(4.2)

where
e_{\rm kin}=w\gamma^{2}v^{2}
is the kinetic energy density
and we have used
the identity
\gamma^{2}v^{2}=\gamma^{2}-1
.
Then, the conservation of energy applied to a single uncollided expanding
bubble implies,

{\cal V}_{w}\epsilon=\int(e_{\rm kin}+\delta e){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}=4\pi\int_{0}^{1}\bigl[X_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)+\delta e_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\bigr]\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(4.3)

where
X_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)=[\sqrt{w_{\text{\tiny ip}}}\,\gamma_{\text{\tiny ip}}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}](\xi)

is the square root of the kinetic energy density profile,
and
{\cal V}_{w}\equiv V_{w}/(t-t_{0})^{3}=\tfrac{4\pi}{3}\xi_{w}^{3}

is the normalized
volume occupied by the bubble.
We note that
X=\sqrt{\bar{w}}\,U_{f}
, where
U_{f}
is the enthalpy-weighted
velocity as defined in refs.
[
61
,
62
,
60
,
31
,
28
]

and
\bar{w}
is the average enthalpy defined in Eq. (
4.5
).
Dividing Eq. (
4.3
) by its left-hand side, we find the
efficiency coefficients of kinetic and thermal
energy conversion,

\kappa
and
\omega

[
77
,
48
,
60
]
,

1=\kappa+\omega\,,

(4.4a)

with

\kappa=\frac{4}{\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha w_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}X_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega=\frac{3}{\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha w_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}\delta w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(4.4b)

where we have used
\epsilon=\tfrac{3}{4}\alpha w_{n}

and
\delta e_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)/e_{n}=\delta w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)/w_{n}
in the bag equation
of state.
Reference
[
48
]
gives a prescription to
determine analytically
\kappa(\alpha,\xi_{w})
.
The result for the benchmark values of
\alpha=0.01,

0.05
, and
0.1

is illustrated by the colored
lines in Fig.
13
, and compared to numerical values from
Eq. (
4.4
) (colorful dots).
We find numerical consistency of this fit
with a maximum relative error of 3% (see numerical values in Table
3
).

Figure 13
:

Kinetic efficiency factor
\kappa

as a function of the wall velocity
\xi_{w}
and phase transition
strength
\alpha
.
The solid lines
are evaluated using the semi-analytical fits of ref.
[
48
]

for different values of
\alpha
, with the colored lines
indicating the benchmark values shown in previous figures,

\alpha=0.01
(green), 0.05 (blue), and 0.1 (red).
The colored dots indicate the values of
\kappa
,
defined in Eq. (
4.4b
),
the triangles indicate
\kappa
when we take
subrelativistic
\gamma\simeq 1

and still allow for large enthalpy perturbations (
\kappa_{\rm sub}
), and crosses
indicate
\kappa
when we treat perturbatively both velocity
and enthalpy perturbations (
\kappa_{\rm pert}
), as done in Eq. (
4.6
).
The dashed gray line indicates the Chapman-Jouget
speed
v_{\rm CJ}
separating deflagrations from detonations.

Since we are interested in studying
fluid perturbations in the linear regime,
we assume small velocity (
\gamma^{2}\approx 1
)
and small enthalpy perturbations, such that
w=\bar{w}+\delta w
,
with
\delta w\ll\bar{w}
.
We define the average value
\bar{w}
as

\bar{w}=\langle w\rangle_{\rm sph\,3d}=\frac{3}{4\pi}\int w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}=3\int_{0}^{1}w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(4.5)

where the angle brackets indicate 3D spherical average over the
normalized volume of the sphere with radius
\xi=1
.
We show the numerical values of
\bar{w}/w_{n}
in Table
3
,
which are close to unity even for the
moderately large value of
\alpha=0.1
.
In the limit of small perturbations,
\bar{w}\simeq w_{n}
.
We note that in previous literature (see, e.g., ref.
[
60
]
),
a similar average is defined
for the rms enthalpy-weighted velocity field, normalizing by the bubble
volume
{\cal V}_{w}
, such that
(\bar{U}_{f}^{1\rm d})^{2}\equiv\langle U_{f}^{2}\rangle_{\rm sph\,3d}/\xi_{w}^{3}
.
The values of
\bar{w}_{1\rm d}\equiv\bar{w}/\xi_{w}^{3}\simeq w_{n}/\xi_{w}^{3}
computed including the
{\cal V}_{w}
normalization
differ from
\bar{w}
by a factor
of
\xi_{w}^{3}
.
Hence,
\bar{w}_{1\rm d}
becomes
very different than
\bar{w}
for small
\xi_{w}
,
even for small perturbations.

Table 3
:

Values of the mean enthalpy
\bar{w}

[see Eq. (
4.5
)], the reference
enthalpy
w_{\rm ref}
[see Eq. (
4.7
)], and the
\kappa
and
\omega

coefficients [see Eq. (
4.4
)]
for benchmark phase
transitions with
\alpha=0.1
.
We also present the
efficiency coefficient
\kappa_{\rm pert}
computed
in the perturbative limit as in Eq. (
4.6
).

In the perturbative
limit of small velocity and enthalpy perturbations,

X=\sqrt{\bar{w}}\,U_{f}\simeq\sqrt{w_{n}}v
since
U_{f}\simeq v
and
\bar{w}\simeq w_{n}
, and the efficiency
\kappa

can be approximated as

\kappa\simeq\kappa_{\rm pert}\equiv\frac{4}{\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}v^{2}_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi=\frac{4}{3\,\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha}\langle v^{2}\rangle_{\rm sph\,3d}\,.

(4.6)

However, as velocity or enthalpy fluctuations become larger,
Eq. (
4.6
)
is not an accurate approximation of the efficiency
\kappa
,
since the omission of
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)

in the integral of Eq. (
4.4
) leads to significant relative
errors, as shown in Fig.
13
and Table
3
.
Alternatively, the enthalpy-weighted velocity
U_{f}=\sqrt{w_{\text{\tiny ip}}/\bar{w}}\,\gamma_{\text{\tiny ip}}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}

[
63
,
31
,
38
,
29
,
36
]
or
the variable
X_{\text{\tiny ip}}\equiv\sqrt{\omega_{\text{\tiny ip}}}\,\gamma_{\text{\tiny ip}}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}

[
50
]
are
used to study the
GW production in the limit of large enthalpy
perturbations, i.e., when
\kappa\neq\kappa_{\rm pert}
.
To estimate this difference, we define a reference
enthalpy
w_{\rm ref}

w_{\rm ref}\equiv\frac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}X_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi}{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi}\,,\qquad\text{such that \ }\kappa=\frac{w_{\rm ref}}{w_{n}}\kappa_{\rm pert}=\frac{4}{\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha}\frac{w_{\rm ref}}{w_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}v^{2}_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\,.

(4.7)

We provide numerical values of
\kappa_{\rm pert}
and
w_{\rm ref}

in Table
3
for
\alpha=0.1
, which allows us to quantify
the importance of nonlinear terms in the fluid dynamics.
As visible in Fig.
13
,
the major source of error in determining
\kappa
for
\alpha\lesssim 0.1

is due to neglecting the enthalpy perturbations.
Indeed, one can see that the values of
\kappa_{\rm sub}
(in triangles),
which is computed as in Eq. (
4.4b
)
assuming subrelativistic velocities (i.e.,
\gamma\simeq 1
)
but still including enthalpy perturbations in the integral over
\xi
,
are much closer to
\kappa
(in dots)
than
\kappa_{\rm pert}
(in crosses).

On a separate note,
since the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, studied in Sec.
2
,
represents the
single-bubble velocity correlation function, its integral
over
z
is related to the integral over
\xi
and, hence,
to
\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
,
via Plancherel’s theorem,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\,z^{2}{\rm\,d}z=4\pi\int_{0}^{1}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi=\frac{4\pi}{3}\langle v^{2}\rangle_{\rm sph\,3d}\,.

(4.8)

Indeed, if we take
{\cal V}=\tfrac{4\pi}{3}

as the fractional volume occupied by the spherical
shell with radius
\xi=1
in Eq. (
3.5
), and reinterpret
the integral over nucleation locations
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\xi$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}}{}_{0}
as an integral over the
single-bubble volume, we find

\frac{\tilde{B}_{ii}(t-t_{0},0)}{(t-t_{0})^{3}}=\frac{3}{4\pi}\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)=\langle v^{2}\rangle_{\rm sph\,3d}\,.

(4.9)

We note that
\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
is identified with
{\cal V}_{w}\,(\bar{U}_{f}^{\rm 1d})^{2}

in previous work

[
62
,
63
,
60
]
,
under the assumption of small perturbations.
Since the field is irrotational, the longitudinal
and normal components of the correlation function satisfy

\tilde{b}_{\rm L}(0)=\tilde{b}_{\rm N}(0)=\frac{1}{3}\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)

[see Eq. (
C.15
)].

To prove the validity of the second equality in
Eq. (
4.8
), we use the expression
of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)=\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)
given in Eqs. (
3.20
) and (
3.21
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\,z^{2}

\displaystyle\,{\rm\,d}z=8\int_{0}^{\infty}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{x}){\rm\,d}\xi_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{y}){\rm\,d}\xi_{y}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\,\biggl[\frac{\sin(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})}{z^{2}}-\frac{2}{z}\xi_{x}\cos(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})+\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}\cos(z\xi_{x})\cos(z\xi_{y})\biggr]{\rm\,d}z\,,

(4.10)

where the integral over
z
can be split in three terms.
The first two terms are

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\,\biggl[\frac{\sin(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})}{z^{2}}-\frac{2\xi_{x}\cos(z\xi_{x})\sin(z\xi_{y})}{z}\biggr]{\rm\,d}z

\displaystyle\,=\frac{\pi}{4}(\xi_{x}+\xi_{y}-|\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}|)-\frac{\pi}{2}\xi_{x}\biggl(1-\frac{\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}}{|\xi_{x}-\xi_{y}|}\biggr)=\frac{\pi}{2}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\xi_{y}\ ,&\quad{\rm when\ }\xi_{x}>\xi_{y}\ ,\\
-\xi_{x}\ ,&\quad{\rm when\ }\xi_{x}\leq\xi_{y}\ .\end{array}\right.

After integration over
\xi_{x}
and
\xi_{y}
, the contribution of
Eq. (
4.1
) to Eq. (
4.10
) vanishes.
The remaining term in Eq. (
4.10
) is

\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos z\xi_{x}\cos z\xi_{y}{\rm\,d}z=\frac{\pi}{2}\xi_{x}\,\xi_{y}\bigl[\delta(\xi_{x}+\xi_{y})+\delta(\xi_{x}-\xi_{y})\bigr]\,,

(4.13)

which, substituted back into Eq. (
4.10
), reduces to the
trace of the correlation function of the velocity field,
\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
,
as we wanted to prove in Eq. (
4.8
).

4.2
Time evolution of the kinetic energy of multiple uncollided bubbles

Extending the previous results to a superposition of multiple bubbles,
the evolution of the ensemble average of the
kinetic energy density, estimated as

\langle e_{\rm kin}\rangle=X_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\bar{w}\,\bar{U}_{f}^{2}\simeq w_{n}v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
in the limit of
small fluid perturbations,
can be computed from
the power spectral density
F_{\rm L}(t,k)
, studied in Sec.
3.3
.
Integrating
F_{\rm L}
[see Eq. (
3.26
)] over
k
, we can find the time evolution of

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(t)\equiv\langle v^{2}(t)\rangle=B_{ii}(t,0)

via Plancherel’s theorem [see Eq. (
C.5
)],

\displaystyle v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(t)=\langle v^{2}(t)\rangle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{\rm L}(t,k)\,k^{2}{\rm\,d}k

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{2}{\rm\,d}k\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{6}\tilde{f}_{\rm L}\bigl[k(t-t_{0})\bigr]{\rm\,d}t_{0}\,.

(4.14)

Changing the integration variables from
t_{0}

and
k
to
t_{0}
and
z\equiv k(t-t_{0})
,
we find

\displaystyle v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tilde{f}_{\rm L}(z)\,z^{2}{\rm\,d}z\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}t_{0}=\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0})(t-t_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}t_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{1}{6}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}=\frac{1}{8}\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}\,,

(4.15)

where we have used
\beta^{3}/n_{b}=8\pi\xi_{w}^{3}=6{\cal V}_{w}
,
given in Eq. (
3.30
),
and
{\cal V}_{w}=\tfrac{4}{3}\pi\xi_{w}^{3}
, such that their ratio is 6.
We have also used the relation
\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)=\pi\xi_{w}^{3}\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha
, which can be obtained combining Eqs. (
4.6
) and (
4.8
).
This
v_{\rm rms}^{2}
represents the evolution of the rms velocity
of a single bubble multiplied by
the expectation value of the fractional
volume occupied by the
N_{b}
nucleated bubbles
over time, shown in Fig.
14
.

Figure 14
:

Evolution of the mean squared fluid velocity

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t})\equiv\langle v^{2}(\tilde{t})\rangle=B_{ii}(\tilde{t},0)
, given in Eq. (
4.2
),
during the phase of bubble expansion, normalized by

\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)/{\cal V}_{w}
,
which corresponds to the value at the beginning of the sound-wave
regime,
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}^{+})
,
as we show in Sec.
4.3
[see Eq. (
4.26
)].
The red and orange lines correspond to the cases of
exponential nucleation with
h_{\ast}=1/e
and 0.71, respectively,
while the blue line corresponds to the case of simultaneous nucleation.
For the cases of exponential nucleation,
v^{2}_{\text{rms}}
is a universal
function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
, independent of the choice
of
h_{\ast}
.
The simultaneous case follows the function
\tfrac{1}{6}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})^{3}
,
which is also followed by the exponential case at late times,

\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gtrsim 20
.
The curves cross unity at
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
, i.e.,
when Eq. (
4.27
) is satisfied, which happens at

\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\simeq 0.063

for exponential nucleation (vertical red dashed line),
and at
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}=6^{1/3}\simeq 1.82
for simultaneous nucleation (vertical blue dashed line).

For simultaneous nucleation,
\gamma_{\rm sim}(\tilde{t})=\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})
,
and
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
becomes

(v_{\text{rms}}^{\rm sim})^{2}(\tilde{t})=\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{1}{6}\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})^{3}=\frac{1}{8}\,\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})^{3}\,.

(4.16)

On the other hand,

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
in the exponential case follows
that of the simultaneous case
only in the asymptotic limit
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gg 1
,
where, as in Sec.
3.3
, we can approximate the peaked distribution
function
\gamma(\tilde{t})\simeq\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})
, and
find

\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}\gg 1}(v_{\text{rms}}^{\rm exp})^{2}(\tilde{t})\simeq\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{1}{6}\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}^{3})=\frac{1}{8}\,\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha\,(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})^{3}\,.

(4.17)

Figure
14
shows the evolution of
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
, normalized by

\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)/{\cal V}_{w}
,
as a function of the time difference with respect to the time
when the peak of
\gamma
is reached,
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
.
We note that
\tfrac{1}{6}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})^{3}
(shown in green)
is followed at all times for the simultaneous case, while it is followed
by the exponential case only at late times
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\gtrsim 20
.

The result in Eq. (
4.2
) also applies for a
deterministic realization of the nucleation history
of
N_{b}
bubbles in a volume
V
.
In this case,
the distribution function
\Gamma(t_{0})
collapses to a delta at each
time of nucleation of a bubble
n
,

\Gamma(t_{0})=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{b}}\delta(t_{0}-t_{n})\,,

(4.18)

and the resulting
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
is

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(t)=\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{V}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{b}}(t-t_{n})^{3}=\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{V}\frac{V_{\rm bp}}{{\cal V}_{w}}=\frac{3}{4}\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha\,{\cal V}_{\rm bp}(t)\,,

(4.19)

where
{\cal V}_{\rm bp}\equiv V_{\rm bp}/V
is the fraction of
the volume in the broken phase
V_{\rm bp}={\cal V}_{w}\sum_{i}(t-t_{i})^{3}
,
and we have
related
\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)
to
\kappa
combining Eqs. (
4.6
) and (
4.8
).
An analogous approach was used in ref.
[
28
]
(see their appendix B)
to track the evolution of
the kinetic energy fraction
K(t)\equiv\langle e_{\rm kin}\rangle/(e_{n}+\epsilon)

during the bubble expansion phase,
which can also be written as
K(t)=\tfrac{4}{3}\tfrac{v_{\rm rms}^{2}(t)}{(1+\alpha)}
,
using
\langle e_{\rm kin}\rangle=w_{n}v_{\rm rms}^{2}
,
e_{n}=3w_{n}/4
,

\epsilon=\alpha\,e_{n}
.
From Eq. (
4.19
), we find that the evolution of the
deterministic kinetic energy fraction is

K(t)=\frac{\kappa\alpha}{1+\alpha}{\cal V}_{\rm bp}(t)=K_{\xi}\,{\cal V}_{\rm bp}(t)\,.

(4.20)

The authors in ref.
[
28
]
compare the fraction

K(t)/{\cal V}_{\rm bp}(t)
to
K_{\xi}\equiv\kappa\alpha/(1+\alpha)

to test the convergence of their simulations in their Figure (14).

4.3
Conservation of kinetic energy and initial
time of the sound-wave phase

As the bubbles expand, eventually they start colliding and producing GWs.
After the period of collisions,
the initial conditions for the velocity field
at the beginning of the
sound-wave regime, following the sound shell model,
are obtained by averaging
the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
over the
bubble lifetimes
T

(see Sec.
D.3
for details about the
bubble lifetime distribution
\nu
)
[
60
,
106
]

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}^{+},\tilde{k})=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{6}{f^{\prime}}^{2}(\tilde{k}\tilde{T}){\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,,

(4.21)

where
\tilde{T}=T\beta
and we define
\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}
as the
starting time of the sound-wave regime.
The first question we want to address is what is the corresponding
kinetic energy density at the beginning of the sound-wave regime,
which is determined by
e_{\rm kin}\simeq w_{n}v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{+})
in the limit of small fluid
perturbations.
Then, we want to understand at
which time,

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t})
in the uncollided expanding phase would reach the value of
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{+})

if one ignores the potential impact of collisions in the kinetic
energy, i.e., assuming that kinetic
energy is conserved.
To answer these questions, we
insert Eq. (
4.21
) in Eq. (
4.14
) and change the first integration
variable to
z=\tilde{k}\tilde{T}
.
Then, the two integrals become independent, and
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
becomes

\displaystyle v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(t_{\rm sw}^{+})=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}^{+},\tilde{k})\,k^{2}{\rm\,d}k=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{3}}\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\,z^{2}{\rm\,d}z\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,.

(4.22)

For any choice of the nucleation distribution,
the third moment of
\nu(\tilde{T})
,
\nu_{3}
,
has a unique interpretation as the normalized
volume in the broken phase at
t_{\rm sw}
, which takes a universal value
of 6, as we prove in Eq. (
4.25
).
It was already pointed out in ref.
[
60
]
that
\nu_{3}=6

for exponential
and simultaneous nucleation.
We demonstrate this for any
\nu(T)
by starting from its definition
[see Eq. (
D.36
)],
using the modeling of the collisions proposed in
the sound shell model (see section 4.2 of
ref.
[
60
]
and Sec.
D.3
),

\displaystyle\nu_{3}=-\frac{1}{\beta\,n_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}p(\tilde{t}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm\,d}h(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T})}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}}\,\tilde{T}^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=\frac{3}{\beta\,n_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}h(x){\rm\,d}x\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{x}p(\tilde{t})\,(x-\tilde{t})^{2}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}\,,

(4.23)

where we have integrated by parts the first expression
and defined the variable
x\equiv\tilde{T}+\tilde{t}
.
Using the relation between
p
and
h
from Eq. (
D.2
),
the following property is satisfied,

3\,h(x)\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{x}p(\tilde{t})\,(x-\tilde{t})^{2}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}=-\frac{\beta^{4}}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{{\rm\,d}h(x)}{{\rm\,d}x}\,,

(4.24)

and Eq. (
4.23
) becomes

\displaystyle\nu_{3}\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta^{3}}{n_{b}}\frac{1}{{\cal V}_{w}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}{\rm\,d}h(x)=\frac{\beta^{3}}{n_{b}}\frac{1}{{\cal V}_{w}}=6\,.

(4.25)

All in all, Eq. (
4.22
) becomes

\displaystyle v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(t_{\rm sw}^{+})=\frac{\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)}{{\cal V}_{w}}=\frac{3}{4}\kappa_{\rm pert}\,\alpha\simeq\bar{U}_{f}^{2}\,,

(4.26)

where the rms enthalpy-weighted velocity
\bar{U}_{f}

reduces to
v_{\text{rms}}(t_{\rm sw}^{+})

in the limit of small perturbations.
Remarkably, the result in Eq. (
4.26
)
depends only on the single-bubble
kinetic energy, characterized by
\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha
[see Eq. (
4.6
)].
As discussed in Sec.
4.1
, when the enthalpy perturbations
become large,
\kappa_{\rm pert}
is no longer a good approximation
of
\kappa
.

We note that our analysis during the expansion phase can be generalized
to larger perturbations.
In fact, the derivation carried out in this section, leading to Eq. (
4.26
), can be applied to any
fluid variable, e.g., to
X_{\text{rms}}^{2}
,
implying that all squared averaged variables from the fluid are assumed
to be conserved across collisions.
This implies that given the collision modeling
of the sound shell model, the kinetic energy
density is conserved upon collisions also in the
regime of large fluid perturbations.
However, vorticity can be produced during collisions
and at later times
[
95
,
40
,
105
,
50
]
,
and the fluid perturbations after collisions might not, in general,
follow those described by the sound shell model
[
38
,
102
,
28
,
36
]
.
Hence, since the sound shell model applies in the
linear regime of perturbations, we restrict our analysis
to small perturbations, such that
X_{i}=\sqrt{w_{n}}\,{U_{f}}_{i}\approx\sqrt{w_{n}}\,v_{i}
,
\kappa\approx\kappa_{\rm pert}
, and
w_{\rm ref}\approx w_{n}
.

We now estimate an effective time setting the
beginning of the sound-wave regime,

\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
, by imposing that
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}

is left unchanged from
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{-}
to
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{+}
.
Then, equality between Eqs. (
4.2
) and (
4.26
)
leads to the following condition to determine
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
,

\frac{v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{-})}{v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}^{+})}=\frac{1}{6}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}=1\,.

(4.27)

The values of
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
predicted for simultaneous and exponential
nucleations are indicated by the vertical dashed lines in Fig.
14
.
In particular, for simultaneous nucleation, we find
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}=6^{1/3}

using Eq. (
4.16
), while for exponential nucleation, we find numerically

\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\simeq 0.063
.

In summary,
we have defined an effective starting time
of the sound-wave regime
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
,
such that the kinetic energy at this time
is identical to the kinetic energy found
at the beginning of the sound-wave
regime, computed by
averaging over the bubble lifetime distribution
\nu(T)
.

In general, for a stochastic distribution of the times
t_{1}
at which the sound-wave regime
starts after collisions,
the velocity power spectral density in the aftermath of collisions is

F_{\rm L}(k)=\int_{t_{c}}^{\infty}{\rm\,d}t_{1}\int_{t_{c}}^{t_{1}}P(t_{0},t_{1})(t_{1}-t_{0})^{6}\tilde{f}_{\rm L}\bigl[(t_{1}-t_{0})k\bigr]{\rm\,d}t_{0}\,,

(4.28)

where
P(t_{0},t_{1})
is the joint distribution function of nucleation
and collision times
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
.
In ref.
[
60
]
, by assuming that the fluid perturbation around
each bubble exactly becomes the initial condition of the sound wave originating
from that bubble, they argue that
F_{\rm L}

does not depend separately on the nucleation and collision times,
but only on their difference.
Hence, they turn

P(t_{0},t_{1})
into a distribution over the bubble lifetimes
T=t_{1}-t_{0}
.
In our approach, based on the definition of a deterministic effective
time
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
satisfying Eq. (
4.27
), the joint distribution function becomes
P(t_{0},t_{1})=\Gamma(t_{0})\,\delta(t_{1}-t_{\rm sw})
,
leading to

F_{\rm L}(k)=\int_{t_{c}}^{t_{\text{sw}}}\Gamma(t_{0})(t_{\text{sw}}-t_{0})^{6}\tilde{f}_{\rm L}\bigl[(t_{1}-t_{0})k\bigr]{\rm\,d}t_{0}\,,

(4.29)

which corresponds to Eq. (
3.26
) evaluated at
t_{\text{sw}}
.
A better modeling of the joint distribution function during the period
of collisions, incorporating
a stochastic distribution of
t_{1}
around
t_{\rm sw}
, could improve the modeling
