presented in our work.

5
Spectrum of the velocity field at the beginning of the
sound-wave phase

In Sec.
2
, we studied the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, which describes the power spectral
density of the velocity field resulting from a superposition of expanding
bubbles randomly nucleated in space.
We showed that the envelope of this function is characterized by the amplitudes in two
asymptotic limits,
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
when
z\to 0
and
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
when
z\to\infty
,
where
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
is related to the integral of the velocity profile over
\xi

[see Eq. (
2.10
)] and
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
is related to its
discontinuities [see Eq. (
2.15
)].
Two scales appear naturally,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, determining the transitions to each
of these asymptotic limits,
potentially allowing for an intermediate regime
with slope
\gamma
when both scales are sufficiently separated [see Eq. (
2.19
)].
Then, in Sec.
3.3
, we described the time evolution of the power spectral
density
F_{\rm L}(t,k)
, resulting from a stochastic distribution of the
nucleation times, considering both exponential and simultaneous nucleation in time.
Finally,
we showed in Sec.
4
that the modeling of collisions in the sound
shell model implies that the kinetic energy density
is conserved
from the uncollided to the sound-wave phase,
allowing us to define an effective time
t_{\text{sw}}

that describes the initial time of the sound-wave regime.
In the following, we propose to use
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
to evaluate
F_{\rm L}
[see Eq. (
4.29
)]
as an estimate of the velocity spectrum at the initial time of the sound-wave regime.
For exponential nucleation,
we show that our approach leads to velocity spectra
that are almost equal to the ones obtained in the sound shell model
[
64
,
60
,
106
]
,
using the bubble lifetime distribution function
\nu

(see Sec.
D.3
), as indicated in Eq. (
4.21
),
in the limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
.
In particular, we find maximum relative differences of up to 8% between the two approaches
(see Fig.
15
).
However, for the case of simultaneous nucleation, we find that the resulting
velocity spectrum using our approach differs from the one found in the sound shell model.
In particular, this is mostly due to the fact that oscillations from
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

are not smoothed out in the simultaneous case in our approach.
For a Gaussian distribution, strongly peaked around
t_{\ast}
, we find good
agreement at large
\tilde{k}
(see Fig.
15
).
The advantage of defining this effective time
is that it does not rely on the determination of the distribution of bubble lifetimes,
and therefore the only distribution function involved is the one for the times
of nucleation,
\Gamma(t)=p(t)\,h(t)
, through Eq. (
4.29
),
reducing the number of manipulations needed to generalize the model.
On the other hand, in our approach, the main assumption is that all collisions
effectively occur at a deterministic time
t_{\rm sw}
,
which is the same for all bubbles.
Effectively, this is a way to bypass our ignorance of the details of the collision phase.
As discussed above,
a better modeling of
F_{\rm L}
could be achieved if we knew the exact distribution function of the collision time.

We show in Sec.
5.1

the asymptotic limits of the velocity spectrum and
how the original scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
convert in the two scales that characterize
the velocity spectrum
F_{\rm L}(t_{\rm sw},k)
at the starting time of the sound-wave regime.
Then, we provide in Sec.
5.2

a template for the function
F_{\rm L}(t_{\rm sw},k)

using the two scales,
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
,
and evaluate its intermediate
slope
\sigma
.
We show that the prefactor
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}
that appears in the template can be related to the coefficient
\kappa
, defined in Eq. (
4.6
).
Therefore, our template of
F_{\rm L}(t_{\rm sw},k)
is expressed
only in terms of the simplest known quantities
that can be evaluated from the 1D profiles (see Fig.
1
), with the minimal
required amount of information, while still improving its accuracy with respect to previous
estimates used in the literature.

We already introduced the velocity power spectral density
at the beginning of the sound-wave phase in Eq. (
4.21
),
following the modeling of collisions of the sound shell model (ssm)

[
60
,
106
]
,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}^{\rm ssm}(\tilde{k})=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{6}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}(\tilde{k}\,\tilde{T}){\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,,

(5.1)

where for the cases of exponential and simultaneous nucleation,

\nu_{\rm exp}(\tilde{T})=e^{-\tilde{T}}
and
\nu_{\rm sim}(\tilde{T})={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,\tilde{T}^{2}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{T}^{3})
(see Sec.
D.3

and refs.
[
64
,
60
]
).
As shown in Sec.
4.3
[see Eq. (
4.29
)],
one can alternatively define an effective
time
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
at which the velocity
spectrum in the bubble expansion phase gives the same
kinetic energy density,
e_{\rm kin}\simeq w_{n}\,v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\tfrac{3}{4}\,w_{n}\,\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha
,
as the one at the beginning of the sound-wave regime,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw},\tilde{k})=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})\,(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{6}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\bigl[\tilde{k}\,(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{0})\bigr]{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}\,.

(5.2)

We show in Fig.
15
the velocity power spectrum

{\cal P}_{v}(\tilde{k})=k^{3}F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})/(2\pi^{2})
,
defined such that
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}(\tilde{t}_{\rm sw})\equiv\int{\cal P}_{v}(\tilde{k}){\rm\,d}\ln\tilde{k}
.
We find that both computations
are almost equivalent
for the exponential nucleation case,
with less than
8\%
relative difference at all
\tilde{k}

in the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
limit
that we focus on.
For finite values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
, the two approaches give
larger relative differences in the power
spectrum.

For the case of simultaneous nucleation, the two approaches lead to velocity spectra that are different in particular for what concerns the oscillatory behaviour.
This is mostly due to the fact that in our approach, the sound-wave time is treated deterministically,
such that the
\delta
function in the nucleation time
distribution,

\gamma_{\rm sim}=\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})
, leads to
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=n_{b}(t_{\rm sw}-t_{\ast})^{6}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\bigl[\tilde{k}(\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})\bigr]\,\Theta(t-t_{\ast})
,
as shown in Eqs. (
3.34
) and (
3.35
).

F_{\rm L}
therefore inherits the oscillatory behaviour of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}
.
However, if one introduces a distribution around
t_{\rm sw}
in Eq. (
4.28
),
as effectively considered in the sound shell model
via the introduction of a lifetime distribution
\nu(T)
,
then
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
is averaged over
different collision times.
As a result, its oscillations are no longer in phase and are therefore smoothed out.
In any case, after taking the convolution of Eq. (
3.44
)
to compute the anisotropic stresses,
we expect that the oscillations that appear in our approach should smooth out, leading
to a resulting GW spectrum that should be similar for both approaches.
We investigate the potential discrepancy between
the two approaches in ref.
[
104
]
.

We also consider the case of Gaussian nucleation with
\ln h_{\ast}\simeq-0.01
,
whose distribution function is shown in Fig.
25
in brown.
The distribution
\gamma(\tilde{t})
in this case
approaches the simultaneous one,
but it is still spread around
\tilde{t}_{\ast}
.
We still observe some differences in the velocity spectra resulting from the two approaches,
but the oscillations are smoother
and the spectra are similar
at large
\tilde{k}
.
At small
\tilde{k}
, the two approaches yield a power law in
\tilde{k}
that differs by a factor of 2.

In all cases, we remind the reader that the integral of
{\cal P}_{v}
over
\ln k
yields
the same result,
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}\equiv\tilde{b}_{ii}(0)/{\cal V}_{w}=\tfrac{3}{4}\,\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha
,
and we expect that the differences between the two choices
in the resulting GW spectrum are suppressed.
In the following, we focus on studying the asymptotic limits of

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})\equiv F_{\rm L}^{\rm ssm}(\tilde{k})
and providing a template based on the minimal
amount of information from the original
velocity self-similar profiles
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
induced in a first-order phase
transition.

Figure 15
:

Velocity spectrum,
{\cal P}_{v}(\tilde{k})\equiv k^{3}F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})/(2\pi^{2})
, defined such that

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\int{\cal P}_{v}(\tilde{k}){\rm\,d}\ln\tilde{k}

at the effective sound-wave time

\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}

[see Eq. (
3.26
)] for
the case of exponential nucleation (red lines),
the case of simultaneous nucleation (blue lines), and
the case of Gaussian nucleation with
\ln h_{\ast}=-0.01

(green lines),
which approaches the
simultaneous case but its
\gamma(t)
is not a delta function,
as shown in Fig.
25
.
The respective velocity spectra
{\cal P}_{v}^{\rm ssm}(\tilde{k})

evaluated according to the sound shell model using Eq. (
5.1
)
are shown for comparison in solid lines.
The choice of phase transition parameters is
\alpha=0.1
and
\xi_{w}=0.4

for illustration, as in Fig.
11
.
We find that only for the case of exponential nucleation the velocity spectra
following both approaches almost
coincide, with a relative difference below 8% (lower plot).
In the case of Gaussian nucleation, both approaches lead to comparable spectra but
differences are larger
at small
\tilde{k}
,
with relative differences up to 180%, while for the simultaneous nucleation, the appearance
of oscillations in
{\cal P}_{v}
makes the differences between the
two approaches large at all scales.
However, we note that the integrated quantities are still leading to the same

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
in both approaches, and that we expect these oscillations
to smooth out once we compute the GW spectrum, as we investigate
in ref.
[
104
]
.

5.1
Asymptotic limits of the velocity spectrum

We show in App.
C
that the causality condition for an irrotational field
implies that
F_{\rm L}\to k^{2}
in the
k\to 0
limit
[
91
,
60
]
.
We indeed find
that, in this limit,
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})
inherits the large-scale behavior of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, i.e.,
\propto z^{2}=\tilde{k}^{2}\tilde{T}^{2}
.
Taking this limit in Eq. (
5.1
), we find

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{0}\,\tilde{k}^{2}+{\cal O}(\tilde{k}^{4})
, where

F_{\rm L}^{0}=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\,{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}A_{0}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }A_{0}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{8}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,.

(5.3)

Using the
\nu(\tilde{T})
of exponential
and simultaneous nucleation [see Eqs. (
D.41
) and (
D.45
)],
we find

\displaystyle A_{0}^{\rm exp}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\tilde{T}}\,\tilde{T}^{8}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=8!=40320\,,\qquad A_{0}^{\rm sim}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{T}^{3}\bigr)\,\tilde{T}^{10}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=6^{8/3}\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{11}{3}\bigr)\,.

(5.4)

We know that in the
z\to\infty
limit,

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)=z^{-4}{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\infty}(z)+{\cal O}(z^{-5})
,
where
f^{\prime}_{\infty}(z)
,
given in Eq. (
2.14b
),
depends on the discontinuity jumps and positions of the velocity profile.
Hence, in the limit
k\to\infty
, we define

F_{\rm L}^{\infty}
, such that
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{\infty}\,\tilde{k}^{-4}+{\cal O}(\tilde{k}^{-5})
,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}^{\infty}=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}\,A_{\infty}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }A_{\infty}=\lim_{\tilde{k}\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\infty}(\tilde{k}\tilde{T})}{{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{2}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,.

(5.5)

Since the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\infty}(z)

over the oscillations at large
z=\tilde{k}\tilde{T}
becomes

{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
, we have introduced it in the definition of
A_{\infty}
.
Then, the coefficient
A_{\infty}
takes the following values

\displaystyle A_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,\tilde{T}^{2}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}\,,

(5.6)

where we find the
{\textstyle{1\over 2}}
factor numerically.
This factor appears due to the fast oscillations
of the
\sin^{2}(z)
and
\cos^{2}(z)
functions that appear in
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\infty}
,
which are effectively averaged out when we take the integral over
\tilde{T}
.
For exponential and simultaneous nucleations, the values of
A_{\infty}
become

\displaystyle A_{\infty}^{\rm exp}=

\displaystyle\,\int_{0}^{\infty}{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,\tilde{T}^{2}\,e^{-\tilde{T}}{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=1\,,\qquad A_{\infty}^{\rm sim}=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,\tilde{T}^{4}\exp\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{T}^{3}\bigr){\rm\,d}\tilde{T}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,6^{2/3}\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{5}{3}\bigr)\,.

(5.7)

5.2
Template of the velocity spectrum

We found in the previous section that, similarly to
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, the function

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})
takes the asymptotic limit
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{0}\tilde{k}^{2}
when

k\to 0
and
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{\infty}\tilde{k}^{-4}
when
k\to\infty
.
Therefore, we can relate the asymptotic limits of
F_{\rm L}
with the integral over
the self-similar velocity profiles (
F_{\rm L}^{0}
) and with the velocity jumps of
the velocity profiles (
F_{\rm L}^{\infty}
).

In analogy to
z_{\text{cross}}
, defined in Eq. (
2.19
), we can define
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}
based on
these two asymptotic limits.
Using Eqs. (
5.3
) and (
5.5
),
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}
becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}=\biggl(\frac{F_{\rm L}^{\infty}}{F_{\rm L}^{0}}\biggr)^{1/6}=\left|\frac{f_{\text{env}}^{\prime}}{f^{\prime}_{0}}\right|^{1/3}\biggl(\frac{A_{\infty}}{A_{0}}\biggr)^{1/6}=z_{\text{cross}}\biggl(\frac{A_{\infty}}{A_{0}}\biggr)^{1/6}\,.

(5.8)

Then, introducing Eqs. (
5.4
) and (
5.7
) into Eq. (
5.8
),
we can find the ratio
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}
for exponential
and simultaneous nucleation,
which allows us to compute the overall shift in the scales that occurs due to the
bubble nucleation history and only depends on the distribution
function of nucleation times,

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}^{\rm exp}}{z_{\text{cross}}}=(8!)^{-1/6}\simeq 0.17\,,\qquad\frac{\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}^{\rm sim}}{z_{\text{cross}}}=320^{-1/6}\simeq 0.38\,.

(5.9)

This result already pinpoints an important remark.
The spectral peak of the power spectrum
F_{\rm L}
can be determined
using the scale
z_{1}

found in Sec.
2.3
, which only depends on the self-similar profiles, and
the constant factor
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}\equiv(A_{\infty}/A_{0})^{1/6}

that only depends on the nucleation history.
Indeed, since we find that the ratio
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}

does not depend on
\xi_{w}

and
z_{1}=z_{\text{cross}}
at
\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}
, we can then
use Eq. (
5.8
) to determine
\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}=\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}
.
This result allows us to predict
the positions of the spectral
peak,
and compare among different nucleation
histories.
Furthermore,
it
provides an explanation
to the location of the spectral
peak,
which has been found to be at larger scales for the
case of
exponential nucleation than
for the simultaneous case in previous work

[
60
,
106
]
.

We can now compare the value of
\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}
with
\tilde{k}_{*}/z_{\ast}
studied in Sec.
3.3
.
We found there that
\tilde{k}_{\ast}/z_{\ast}
for exponential
nucleation remained constant and equal
to
\sim 0.13
at times
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\lesssim 3
(see Fig.
12
), which is the range
where
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}\simeq 0.063
lays (see Fig.
14
).
Since
z_{\ast}\simeq z_{1}
, we consistently find the very similar values 0.13 and 0.17.
For simultaneous nucleation, on the contrary,
we found in Sec.
3.3
that
\tilde{k}_{\ast}/z_{\ast}=(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})^{-1}
,
which, evaluated at
\tilde{t}_{\text{sw}}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}=6^{1/3}
(see Fig.
14
), gives us the
value
\tilde{k}_{\ast}/z_{\ast}\simeq 0.55
.
This value significantly differs from the value of
0.38 found in Eq. (
5.9
) using
\nu(T)
to compute
F_{\rm L}
,
as it can also be seen in Fig.
15
comparing the blue
solid and dash-dotted lines.
This is not surprising, since in this case, the resulting
power spectrum
{\cal P}_{v}
is different in the two approaches.

Analogously to the templates that we have presented for
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

in Sec.
2.4
,
we provide single and double broken power laws for
F_{\rm L}

in the following.
When the two scales
\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
are close enough,
such that there is no evident intermediate slope,

F_{\rm L}
can be fit
with a single broken power law,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}^{\rm SBPL}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{0}\,\tilde{k}^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{-6/b_{1}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\lesssim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)\,.

(5.10)

In the case in which the separation
between
the two scales is sufficiently large, an intermediate slope
\sigma
appears,
and we need to fit
F_{\rm L}
using a double broken power law,

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}^{\rm DBPL}(\tilde{k})=F_{\rm L}^{0}\,\tilde{k}^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{\frac{\sigma-2}{b_{1}}}\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{b_{2}}\Biggr]^{\frac{-\sigma-4}{b_{2}}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\gtrsim\tfrac{1}{2}\,v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)\,,

(5.11)

where

\displaystyle\sigma=2\left[1-3\,\frac{\log(\tilde{k}_{2}/\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}})}{\log(\tilde{k}_{2}/\tilde{k}_{1})}\right]\in[-4,2]\,,\qquad\text{with }\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}=\biggl|\frac{f_{\rm env}^{\prime}}{f_{0}^{\prime}}\biggr|^{1/3}\biggl(\frac{A_{\infty}}{A_{0}}\biggr)^{1/6}\,.

(5.12)

The power spectral density of the velocity,
F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})
,
found numerically for phase transitions with

\alpha=0.1
and different wall velocities, is shown in Figs.
16
and
17
,
respectively for the exponential and simultaneous nucleation cases,
compared with the single broken and double broken power law fits.
We have fixed
b_{1}=2
and
b_{2}=4
in the exponential case,
and
b_{1}=4
and
b_{2}=4
in the simultaneous case for all values of
\xi_{w}
.
Moreover, we compare the results of the full profiles with those
corresponding to
the toy models described in
Sec.
2.5
, which are quadratic profiles for subsonic
deflagrations and detonations, and linear-constant profiles for hybrids.
We can observe how the resulting
F_{\rm L}
obtained using the toy models accurately
describes the numerical results, exactly reproducing the asymptotic limits
and the intermediate slope by construction.
This is true even for hybrids, for which the envelope of the function

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in the
z\to\infty
was fixed, but not the oscillations in
z
(see discussion in
Sec.
2.5
), showing that
F_{\rm L}
is not strongly affected by the exact
oscillations of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
at large
z
.

Figure 16
:

Numerical results for the power spectral
density of the velocity field,
F_{\rm L}(k)
, computed from Eq. (
5.1
) for an exponential (EXP)
nucleation history in the limit
\tilde{t}_{*}-\tilde{t}_{c}\to\infty

(see details in Sec.
D.1
), considering benchmark phase transitions
with
\alpha=0.1
and a range of
\xi_{w}
.

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})
is normalized by its asymptotic
k\to 0
branch,
\tilde{k}^{2}F_{\rm L}^{0}
.
The plot also shows the single or double broken power law fit presented in Sec.
5.2
and the velocity spectral
density computed from the toy models for the velocity profiles presented in Sec.
2.5
.
The vertical lines indicate the locations of
\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
,
given in Eq. (
5.13
).
The red dot is at the location of
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}
[see Eq. (
5.12
)], which is defined
as the location of the intersection between the two asymptotic limits

F_{\rm L}^{0}\,\tilde{k}^{2}
and
F_{\rm L}^{\infty}\tilde{k}^{-4}
.

Figure 17
:

Same as Fig.
16
for the case of simultaneous (SIM) nucleation.

We now proceed to compute the ratios
\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}/z_{2}
numerically, by performing the integration over
\nu(T)

in Eq. (
5.1
).
We find that the factors
\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}

and
\tilde{k}_{2}/z_{2}
are different from each other, and that they only depend on
the type of nucleation probability distribution,
but not on the strength
\alpha
of the phase transition neither on the bubble
wall velocity
\xi_{w}
,

\frac{\tilde{k}_{1,2}^{\rm exp}}{z_{1,2}}\simeq\{0.17,0.21\}\,,\qquad\frac{\tilde{k}_{1,2}^{\rm sim}}{z_{1,2}}\simeq\{0.38,0.25\}\,.

(5.13)

These values accurately describe the positions of the spectral peaks for
the single and double broken power laws that can be used to reproduce the velocity
spectrum for different phase transitions, as can be seen in Figs.
16
and
17
.
Furthermore, as argued after Eq. (
5.9
),
\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}
is equal
to
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}
.

We note that the different coefficients in the two ratios of Eq. (
5.13
) modify the slope

\sigma
(see Fig.
18
) with respect to
\gamma
, i.e., the intermediate
slope found for
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
(see Fig.
6
), not to be confused with the
normalized nucleation rate.
Indeed, defining
\tilde{k}_{\rm cross}/z_{\rm cross}=\tilde{k}_{1}/z_{1}=a

and
\tilde{k}_{2}/z_{2}=b
,
the slope
\sigma
in Eq. (
5.12
) becomes

\sigma=2\biggl[1-3\frac{\log(z_{2}/z_{\text{cross}})+\log(b/a)}{\log(z_{2}/z_{1})+\log(b/a)}\biggr]=\gamma-6\log(b/a)\frac{\log(z_{\text{cross}}/z_{1})/\log(z_{2}/z_{1})}{\log(z_{2}/z_{1})+\log(b/a)}\,.

(5.14)

Hence, the value of
\log(b/a)
quantifies how different the slope
\sigma
is to the
slope
\gamma
computed in Sec.
2.4
,
and
\sigma
reduces to
\gamma

in the limit
b=a
, where the slope is preserved.
Of course,
\sigma
also reduces to
-4
as

\gamma
does, in the limit
z_{2}\to z_{\text{cross}}\to z_{1}
,
independently of the ratio
b/a
.
We note that the value of
\sigma\to-3
as
z_{\text{cross}}\to z_{1}
in Fig.
18

(see
\sigma
at
\xi_{w}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}
), which
seems in contradiction with the appearance of only one power law, is just
a numerical artifact due
to the value of the smoothness parameter

b_{1}=2
used for exponential nucleation, which makes
the transition smoother than when we use
b_{1}=4
for
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in Fig.
6

and for simultaneous nucleation.

Figure 18
:
Intermediate slope
\sigma
, defined in Eq. (
5.12
),
as a function of
\xi_{w}
for different values of
\alpha=0.01
and
0.1
.
For each value of
v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
,
we plot
\sigma
only
in the range
\xi_{w}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
(indicated
by vertical dashed lines),
in which we fit the envelope of
F_{\rm L}

with a double broken power law [see Eq. (
5.11
)].
The sound (
c_{\rm s}
) and the Chapman-Jouget (
v_{\rm CJ}
) speeds,
determining the transition between sub and supersonic deflagrations
and between deflagrations and detonations, are indicated with
vertical solid lines.
We show the results for both exponential (EXP) and simultaneous (SIM) nucleation histories.

For both the single and the double broken power law fits,
the amplitude of the velocity spectrum in the
k\to 0
limit,

F_{\rm L}^{0}
, independently
of the nucleation history,
is proportional to
{f^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}

[see Eq. (
5.3
)].
The latter is related to the third moment of the single-bubble
velocity profile
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)

[see Eq. (
2.10
)].
However, as shown in Sec.
4.2
, the integral over
k
of
{\cal P}_{v}/k\equiv k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k)/(2\pi^{2})

should be proportional to
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}\equiv\tfrac{3}{4}\kappa_{\rm pert}\,\alpha

[see Eq. (
4.26
)].
This allows us to write

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{k})=v_{\text{rms}}^{2}\,{\cal N}\,S(\tilde{k})=\tfrac{3}{4}\,\kappa_{\rm pert}\,\alpha\,{\cal N}\,S(\tilde{k})\,,

(5.15)

where
{\cal N}
is a normalization constant, defined
such that
\tfrac{\beta^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\int S(\tilde{k})\,\tilde{k}^{2}{\rm\,d}\tilde{k}={\cal N}^{-1}
,
and
S
represents either the single or the double broken power law,
based on the appropriate fit for each case,

\displaystyle S_{\rm SBPL}(\tilde{k})=

\displaystyle\,\tilde{k}^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{-6/b_{1}}\,,

(5.16a)

\displaystyle S_{\rm DBPL}(\tilde{k})=

\displaystyle\,\tilde{k}^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{\frac{\sigma-2}{b_{1}}}\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{b_{2}}\Biggr]^{\frac{-\sigma-4}{b_{2}}}\,.

(5.16b)

The normalization constants
{\cal N}
relate the amplitude
F_{\rm L}^{0}

and
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}
for both the SBPL and the DBPL, and can be expressed analytically (see App.
E
for a detailed derivation of these
expressions, in particular Eq. (
E.3
) for the single broken power law,
and Eqs. (
E.5
) and (
E.15
) for the double broken power law with
b_{1}=b_{2}

and
b_{1}\neq b_{2}
respectively).

Combining Eqs. (
5.15
) and (
5.16a
),
and introducing
{\cal N}_{\rm SBPL}
from Eq. (
E.3
), we find

\displaystyle\frac{k^{3}F^{\rm SBPL}_{\rm L,\,EXP/SIM}(\tilde{k})}{2\pi^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{1}\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{6}{b_{1}}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{5}{b_{1}}\bigr)\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{1}{b_{1}}\bigr)}\,v_{\text{rms}}^{2}\,\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{5}\,\biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\biggr]^{-\frac{6}{b_{1}}}\quad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}\,,

(5.17)

for the velocity spectra that can be approximated as a single broken power law.
The prefactor
b_{1}\Gamma(6/b_{1})\Gamma(5/b_{1})^{-1}\Gamma(1/b_{1})^{-1}
takes the values
1.09 and 1.70 for
b_{1}=4
and
b_{1}=2
, respectively.

For the velocity spectra described by double broken power laws,
the integral of
S_{\rm DBPL}
can be expressed via the hypergeometric function
when
b_{1}=b_{2}=b
, which we find to represent
F_{\rm L}
for simultaneous nucleation
(
b=4
).
Introducing
{\cal N}_{\rm DBPL}
from Eq. (
E.5
), we write

\displaystyle\frac{k^{3}F_{\rm L,SIM}^{\rm DBPL}(\tilde{k})}{2\pi^{2}}=\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{6}{b}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{5}{b}\bigr)\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{1}{b}\bigr)}

\,\!\,{}_{2}F_{1}^{-1}\Bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b},\tfrac{5}{b};\tfrac{6}{b};1-\bigl[\tfrac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\bigr]^{b}\Bigr)\,v_{\text{rms}}^{2}

\displaystyle\times\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{5}

\displaystyle\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b}\Biggr]^{\frac{\sigma-2}{b}}\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{b}\Biggr]^{\frac{-\sigma-4}{b}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\gtrsim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}\,.

(5.18)

In this case, the amplitude of the velocity spectrum depends on the slope
\sigma

and the ratio
k_{2}/k_{1}
.

For the case of exponential nucleation, we find that different smoothness
parameters (
b_{1}=2
and
b_{2}=4
) are required to fit the numerical
results.
In this case,
{\cal N}_{\rm DBPL}
can be expressed using the Fox
H
-function
in Eq. (
E.15
),

\displaystyle\frac{k^{3}F_{\rm L,EXP}^{\rm DBPL}(\tilde{k})}{2\pi^{2}}=b_{2}\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{4+\sigma}{b_{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\,\Biggl\{H_{2,2}^{2,2}\!\left[\left(\frac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\right)^{b_{1}}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\bigl(-4,\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\bigr),\;\bigl(1-\frac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}},1\bigr)\\
(0,1),\;\bigl(\frac{\sigma+4}{b_{2}}-5,\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\bigr)\end{array}\right]\Biggr\}^{-1}\,v_{\text{rms}}^{2}

\displaystyle\times\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{5}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}

\displaystyle\,\Biggr]^{\frac{\sigma-2}{b_{1}}}\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{k}_{2}}\biggr)^{b_{2}}\Biggr]^{\frac{-\sigma-4}{b_{2}}}\qquad\text{for \ }\xi_{w}\gtrsim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}\,.

(5.21)

These templates provide accurate fits to the velocity power spectral density
with the minimum amount of information required from the original
single-bubble fluid velocity profiles
and the distribution function of the nucleation times.
The scales

\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
for exponential and simulteanous
nucleation are computed using Eq. (
5.13
)
from the scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
[see Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.16
)],
which characterize the power spectral density of the velocity field
averaged over nucleation locations,
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.
Then, the intermediate slope
\sigma
is defined in Eq. (
5.12
)
and can be computed from
\tilde{k}_{1}
,

\tilde{k}_{2}
, and
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}
, with
\tilde{k}_{\text{cross}}/z_{\text{cross}}

given in Eq. (
5.9
).

z_{\text{cross}}
depends on the ratio
f^{\prime}_{0}/f^{\prime}_{\rm env}
,
where
f^{\prime}_{0}
is given by the integral over the
self-similar velocity profile in Eq. (
2.10
),
and
f^{\prime}_{\rm env}
by the discontinuities of the velocity profile in Eq. (
2.15
).
Finally, the amplitude can either be expressed directly
using
F_{\rm L}^{0}
[see
Eqs. (
5.10
) and (
5.11
)], or using the integrated amplitude

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\tfrac{3}{4}\kappa_{\rm pert}\alpha

[see Eqs. (
5.17
–
5.2
)]
where
\kappa_{\rm pert}
is
given by Eq. (
4.6
) and
characterizes the kinetic energy density in the perturbative
limit of small fluid perturbations.
Hence, we have clearly identified the origin of each of the scales, asymptotic limits,
and intermediate slopes, and our results can be directly applied for all different
types of fluid profiles and two common choices of distribution functions of nucleation times.

6
Conclusions

The gravitational wave (GW) background produced during a first-order phase
transition in the early Universe is one of the most promising
cosmological backgrounds that can be potentially observed with
current and future GW experiments.
It is of particular relevance to explore BSM physics leading
to a first-order electroweak phase transition that can be probed
by the Laser Interferometer Space Antenna.

In this work, we investigate the fluid perturbations associated
to first-order phase transitions, paying particular attention
to the origin of the scales that are commonly assumed to describe the
GW spectrum in the literature based on fits to numerical simulations
of the fluid and scalar field dynamics during first-order
phase transitions
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
38
,
70
,
69
,
28
,
36
]
,
and on theoretical modeling based on the sound shell model
[
64
,
60
,
106
,
113
]
.
To describe the fluid perturbations,
we restrict our analysis to the bag equation of state
[
48
]
.
The results presented in our work provide the initial conditions
for the sound-wave regime that some of the authors
considered in ref.
[
106
]
.
The study of the fluid perturbations during the sound-wave regime
and the impact of our results to determine the GW spectrum, in
particular the relevant scales and the different slopes, is presented
in a separate publication
[
104
]
.

We study the statistical properties and spectral shape
of the velocity field, whose power spectral
density is shown to
be fully characterized by its longitudinal
component, as expected for fields with zero vorticity (irrotational),
which is given in Eq. (
3.26
),

F_{\rm L}(\tilde{t},\tilde{k})=\frac{n_{b}}{\beta^{6}}\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\gamma(\tilde{t}_{0})(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})^{6}\,{f^{\prime}}^{2}\bigl[(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})\,\tilde{k}\bigr]{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}_{0}\,,

(6.1)

where
\gamma
describes the normalized nucleation rate of broken-phase
bubbles, described in detail in App.
D
,

n_{b}\equiv\beta^{3}/(8\pi\xi_{w}^{3})
is the expected
number density of bubbles at the end of the phase transition,
\beta
is
the nucleation rate parameter,
\xi_{w}
the wall velocity, and
\tilde{k}=k/\beta

and
\tilde{t}=t\beta
are normalized wave numbers and times.
The power spectrum in Eq. (
6.1
) describes the velocity field
during the phase of expansion of uncollided bubbles.

The spectral function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
,
where
z=(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{0})\,\tilde{k}
, can be traced back
to the Fourier transform of the single-bubble velocity profile

v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
,
as given by Eq. (
2.8
), where
\xi=r/(t-t_{0})
,
r
being the distance to the
center of the bubble.
For a benchmark phase transition with
\alpha=0.1
, we show
the velocity profiles in Fig.
1
, following the description provided
in App.
A
.
The relation between
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
and
f^{\prime}(z)
, provides us with key
information on the spectral shape of the resulting velocity
spectrum.
Indeed, we found
in Sec.
2.3
that the two asymptotic limits of

{f^{\prime}}(z)
when
z\to 0
(large scales) and
z\to\infty
(small scales)
can be determined
analytically from specific properties of
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
.
In particular, the third moment of the velocity profile
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)

determines the value of
{f^{\prime}}(z)

at large scales [see Eq. (
2.10
)],

\lim_{z\to 0}f^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{4\pi}{3}z\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi^{3}\,v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(6.2)

while the locations of the discontinuities of
v_{\rm ip}(\xi)
and
the values of the velocity jumps
\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}
, determine exactly
the asymptotic limit of
{f^{\prime}}(z)
at small scales [see Eqs. (
2.14
)],

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}f^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{4\pi}{z^{2}}\bigl[\xi_{\text{sh}}\,\sin(z\xi_{\text{sh}})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\text{sh}})+\xi_{w}\,\sin(z\xi_{w})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})\bigr]\,.

(6.3)

The above equation holds for deflagrations,
which present two discontinuities: one at
\xi_{w}
and one at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
For detonations, with only one discontinuity at
\xi_{w}
, the asymptotic limit of
f^{\prime}(z)
is

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}f^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{4\pi}{z^{2}}\,\xi_{w}\,\sin(z\xi_{w})\,\Delta v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{w})\,.

(6.4)

Furthermore, we note that
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)\sim z^{2}
and
\sim z^{-4}

respectively in the two asymptotic limits
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
.
This behavior can be directly inferred from the statistical
properties of discontinuous, causal, and
irrotational fields that follow
statistically homogeneous and isotropic distributions, as we discuss
in Sec.
3
and App.
C
.
We
found that the scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
at which both
asymptotic limits are reached, depend precisely on the
locations of the two discontinuities that
appear in the velocity profiles of deflagrations:
one discontinuity is always present at the bubble wall (
\xi_{w}
),
and a second discontinuity is due to the formation of a shock in front
of the wall at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
For detonations, only one discontinuity is present.
In this case, we found
that the two scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are determined by the front and the back
of the fluid shell, as previously argued in the literature.
Also for subsonic deflagrations, the front and the back locations
of the fluid shell coincide with the discontinuity locations.
For hybrids, however, one needs to correct the
estimate of the separation between the two scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}

in previous literature.
To summarize, for a broad range of
\xi_{w}
and
\alpha
,
the locations of the two scales determining the breaks
of the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
are
the following [see Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.16
)],

\xi_{1}=\frac{2\pi}{z_{1}}\simeq{\frac{1}{3}\,\xi_{+}}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}4\,,\\
5\,,\end{array}\right.\qquad\xi_{2}=\frac{2\pi}{z_{2}}\simeq{\frac{1}{3}\,\tilde{\Delta}\xi}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}6\,,&\qquad\rm{for\ deflagrations}\,,\\
4\,,&\qquad\rm{for\ detonations}\,.\end{array}\right.

(6.5)

We identified two scales,
\xi_{+}
and
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
, where the former
is
\xi_{+}=\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{w}{=\xi_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}}
for deflagrations and
\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm b}={\xi_{w}+c_{\rm s}}
for detonations,
and the latter is
\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{w}={\xi_{\text{sh}}-\xi_{w}}
for deflagrations
and
\tilde{\Delta}\xi=\Delta\xi=\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b}={\xi_{w}-c_{\rm s}}
for detonations.
The scale
\tilde{\Delta}\xi
coincides with the
fluid shell thickness
\Delta\xi=\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b}
for subsonic deflagrations
and detonations, but not for hybrids.
For subsonic deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
, the velocity jump becomes negligibly small at
\xi_{\text{sh}}\to c_{\rm s}
, where
c_{\rm s}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
is the speed
of sound and
v_{\rm CJ}
is the Chapman-Jouget speed.
In these cases, the second scale does not appear in the velocity
spectral density, which is then only characterized by
\xi_{1}
.
This is a consequence of a vanishing
discontinuity jump at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
as
\xi_{\text{sh}}
approaches
c_{\rm s}
.

The resulting
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
is, in general, a highly oscillatory function at
large values of
z
, as can be seen in Eq. (
6.3
).
Its envelope can be determined by a single or a double broken power law,
depending on whether we have one or two relevant scales.
We provided in Sec.
2.4
templates that accurately reproduce the envelope
that we find from the numerical velocity profiles (see Fig.
3
).
These fits reproduce accurately the locations
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, the asymptotic limits,
and the intermediate slopes in the cases described by a double broken
power law.
The intermediate slope in
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
can be found using Eq. (
2.19
),
and it only depends on the asymptotic amplitudes and locations of the
spectral breaks, which can be traced back to the properties of the
single-bubble velocity profiles.

To test the robustness of our results and provide analytical results, we studied toy models in Sec.
2.5
.
We found that the simplest toy models that accurately reproduce
the velocity profiles and the spectral shape of the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)

are quadratic (for subsonic deflagrations and detonations) and piecewise
linear-constant with a discontinuity at
\xi_{w}
(for hybrids).
The proposed
toy models can be used to compute
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
analytically,
providing a useful tool to study the resulting velocity
spectrum at the beginning of the sound-wave regime and, hence,
the resulting anisotropic stresses and the GW spectrum.

In Sec.
3
, we studied the correlation function
of the velocity field as a function of the separation
\textstyle r
between
two points in coordinate space,
B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})
,
and related it to the
power spectral density
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
in momentum space.
We revised previous studies in
the literature, and provided new results regarding the relation between
the longitudinal and normal components of the correlation function
[see Eq. (
3.10
)],
which is a consequence of the curl-free condition of the
spherical velocity profiles.

In Sec.
3.3
, we presented how the scales and amplitude of the
velocity spectrum
F_{\rm L}
, obtained from
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
after taking the average
over nucleation times, as indicated in Eq. (
6.1
), evolve with time
during the phase of expanding uncollided bubbles.
We focus our study on two types of nucleation distributions that are
commonly considered for phase transitions in the literature
(exponential and simultaneous), which we review
in App.
D
.
To conclude our analysis of the fluid perturbations during the phase
of bubble expansion, we reviewed in Sec.
3.4

the implications of applying
Wick’s theorem during this phase, which leads to a non-vanishing
two-point correlator of the anisotropic stress tensor, in contradiction with the spherical symmetry
of the system.
We explicitly indicate how this is a consequence of the non-Gaussianities
of the field during this period and, hence, the inapplicability
of Wick’s theorem.

We then evaluate the conservation of kinetic energy density during
the expansion phase of multiple uncollided bubbles, showing how
the kinetic energy density of the system can be described by the efficiency factor

\kappa

[
81
,
48
]
, obtained from the single-bubble fluid profiles, and used
in the literature to determine the amplitude of the GW spectrum.
Furthermore, we showed that the modeling of collisions via
the introduction of a bubble lifetime distribution in the sound
shell model implicitly implies that the mean squared fluid fields
(i.e., velocity and enthalpy) are conserved during the period of
collisions.
Based on this finding, we proposed an alternative method to determine
the velocity power spectrum at the initial time of the sound-wave
phase that is instead only
based on the conservation of kinetic energy density during the
initial period of collisions.
Following this approach, we defined the characteristic,
deterministic time at which the sound-wave regime starts,
\tilde{t}_{\rm sw}
,
and computed it for the cases of exponential
and simultaneous nucleation in Sec.
4.3
.
The proposed approach leads to the same velocity power spectrum
that the original sound shell model in the case of exponential
nucleation (with relative errors in the spectrum below 8%),
as can be seen in Fig.
15
,
but it leads to different results as the
distribution function becomes closer to the simultaneous case,
i.e., as the dispersion of the distribution function around
the peak time
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
diminishes.
This is a consequence of the fast oscillations in
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
, which
are not suppressed when we compute
F_{\rm L}
in the simultaneous case
following our approach.
When we introduce an average over the bubble lifetimes, as done
in the sound shell model, these oscillations are smoothed out.
However, we expect that the resulting anisotropic stresses and GW
spectrum will not be strongly affected by the presence of the oscillations,
since they are computed from a convolution of the velocity power
spectrum, as indicated in Eq. (
3.44
).
We investigate the resulting GW spectrum following both
approaches in a separate publication
[
104
]
.

Finally, in Sec.
5
, we provide a template of the
velocity power spectrum at the initial time of the sound-wave regime,
which is the key ingredient to determine the GW
spectrum from sound waves.
We evaluated the asymptotic limits of
F_{\rm L}
at large and small
scales, which depend on the asymptotic limits of the original

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
function, and on the eighth (large-scales limit) and
second (small-scales limit) moments
of the distribution
function of the bubble lifetimes [see Eqs. (
5.3
) and (
5.6
)].
We predict the positions of the velocity power spectrum
F_{\rm L}
,

\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
, which are related to
z_{1}

and
z_{2}
, as indicated in Eq. (
5.13
),

\frac{\tilde{k}_{1,2}^{\rm exp}}{z_{1,2}}\simeq\{0.17,0.21\}\,,\qquad\frac{\tilde{k}_{1,2}^{\rm sim}}{z_{1,2}}\simeq\{0.38,0.25\}\,.

(6.6)

The resulting
\tilde{k}_{1}\equiv k_{1}/\beta
and
\tilde{k}_{2}\equiv k_{2}/\beta

determine the characteristic
scales of
F_{\rm L}
, given as a fraction of the nucleation
rate parameter
\beta
, which can be related to the mean separation of the bubbles

R_{\ast}
using Eqs. (
3.30
) and (
D.17
).
The two scales
\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
have different
ratios to the original scales
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, hence implying that
the particular nucleation distribution can impact not only
the position of the resulting scales, but also the intermediate slope
of the velocity spectrum, which can be computed using Eq. (
5.12
).
However, we find that the ratios
\tilde{k}_{1,2}/z_{1,2}
are independent
of
\xi_{w}
and
\alpha
and, hence,
only depend on the distribution function
of nucleation times.
We provided a single and double broken power law template
for the velocity spectrum
F_{\rm L}
, which only depends on the two scales

\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
, on the intermediate slope
\sigma
,
and on the amplitude in the
k\to 0
limit,
F_{\rm L}^{0}
, given in Eq. (
5.3
).
We also showed that, instead of using
F_{\rm L}^{0}
, we can use the integral
of
F_{\rm L}
over wave numbers, which is determined by the efficiency factor

\kappa
[see Eq. (
4.6
) and Fig.
13
]

\kappa\simeq\kappa_{\rm pert}\equiv\frac{4}{\xi_{w}^{3}\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)\,\xi^{2}{\rm\,d}\xi\,,

(6.7)

where
\kappa_{\rm pert}
approximates

\kappa
in the linear regime
of small fluid perturbations.

Based on analytical relations between the amplitude and the
integral of the single and double broken power law templates presented
in App.
E
, we provided in Sec.
5.2
a template for the velocity spectrum
in which the amplitude is determined by
\kappa
.

The main result of our work is a generic double broken
power law template of the velocity spectrum at the beginning
of the sound-wave regime, given
in Eqs. (
5.17
–
5.2
), that contains the
minimal required information from the original velocity profiles,
described by:

1.

The two scales
\tilde{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{k}_{2}
of the
velocity power spectral density
F_{\rm L}
, which are characterized by
either the positions of the discontinuities of the
single-bubble profiles
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)

or the positions
of the front and the back of the fluid shells, and a constant
factor that depends on the nucleation distribution function;

2.

The overall amplitude, which can be recast in terms of the
integral over of the kinetic
energy density fraction of the single-bubble profile,
\kappa
,
given in Eq. (
4.4
);

3.

The intermediate slope
\sigma
, which is computed using the
two asymptotic limits of the function
{f^{\prime}}(z)
and integrals over
the bubble lifetime distributions, as given in Eq. (
5.12
), and shown
in Fig.
18
.

The spectral breaks and slopes found in the velocity spectrum
determine the power
spectrum of the anisotropic stresses and of the GW spectrum produced in the sound-wave
phase.
Hence, the two scales that we identified in the velocity spectrum indicate the
origin of the two scales that are usually considered in the literature
for GW backgrounds from first-order phase transitions sourced by
sound waves, based on theoretical modeling following
the sound shell model and on numerical simulations.
These findings are crucial because they predict the geometric parameters of the GW spectrum arising from first-order
phase transitions, which can then be used to reconstruct the thermodynamic
and fundamental parameters. These respectively describe the dynamics
of first-order phase transitions and the underlying fundamental BSM theories leading to the first-order phase transition
[
29
]
.
Providing simple analytical formulas for the geometric parameters can
significantly improve the efficiency
of our studies of parameter reconstruction from
the GW signals, allowing us to explore a broader range of phase
transition parameters efficiently, and allowing us to explore new physics
from GW observables in the early Universe.

Data availability

The calculations that are provided in this work have been included in the
public Python package
CosmoGW

[
109
,
101
,
116
]
, which allow
to compute the single-bubble velocity profiles,
the velocity spectrum during the bubble expansion phase, and the velocity
spectrum at the initial time of the sound-wave regime.
All the results and plots presented in our work
can be reproduced using
CosmoGW
,
where we have also included the libraries
to compute the GW spectrum in the sound shell model, as presented in ref.
[
106
]
.
Updated documentation and tutorials
can be found at
Read the Docs
.

Appendix A
Hydrodynamics of an expanding bubble

In this section, following refs.
[
48
,
64
]
, we solve the hydrodynamic conservation laws,
assuming spherical symmetry for a single broken-phase
bubble expanding at a terminal
velocity
\xi_{w}
,
such that the resulting fluid velocity and enthalpy profiles,

v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
and
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
,
can be described in terms of the reduced parameter
\xi\equiv r/t
,
where
r
is the distance to the center of the bubble and
t
is the time since its nucleation.
We provide a Python library,
hydro_bubbles
,
as part of the
CosmoGW
public code
[
109
,
101
]

that computes
the self-similar profiles for different wall velocities and strengths of the phase transition,
following the description given in this appendix.

The hydrodynamic equations can be found by projecting the fluid conservation laws,

\partial_{\mu}T^{{\mu\nu}}=0\,,

(A.1)

in the parallel
and perpendicular directions of the fluid

[
48
,
97
,
105
]
,
where
T^{\mu\nu}\equiv w\,U^{\mu}U^{\nu}+p\,\eta^{\mu\nu}
models
a perfect fluid [see Eq. (
2.1
)], i.e., a fluid in local thermal equilibrium,
described in Minkowski space-time with metric tensor
\eta^{\mu\nu}
.
The conservation laws obtained from Eq. (
A.1
) are exact when
we are describing processes much shorter than one Hubble time, such
that the expansion of the Universe can be neglected.
As mentioned in Sec.
2
, in our work we assume short phase transition
durations, such that
\beta/H_{\ast}\gg 1
.
Moreover, we consider the bag equation of state to describe
the scalar field such that, out of the bubbles,
T^{\mu\nu}
is
only described by the fluid, as discussed at the beginning of Sec.
2
.

A.1
Spherically symmetric fluid solutions

Spherical symmetry implies that

U^{\mu}=\gamma(1,v\,\hat{r}^{i})
, being
v
the radial velocity,

\gamma=(1-v^{2})^{-1/2}
the corresponding Lorentz factor,
and
\hat{r}^{i}
the unitary radial vector.
Moreover, we express space-time derivatives with respect to the self-similar
variable
\xi
,

t\partial_{t}=-\xi\partial_{\xi}\,,\qquad r\partial_{r}=\xi\partial_{\xi}\,,

(A.2)

where we omit any residual time dependence not incorporated in
\xi
,
due to the assumption
that the fields have reached stationary self-similar profiles.
Projection of Eq. (
A.1
) in the parallel direction
to the fluid velocity
leads to the equation of energy conservation,

U_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}T^{{\mu\nu}}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad U^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}e+w\partial_{\mu}U^{\mu}=0\,,

(A.3)

where we have used the properties
U^{\mu}U_{\mu}=-1
and

U_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}U^{\nu}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\partial_{\mu}(U^{\nu}U_{\nu})=0
.
Using Eq. (
A.2
), the spherically symmetric version of the
continuity equation (
A.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}\ln w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{{\rm\,d}\xi}=\frac{1+c_{s}^{2}}{\xi-v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}\biggl(2\frac{v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{\xi}-\bigl[1-\xi\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)\bigr]\frac{{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{{\rm\,d}\xi}\biggr)\,.

(A.4)

On the other hand, the projection of Eq. (
A.1
) in the orthogonal direction using the projection
tensor
h^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}+U^{\mu}U^{\nu}
, with the property

h^{\mu}_{\ \,\nu}\,U^{\nu}=0
, yields the relativistic Euler equation,

\displaystyle h^{\alpha}_{\ \,\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad wU^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}U^{\alpha}+\partial^{\alpha}p+U^{\alpha}U^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}p=0\,,

(A.5)

which in the direction parallel to
\hat{r}^{i}
becomes

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}\ln w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{{\rm\,d}\xi}=\gamma^{2}_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)(1+c_{s}^{-2})\,\mu(\xi,v_{\text{\tiny ip}})\frac{{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{{\rm\,d}\xi}\,.

(A.6)

The Lorentz transformation of the velocity is

\mu(\xi,v)\equiv\frac{\xi-v}{1-\xi v}\,.

(A.7)

Combining Eqs. (
A.4
) and (
A.6
), we obtain the differential equation for
the radial velocity field
[
48
]
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{{\rm\,d}\xi}

\displaystyle=2\,\frac{v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{\xi}\frac{1-v_{\text{\tiny ip}}^{2}(\xi)}{1-\xi\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}\biggl(\frac{\mu^{2}(\xi,v_{\text{\tiny ip}})}{c_{s}^{2}}-1\biggr)^{-1}\,.

(A.8)

We note that Eq. (
A.8
) has a singularity point at
\mu(\xi,v)=c_{\rm s}
, i.e., when
v=\mu(\xi,c_{\rm s})
.
Hence, since
physical solutions are not allowed to cross this singular point,
the velocity profile

v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
remains monotonic at each side of the bubble wall (it can either increase or decrease,
but the sign of
{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}/{\rm\,d}\xi
can only change across a discontinuity).
To find the fixed points of the trajectory expressed by Eq. (
A.8
), we write it
in terms of a path parameter
q
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}}{{\rm\,d}q}=2\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\,v_{\rm ip}\,(1-v_{\rm ip})^{2}\,(1-\xi v_{\rm ip})\ ,

(A.9a)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}\xi}{{\rm\,d}q}=\xi\bigl[(\xi-v_{\rm ip})^{2}-c_{\rm s}^{2}\,(1-\xi v_{\rm ip})^{2}\bigr]\,.

(A.9b)

Fixed points (fp)
are then found by imposing
{\rm\,d}v_{\rm ip}/{\rm\,d}q={\rm\,d}\xi/{\rm\,d}q=0

[
48
]
,

(\xi,v_{\text{\tiny ip}})_{\rm fp}=\{(0,0),(c_{\rm s},0),(1,1)\}\,.

(A.10)

Equation (
A.8
) for
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
is decoupled from Eq. (
A.6
), so it can be solved first, and then Eq. (
A.6
) can be
integrated to find the enthalpy profile
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
,

w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)=w_{0}\exp\biggl[\bigl(1+c_{\rm s}^{-2}\bigr)\int_{v_{0}}^{v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}\gamma^{2}\mu(\xi,v)\,{\rm\,d}v\biggr]\,,

(A.11)

where
v_{0}
is a reference velocity for
which the value of the enthalpy,
w_{0}
, is known.

The enthalpy
w
can alternatively be described by the
energy density fluctuations

\lambda\equiv(e-e_{\rm sym})/w_{n}
,
where
e_{\rm sym}=e_{n}+\epsilon
refers to the radiation energy density
in the region where the fluid is not perturbed outside the bubble
in the symmetric phase
[
63
,
53
]
,
being
e_{n}
and
w_{n}
the background
energy density and enthalpy evaluated
at the nucleation temperature
T_{n}
.
This field is useful to describe the fluid perturbations during the sound-wave
regime in refs.
[
64
,
60
,
106
,
105
]
,
as we discuss in ref.
[
104
]
.
As mentioned in Sec.
2
, we restrict the description of the
thermal potential from the phase transition to the bag constant
\epsilon
,
such that
\epsilon_{+}=\epsilon
and
\epsilon_{-}=0
,
and to a radiation equation of state with

c_{\rm s}^{2}=\partial p/\partial e=\tfrac{1}{3}

in both the symmetric and broken phases.
This choice corresponds to the bag equation of state

[
33
,
48
]
,
and the density fluctuations
become

\lambda_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)=\tfrac{3}{4}\Bigl(\tfrac{w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)}{w_{n}}-1\Bigr)-\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}{\textstyle{3\over 4}}\alpha&{\rm\ when\ }\xi\leq\xi_{w}\,,\\
0&{\rm\ when\ }\xi>\xi_{w}\,,\end{array}\right.

(A.12)

where we have defined
\alpha={\textstyle{4\over 3}}\epsilon/w_{n}
as the parameter
that describes the strength of
the phase transition.

A.2
Matching conditions across the bubble wall

The fluid across the bubble wall can be modeled as a
discontinuity in the limit of infinitesimally small bubble wall
thickness.
In this limit, the dynamics of the scalar field are restricted to the
bubble wall. Out of the bubbles, we assume that
\partial_{\xi}\varphi=0
and that
the fluid conservation laws describe the full dynamical system.
In the frame where the bubble wall is at rest, the equations of momentum and energy
conservation, Eq. (
A.1
), can be rewritten as
\partial_{r}T_{0r}=\partial_{r}T_{rr}=0
.
Integrating them across the wall leads to the following
conditions for the enthalpy, the pressure, and the velocity field in the wall frame,

\tilde{v}
, expressed as
[
48
,
60
]

\displaystyle w_{+}\tilde{\gamma}_{+}^{2}\tilde{v}_{+}^{2}+p_{+}=w_{-}\tilde{\gamma}_{-}^{2}\tilde{v}_{-}^{2}+p_{-}\,,\qquad w_{+}\tilde{\gamma}_{+}^{2}\tilde{v}_{+}=w_{-}\tilde{\gamma}_{-}^{2}\tilde{v}_{-}\,.

(A.13)

These equations can be rearranged in the following way
[
48
]

\tilde{v}_{+}\tilde{v}_{-}=\frac{p_{+}-p_{-}}{e_{+}-e_{-}}\,,\qquad\frac{\tilde{v}_{+}}{\tilde{v}_{-}}=\frac{e_{-}+p_{+}}{e_{+}+p_{-}}\,.

(A.14)

The pressure and energy density in and outside the bubbles are given by

p_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{3}a_{\pm}T_{\pm}^{4}-\epsilon_{\pm}\,,\qquad e_{\pm}=a_{\pm}T_{\pm}^{4}+\epsilon_{\pm}\,,

(A.15)

with
\epsilon_{-}=0
and
\epsilon_{+}=\epsilon
in the bag equation of state.
The relations in
Eq. (
A.14
) then become
[
48
,
60
,
52
]

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{+}\tilde{v}_{-}=\frac{1-(1-3\alpha_{+})\,r}{3-3(1+\alpha_{+})\,r}\,,\qquad\frac{\tilde{v}_{+}}{\tilde{v}_{-}}=\frac{3+(1-3\alpha_{+})\,r}{1+3(1+\alpha_{+})\,r}\,,

(A.16)

where

\alpha_{+}=\frac{4}{3}\frac{\epsilon}{w_{+}}\quad\text{and}\quad r=\frac{w_{+}}{w_{-}}=\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{-}^{2}\tilde{v}_{-}}{\tilde{\gamma}_{+}^{2}\tilde{v}_{+}}\,.

(A.17)

The two matching conditions across the wall in
Eq. (
A.16
) can be combined to obtain the velocities
\tilde{v}_{\pm}

as a function of the parameter
\alpha_{+}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{+}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2(1+\alpha_{+})}\left[\tilde{v}_{-}+\frac{1}{3\tilde{v}_{-}}\pm\sqrt{\biggl(\tilde{v}_{-}-\frac{1}{3\tilde{v}_{-}}\biggr)^{2}+4\alpha_{+}^{2}+\frac{8}{3}\alpha_{+}}\ \right]\,,

(A.18a)

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{-}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left[(1+\alpha_{+})\tilde{v}_{+}+\frac{1-3\alpha_{+}}{3\tilde{v}_{+}}\pm\sqrt{\biggl[(1+\alpha_{+})\tilde{v}_{+}+\frac{1-3\alpha_{+}}{3\tilde{v}_{+}}\biggr]^{2}-\frac{4}{3}}\ \right]\,.

(A.18b)

Equivalently, we can also express
\alpha_{+}
as

\alpha_{+}=\frac{(1-\tilde{v}_{+}/\tilde{v}_{-})(1-3\tilde{v}_{+}\tilde{v}_{-})}{3\,(\tilde{v}_{+}^{2}-1)}\,.

(A.19)

The strength of the phase transition is described by the parameter
\alpha\equiv{\textstyle{4\over 3}}\,\Delta\theta/w_{n}
, which reduces to
\alpha=\epsilon/e_{n}
in the
bag equation of state.
The relation between
\alpha
and
\alpha_{+}

is straightforward if
w_{+}/w_{n}
is known:
\alpha\,w_{n}=\alpha_{+}\,w_{+}
.

A.3
Classification of solutions

To find the boundary conditions to solve Eq. (
A.8
), we take into
account that the fluid is at rest,
v=0
, at
\xi=0
due to radial symmetry
and at
\xi\geq 1
due to causality.
Furthermore,
depending on the bubble wall speed
\xi_{w}
and the phase transition strength
\alpha
,
we distinguish three types of solutions: detonations, subsonic deflagrations,
and supersonic deflagrations (also known as hybrids)
[
48
]
.
In the following subsections,
we describe the boundary conditions across the
bubble wall leading to each of these types of solutions.
The radial profiles of the velocity,
v_{\rm ip}(\xi)
,
and the enthalpy,
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
, obtained
in the bag equation of state for the different types of solutions
are shown in Fig.
1

for a benchmark value of

\alpha=0.1
and a range of wall velocities
\xi_{w}
from 0.1 to 1.

The solutions shown in Fig.
1
are a subset of
the generic dynamical solutions
v(\xi)
to Eq. (
A.8
),
represented by the gray curves in the left panel of Fig.
19
.
This subset of solutions is obtained by integrating the equations of motion starting
at the
(\xi,v)
point determined
by the boundary conditions and ending either at a fixed point or when a fluid
shock is formed.
The resulting fluid velocity is then perturbed within a fluid shell

\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
and becomes zero outside the shell.
The right panel of Fig.
19
shows the velocity at both sides of the wall
in the wall reference frame,
\tilde{v}_{\pm}
, for different
values of
\alpha_{+}
.
The relation
\tilde{v}_{+}
vs
\tilde{v}_{-}
can be computed
using Eqs. (
A.18
).

Figure 19
:
Left panel:
Generic solutions
v(\xi)
(gray lines) to Eq. (
A.8
) that
start at the boundary conditions (colored dots) of each solution
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)

shown in Fig.
1
for
\alpha=0.1
.
Physical solutions occur below the diagonal dashed line
v=\xi

to ensure
\tilde{v}_{+}\geq 0
.
The other two black dashed lines indicate respectively
the position of the shock formation

(\xi_{\text{sh}},v_{\text{sh}})
[see Eq. (
A.22
)] and the
singularity point
\mu(\xi,v)=c_{\rm s}
, where
{\rm\,d}v/{\rm\,d}\xi\to-\infty
[see Eq. (
A.7
)].

Right panel:
Possible branches of
\tilde{v}_{+}
and
\tilde{v}_{-}
for different values of
\alpha_{+}

(10 uniformly log-spaced values between 0.01 and 1) are shown by solid blue and purple lines,
being
\tilde{v}_{+}=\tilde{v}_{-}
and
\tilde{v}_{+}\tilde{v}_{-}=\tfrac{1}{3}

the limiting branches when
\alpha=0
.
For
\alpha_{+}>\tfrac{1}{3}
,
\tilde{v}_{+}<0
so no deflagration
solutions exist (see blue lines).
The lower left branch indicates deflagrations with
\tilde{v}_{\pm}\leq c_{\rm s}
, while
the upper right branch,
\tilde{v}_{\pm}>c_{\rm s}
,
indicates detonations.
For hybrids,
\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
(see vertical dashed line).
The values corresponding to the solutions in Fig.
1

are shown as colorful dots, where we note that
\alpha_{+}(\xi_{w})<\alpha

for deflagrations, so the dots are not aligned with
any constant
\alpha_{+}
curves. Colors of dots correspond to the
values of
\xi_{w}
indicated in Fig.
1
.

Detonations

A detonation occurs when the bubble wall propagates faster than any perturbation
in the fluid, such that the fluid is at rest
outside the bubble,
\tilde{v}_{+}=\xi_{w}>c_{\rm s}
.
The relative velocity
\tilde{v}_{-}
can then be computed using Eq. (
A.18b
),
and converted to a velocity in the global reference frame by
its Lorentz transform,
v_{-}=\mu(\xi_{w},\tilde{v}_{-})
, using Eq. (
A.7
).
The solution
v_{\rm ip}(\xi)
for
c_{\rm s}\leq\xi\leq\xi_{w}
is obtained by integrating Eq. (
A.8
)
from the bubble wall at
(\xi,v)=(\xi_{w},v_{-})
inwards
until the stationary
point
(c_{\rm s},0)
is reached
[
48
]
.
Hence, at
\xi
in the range
c_{\rm s}\leq\xi\leq\xi_{w}
, we require that
the velocity increases with
\xi
,
{\rm\,d}v_{\rm ip}/{\rm\,d}\xi>0
.
This condition implies that
\tilde{v}_{-}\geq c_{\rm s}
, as it can be inferred from Eq. (
A.8
),
which can only be obtained by taking the positive argument in Eq. (
A.18b
).
In detonations, the front and back positions of the fluid shell
are then
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
and
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
,
and only one discontinuity is present at
\xi_{w}
.
In addition, detonation
solutions exist only if the integrand in Eq. (
A.18b
) is positive.
This condition provides the minimum value of
\xi_{w}
that leads to a detonation,

\xi_{w}=\tilde{v}_{+}\geq v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha_{+})=\frac{1+\sqrt{\alpha_{+}(2+3\alpha_{+})}}{\sqrt{3}(1+\alpha_{+})}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\,,

(A.20)

known as the Chapman-Jouget speed
v_{\rm CJ}
.
At smaller wall velocities
\xi_{w}
, a deflagration solution will develop.
When
\xi_{w}=v_{\rm CJ}
, we find the limiting case
\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
.
Finally, Eq. (
A.11
) is integrated in the same range of
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})

to obtain the enthalpy profile
w_{\rm ip}(\xi)
.
We start the integration at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
, where
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{-}

and the value
w_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=w_{-}
is found from the matching condition in Eq. (
A.13
),

\frac{w_{-}}{w_{n}}=\frac{w_{-}}{w_{+}}=\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{+}^{2}\tilde{v}_{+}}{\tilde{\gamma}_{-}^{2}\tilde{v}_{-}}\,.

(A.21)

Since the fluid is unperturbed in the outer side of the wall,
we have
w_{+}=w_{n}
and
\alpha_{+}=\alpha
.

Subsonic deflagrations

Subsonic deflagrations take place when the fluid perturbations can propagate
faster than the wall,
\xi_{w}\leq c_{\rm s}
.
In this case,
the fluid is at rest
inside the bubble so
\tilde{v}_{-}=\xi_{w}
and
\tilde{v}_{+}
is computed using Eq. (
A.18a
).
The value of the velocity just outside the wall is given by its Lorentz transform,

v_{+}=\mu(\xi_{w},\tilde{v}_{+})=(\xi_{w}-\tilde{v}_{+})/(1-\xi_{w}\tilde{v}_{+})
,
and positivity of
v_{+}>0
requires taking
the negative argument in Eq. (
A.18a
).
In addition, to ensure that the fluid velocity is always smaller
than the wall velocity, we require that
\tilde{v}_{+}\geq 0
.
This can only be achieved when
\alpha_{+}\leq{1\over 3}
, indicating
that subsonic deflagrations are not possible for larger values of
\alpha_{+}
.
This upper bound on
\alpha_{+}
leads to an upper bound

\alpha\leq\alpha_{\rm max}\simeq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}(1-\xi_{w})^{-13/10}
, found empirically in
ref.
[
48
]
, where
\xi_{w}\leq c_{\rm s}
.
Hence, subsonic deflagrations can only be found for

\alpha\lesssim 1.02
.
Figure
2
shows the value of
\alpha
found numerically
for
\alpha_{+}=\tfrac{1}{3}
for deflagrations, compared to the analytical fit
given in ref.
[
48
]
.

The fluid velocity outside the bubble is then computed solving Eq. (
A.8
), and it decreases
until a shock front is encountered and the velocity field drops to zero.
The appearance of the shock does not allow the solution to
cross the singularity in
{\rm\,d}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}/{\rm\,d}\xi

at
\mu(\xi,v)=c_{\rm s}
that would
lead to an unphysical double-valued function
\xi(v)
.
Since the region where the shock occurs is in the symmetric phase, the trace
anomaly across the shock is zero, i.e.,
\alpha_{+}^{\text{sh}}=0
and, hence,
the matching condition in Eq. (
A.16
) yields
\tilde{v}_{+}^{\text{sh}}\,\tilde{v}_{-}^{\text{sh}}={1\over 3}
,
where now
\pm
denote the values at the left and right of the shock.
At
\xi\geq\xi_{\text{sh}}
, the fluid is at rest, with

\tilde{v}_{+}^{\text{sh}}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
and

v_{-}^{\text{sh}}=\mu(\xi_{\text{sh}},\tilde{v}_{-}^{\text{sh}})=\frac{\xi_{\text{sh}}-\tilde{v}_{-}^{\text{sh}}}{1-\tilde{v}_{-}^{\text{sh}}\,\xi_{\text{sh}}}=\frac{3\xi_{\text{sh}}^{2}-1}{2\xi_{\text{sh}}}\,.

(A.22)

It follows that this condition is equivalent to
\xi_{\text{sh}}\,\mu(\xi_{\text{sh}},v_{-}^{\text{sh}})={1\over 3}
.
In this case, the positions of the front and the back of the fluid shell are
respectively
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}\geq c_{\rm s}
and
\xi_{\rm b}=\xi_{w}
,
and a discontinuity is present at both locations.
We note that even though a shock always forms, as
\xi_{w}
decreases,
the position of the shock becomes closer
to
c_{\rm s}
and, hence, the velocity jump across the shock becomes close
to zero for small
\xi_{w}
.

The enthalpy is obtained by integrating Eq. (
A.11
) once
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi)
is known.
We start by the value at the inner side of the shock,
which, using Eq. (
A.13
), is

\frac{w_{-}^{\text{sh}}}{w_{n}}=\frac{w_{-}^{\text{sh}}}{w_{+}^{\text{sh}}}=\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{-}^{\rm sh\,^{2}}\tilde{v}_{-}^{\text{sh}}}{{\tilde{\gamma}_{+}^{\rm sh\,^{2}}}\tilde{v}_{+}^{\text{sh}}}=\frac{9\xi_{\text{sh}}^{2}-1}{3(1-\xi_{\text{sh}}^{2})}\,,

(A.23)

and integrate inwards in the range
\xi_{w}\leq\xi\leq\xi_{\text{sh}}
until
w_{+}/w_{n}
.
As mentioned above, for small values of
\xi_{w}
,
the presence of a shock is negligible, and we can
integrate inwards from
\xi_{\text{sh}}\simeq c_{\rm s}
, where
w_{-}^{\text{sh}}\simeq w_{n}
, until
w_{+}/w_{n}
.
Inside the bubble at
\xi<\xi_{w}
,
we use the matching condition in Eq. (
A.13
)
to find
w_{-}
from
w_{+}
.

For subsonic deflagrations, the fluid outside the bubble
is perturbed up to
\xi_{\text{sh}}>\xi_{w}
.
Hence, the enthalpy on the outer side of the bubble wall is different from its unperturbed
value at the
nucleation temperature,
w_{+}>w_{n}
, such that
\alpha_{+}<\alpha

[
48
,
60
,
51
]
.
Since we are in general
interested in computing the velocity and enthalpy profiles for
a given value of
\alpha
at
T_{n}
, instead of for
\alpha_{+}
,
we need to use a
shooting algorithm based on the Newton-Raphson method to iteratively
solve the fluid profiles.
First,
we start with the guess
\alpha_{+}^{(0)}=\alpha
.
Then, we compute

w_{+}^{(0)}/w_{n}
and
\alpha^{(0)}
from the solution and correct the guess

\alpha_{+}^{(1)}\to\alpha^{(0)}w_{n}/w_{+}^{(0)}
.
We proceed iteratively until the correct value of
\alpha_{+}
that leads to
the desired
\alpha
outside the bubble is found.

Supersonic deflagrations (hybrids)

Supersonic deflagrations, also known as hybrids,
take place at intermediate wall speeds,
c_{\rm s}\leq\xi_{w}\leq v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)
[see Eq. (
A.20
)].
At larger
\xi_{w}
, the solution becomes a detonation, such that

\alpha_{+}=\alpha
and hence
v_{\rm CJ}
denoting the boundary
between detonations and hybrids can be evaluated from
\alpha
, as done in Eq. (
2.6
).
In this case, the fluid is perturbed at both sides of the bubble wall.
Outside the bubble wall there is a
shock wave, as in a subsonic deflagration; however, in this case
\xi_{w}>\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
because there is also
a rarefaction wave propagating inwards until the
stationary point
(\xi,v)=(c_{\rm s},0)
is reached inside the bubble.
One thus solves Eq. (
A.8
) inwards, starting at the wall where

v_{-}=\mu(\xi_{w},c_{\rm s})
, to find the fluid velocity inside the bubble.
Like in a subsonic deflagration, outside the bubble wall the velocity decreases down to the formation of a shock front.
To find the fluid perturbations outside the bubble,
we now integrate outwards with
v_{+}=\mu(\xi_{w},\tilde{v}_{+})
, where
\tilde{v}_{+}
is computed
from
\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
using Eq. (
A.18a
), until the shock front is reached [see Eq. (
A.22
)].
In this case, the fluid shell is confined between
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}>\xi_{w}>c_{\rm s}
and
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
,
and it presents a discontinuity inside the fluid shell, at
\xi_{w}
, and a second
discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm f}
.
As for subsonic deflagrations, to avoid negative
v_{+}
, we take the negative argument in Eq. (
A.18a
),
and to avoid negative
\tilde{v}_{+}
, we require
\alpha_{+}\leq{1\over 3}
,
again corresponding to
\alpha_{\rm max}\simeq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}(1-\xi_{w})^{-13/10}

[
48
]
.
Finally, we integrate Eq. (
A.11
) to obtain the enthalpy profile
w_{\rm ip}(\xi)

and use
w_{-}^{\text{sh}}
and
v_{-}^{\text{sh}}
for the reference values, as done for subsonic
deflagrations, until
\xi_{w}
is reached.
Then,
w_{-}
is computed from
w_{+}
using the matching condition in Eq. (
A.13
), and the enthalpy
profile inside the bubble can be found integrating inwards from
\xi_{w}
to
c_{\rm s}
.

The resulting
\alpha
is again larger than
\alpha_{+}
and,
as in subsonic deflagrations, we need to perform a shooting algorithm to find the value of
\alpha_{+}
that yields
the desired value of
\alpha
.
We note that when
\alpha_{+}<\alpha
, the condition

v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha)>\xi_{w}>v_{\rm CJ}(\alpha_{+})
could be reached, naively
indicating a detonation solution.
This can be seen for example
in Fig.
2
, where for
\alpha_{+}=\tfrac{1}{3}
, one
would naively find
v_{\rm CJ}(\tfrac{1}{3})=\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
.
However, notice that the condition in Eq. (
A.20
) for the solution to
be a detonation is on

\tilde{v}_{+}
, not on
\xi_{w}
, and the two are different for hybrids, where

\tilde{v}_{+}
is found from the matching conditions
with
\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
.
From
\tilde{v}_{-}=c_{\rm s}
one finds an upper bound on
\tilde{v}_{+}\leq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
,
which is then always smaller than
v_{\rm CJ}\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
,
allowing for supersonic deflagrations to always exist as long as

\alpha\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 3}}(1-\xi_{w})^{-13/10}
(see Fig.
2
).

Appendix B
Constant and linear toy models for the velocity profiles

In Sec.
2.5
, the quadratic
and linear-constant toy models for the velocity profiles have been introduced.
The former model is used
to mimic subsonic deflagrations and detonations,
while the latter model is used for hybrids.
We have argued that such profiles represent the simplest choice
that accurately reproduces the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
from the full velocity profiles.
In this section, we explore the simpler constant and linear toy models
and compare them to the ones presented in Sec.
2.5
,
as well as to the numerical profiles.

B.1
Toy models for subsonic deflagrations

Subsonic deflagrations are, in general, characterized
by two discontinuities: one at
\xi_{\rm b}=\xi_{w}
,
and another one at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}
.
The corresponding velocity profiles are continuous in the
range
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
.
The simplest choice to mimic such profiles is the constant toy model,

\displaystyle v_{\rm const}(\xi)=v_{\rm const}\,\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,

(B.1)

which corresponds to the general quadratic profile in Eq. (
2.20
)
with
C_{2}=C_{1}=0
and
C_{0}=v_{\rm const}
.
Using this toy model, we can either reproduce the
z\to 0

or the
z\to\infty
limit of the envelope of the
f^{\prime}(z)
function,
but not both limits.
In particular, to reproduce the latter,
the condition in Eq. (
2.23b
) reduces
to the one obtained from Eq. (
2.15
), which is less restrictive since
it does not depend on
z
,

\displaystyle v_{\rm const}(\xi_{\rm f}+\xi_{\rm b})=\xi_{\text{sh}}v_{\text{sh}}+\xi_{w}|v_{+}-v_{-}|\,.

(B.2)

From Eq. (
2.23a
) and Eq. (
B.2
),
we then find the following choices for
v_{\rm const}
,

\displaystyle v_{\rm const}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{0}=\langle v\rangle_{{\rm sph}\,4\rm d}\,,&\ \quad\text{to reproduce $f^{\prime}(z)$ in the $z\to 0$ limit}\,,\\
v_{\infty}=\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{\rm b}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})+\xi_{\rm f}\,v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})}{\xi_{+}}\,,&\ \quad\text{to reproduce $f^{\prime}_{\rm env}$ in the $z\to\infty$ limit}\,,\end{array}\right.

where
\xi_{+}=\xi_{w}+\xi_{\text{sh}}
,
and we have defined the spherical-shell average in
N
dimensions as

\langle v\rangle_{{\rm sph}\,N\rm d}=\frac{N}{\xi_{\rm f}^{N}-\xi_{\rm b}^{N}}\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{N-1}v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,.

(B.5)

We note that in the case of subsonic deflagrations with
\xi_{w}\lesssim{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,v_{\rm CJ}
,
the discontinuity at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}

becomes negligible and
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{\text{sh}}\simeq 0
.

If we are also interested in reproducing the oscillations of
f^{\prime}(z)
at large
z

(not just its envelope), the simplest choice to do so is
a linearly decreasing toy model,

\displaystyle v_{\rm lin,dec}(\xi)=\frac{(\xi_{\rm f}-\xi)v_{\rm b}+(\xi-\xi_{\rm b})v_{\rm f}}{\Delta\xi}\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,\qquad\text{with \ }v_{\rm b}>v_{\rm f}\,,

(B.6)

which corresponds to choosing
C_{2}=0
,

C_{1}=(v_{\rm f}-v_{\rm b})/\Delta\xi
, and

C_{0}=v_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm f}\,C_{1}=(v_{\rm b}\xi_{\rm f}-v_{\rm f}\xi_{\rm b})/\Delta\xi
in Eq. (
2.20
).
This toy model is characterized by
two constants,
v_{\rm b}
and
v_{\rm f}
.
Fixing
v_{\rm f}=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{\text{sh}}
, the constant
v_{\rm b}
can be chosen to exactly match the amplitude of
either of the two independent
z\to 0
and

z\to\infty
limits of
f^{\prime}(z)
[see Eqs. (
2.10
) and (
2.14b
)],

\displaystyle v_{\rm b}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{0}={\cal A}_{f}\displaystyle\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{3}v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi-v_{\rm f}{\cal A}_{f}/{\cal A}_{b}\,,&\ \quad\text{to
reproduce $f^{\prime}(z)$ in the $z\to 0$ limit}\,,\\
v_{\infty}=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})=v_{+}\,,&\ \quad\text{to
reproduce $f^{\prime}(z)$ in the $z\to\infty$ limit}\,,\end{array}\right.

where

{\cal A}_{f}=\frac{20\,\Delta\xi}{\xi_{\rm f}^{5}+4\xi_{\rm b}^{5}-5\xi_{\rm f}\xi_{\rm b}^{4}}\,,\qquad{\cal A}_{b}=\frac{20\,\Delta\xi}{\xi_{\rm b}^{5}+4\xi_{\rm f}^{5}-5\xi_{\rm b}\xi_{\rm f}^{4}}\,.

(B.9)

Figure 20
:

Comparison of the velocity profiles (left panels) and the
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
functions (right panels),
for
\alpha=0.1
, and two values of
\xi_{w}=0.3
and 0.5,
corresponding both to subsonic deflagrations.
The deflagration with
\xi_{w}=0.3
does
not present a visible shock at
\xi_{\rm f}\to c_{\rm s}
(upper panels),
while the deflagration with
\xi_{w}=0.5
presents
a distinguishable shock at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\text{sh}}>c_{\rm s}

(lower panels).
The results for the full numerical profiles are shown
in black lines and compared to
the constant (dotted), linearly decreasing (dashed), and quadratic (dash-dotted) toy models.
The constant and linear toy models can only reproduce either the
z\to 0
or the
z\to\infty

limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
based on the choice of the model parameters
[see Eqs. (
B.1
) and (
B.1
)].
On the other hand,
the quadratic toy model
can accurately reproduce both limits for the choice
of parameters given in Eq. (
2.26
).
We show black vertical lines
at
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, defined in Eqs. (
2.12
) and (
2.16
) for
deflagrations, which accurately predict
the locations of the

{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
breaks for all cases (numerical profiles
and toy models).

In Fig.
20
,
we compare the numerical profiles to the toy models for two choices of the wall velocity,
corresponding to subsonic deflagrations
where, for one case, the velocity jump at the shock is negligible
(
\xi_{w}=0.3
with
\xi_{\text{sh}}\to c_{\rm s})

and, for the other case, the shock presents a clear
discontinuity
v_{\text{sh}}
at
\xi_{\text{sh}}
(
\xi_{w}=0.5
).
We compare the toy models presented here, the quadratic model
presented in Sec.
2.5.1
, and show how the quadratic toy model
is the only one capable of accurately reproducing both asymptotic limits
of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
function, including the oscillations at large
values of
z
.
The constant and linear toy models, even though simpler,
fail to reproduce both asymptotic limits at the same time,
since they do not have enough parameters to satisfy
both constraints in Eqs. (
2.23
).
Furthermore, while the linear toy model can reproduce
the oscillations at large
z
for the choice of

v_{\rm b}=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})
and
v_{\rm f}=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})
,
the constant toy model can only capture the
envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}_{\!\!\!\rm env}
using
v_{\rm const}=v_{\infty}
as
indicated in Eq. (
B.1
).
We note that for
all the toy models presented, we have chosen

\xi_{\rm f}
and
\xi_{\rm b}
to be those of the numerical
profiles, and we find that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are
accurately predicting the positions of the breaks for
all cases (see Fig.
20
).
This result further reinforces the finding in Sec.
2.3

that these scales only depend on
\xi_{\rm f}
and
\xi_{\rm b}
.
Furthermore, since
z_{1,2}
and the limit
z\to 0

and
z\to\infty
of
f^{\prime}(z)
are reproduced, the quadratic toy
models predict the same slope
\gamma
at intermediate
z

as the original profiles.

B.2
Toy models for detonations

Detonation solutions for the velocity profile are characterized by a single discontinuity at

\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{w}
, and vanish at
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
.
Hence, as before, the simplest way to model detonations
is to choose a constant model as in Eq. (
B.1
).
We can then reproduce either the
z\to 0
or the
z\to\infty

limits of the envelope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
with the choices
already presented in Eq. (
B.1
), where in this case,
we set
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm b}^{+})\to 0
and
v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{-}
.
If we also want to reproduce the oscillations in the
z\to\infty
limit, then the simplest way is to model
the velocity profile with
a linearly increasing toy model
that vanishes at
\xi_{\rm b}
and is smooth in the range
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
,

\displaystyle v_{\rm lin,inc}(\xi)=v_{\rm f}\frac{\xi-\xi_{\rm b}}{\xi_{\rm f}-\xi_{\rm b}}\mathcal{X}_{(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})}(\xi)\,,

(B.10)

which corresponds to Eq. (
B.6
) setting
v_{\rm b}=0
.
Here, the constant
v_{\rm f}=v_{\rm lin,inc}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})
can be chosen to reproduce either of the
z\to 0
or
z\to\infty
limits of
f^{\prime}(z)
.
Similar choices to those presented in Eq. (
B.1
)
can be selected for
v_{\rm f}
, such that they
reproduce one of the two limits,

\displaystyle v_{\rm f}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{0}=\displaystyle{\cal A}_{b}\int_{\xi_{\rm b}}^{\xi_{\rm f}}\xi^{3}v_{\rm ip}(\xi){\rm\,d}\xi\,,&\ \quad\text{to
reproduce $f^{\prime}(z)$ in the $z\to 0$ limit}\,,\\
v_{\infty}=v_{\text{\tiny ip}}(\xi_{\rm f}^{-})=v_{-}\,,&\ \quad\text{to
reproduce $f^{\prime}(z)$ in the $z\to\infty$ limit}\,.\end{array}\right.

Figure 21
:

Comparison of the velocity profile (left) and the
function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
(right) from the numerical profile with those
obtained using the toy models
for
\alpha=0.1
and
\xi_{w}=0.9

(corresponding to a detonation).
We find that the constant and the linearly increasing toy models can reproduce either the

z\to 0
or the
z\to\infty
limits of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
for the choice of parameters indicated in the text,
but not both of them, as expected.
Although for this case, the difference between the toy models
and the numerical profiles is not large,
