the quadratic toy model is more accurate to reproduce
the numerical profile, being
able to exactly reproduce both limits.

In Fig.
21
, we compare the full numerical profiles to the toy models for a choice of the wall velocity corresponding to a detonation
(
\xi_{w}=0.9
). Also in this case we see that, even if
z_{1}

and
z_{2}
are captured by all the presented toy models,
only the quadratic toy model can accurately reproduce
both
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
limits at the same time,
and hence, also the intermediate slope of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
.

B.3
Toy models for hybrids

Hybrid velocity profiles are characterized by two discontinuities: one at

\xi_{w}>\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
,
and another one at
\xi_{\rm f}=\xi_{\rm sh}
.
Therefore, they present a discontinuity in the range
\xi\in(\xi_{\rm b},\xi_{\rm f})
.
The simplest way to mimic such a profile with two discontinuities is
using the constant toy model of Eq. (
B.1
).
However, in this case, since we found in Sec.
2.3
that the scales

z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are characterized by the positions of the discontinuities instead of by
\xi_{\rm b}
and
\xi_{\rm f}
,
we set
\xi_{\rm b}\to\xi_{w}
instead of
\xi_{\rm b}=c_{\rm s}
for the
constant toy model.
Since the constant toy model is characterized by a single parameter

v_{\rm const}
, it can only reproduce either the
z\to 0
or the
envelope of
f^{\prime}(z)
in the
z\to\infty
limit.
Furthermore,
setting
v_{\rm const}
to the value in Eq. (
B.2
),
the
z\to\infty
limit
cannot be satisfied for all
z
using this toy model,
which can only reproduce its envelope over oscillations.
If we want to alternatively reproduce the
z\to 0
limit of
f^{\prime}(z)
,
we can instead choose
v_{\rm const}=v_{0}
, where
v_{0}
is given
in Eq. (
B.1
).

In Fig.
22
, we compare the full numerical profiles to the constant
and linear-constant toy models for a choice of the wall velocity corresponding to a hybrid
solution (
\xi_{w}=0.7
).
We clearly see that a constant profile, contrary to the linear-constant toy model,
cannot accurately reproduce both limits
z\to 0
and the envelope
in the
z\to\infty
limit of
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
at the same time.

Figure 22
:

Comparison,
for
\alpha=0.1
and
\xi_{w}=0.7
(corresponding to a hybrid),
of the velocity profile (left) and the function
{f^{\prime}}^{2}(z)
(right)
obtained from the full profiles and
from the toy models.
The constant toy models
reproduce either the
z\to 0
or
z\to\infty
limit of
f^{\prime}(z)
.
The linear-constant (lc) toy model of Eq. (
2.27
)
exactly reproduces the
z\to 0
limit as well as the envelope of
f^{\prime}(z)

in the
z\to\infty
limit.

Appendix C
Correlations of statistically homogeneous and isotropic fields

The velocity field presented in Sec.
3
,
which is produced by multiple expanding bubbles with
nucleation locations distributed homogeneously in space,
is statistically homogeneous and isotropic.
In the present section, we review the statistical description of
this type of fields.
The generic results are applied to irrotational fields
in Sec.
C.3
.

C.1
Two-point correlation function

The two-point correlation function of a statistically homogeneous and
isotropic
field
\textstyle v
only depends on the separation vector
\textstyle r

between two points
\textstyle x
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}
.
Due to homogeneity and isotropy,
its tensor structure is given entirely by a linear combination
of the tensors
\delta_{ij}
and
\hat{r}_{i}\hat{r}_{j}
, invariant under spatial
rotations and translations, yielding the unique
2
2

2

We note that helical
fields violate invariance under mirror reflections,
which leads to an additional independent component

B_{\rm H}(r)
in Eq. (
C.1
), proportional to
\hat{r}_{l}\varepsilon_{lij}
,
to generically describe
B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})

(see, e.g., ref.
[
17
]
).
In the present work, we only consider non-helical fields with
B_{\rm H}=0
,
which implies that
\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,\omega_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\rangle=0
,
with
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}
.

decomposition
[
91
]

B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})=\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,v_{j}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})\rangle=(\delta_{ij}-\hat{r}_{i}\hat{r}_{j})\,B_{\rm N}(r)+\hat{r}_{i}\hat{r}_{j}\,B_{\rm L}(r)\,,

(C.1)

where the brackets indicate an average over ensemble realizations.
The results of this section are independent of the time
at which the fields in Eq. (
C.1
) are evaluated. Hence,
we omit any time dependence in the following.

B_{\rm N}(r)
and
B_{\rm L}(r)
are the normal (N) and longitudinal (L)
components of the correlation function,
computed respectively from the
perpendicular and the parallel components of the
field
\textstyle v
with respect to
\textstyle r
,

v_{\rm N}(r)
and
v_{\rm L}(r)
.
The normal and longitudinal correlation functions only depend on the
distance between the two points,
r=|\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}|
.

Because of the invariance under translations in coordinate space,
momenta are conserved in Fourier space,

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v_{j}^{*}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\int{\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}\int\,e^{i(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})}B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\int e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}}B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,{\cal F}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,,

(C.2)

where the spectral density
{\cal F}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})
has been defined as
the Fourier transform of
B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})
,
and it can be decomposed analogously in terms of
its longitudinal and normal components
[
91
]
,

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\equiv\int e^{i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}}B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}){\rm\,d}^{3}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}=P_{ij}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})\,F_{\rm N}(k)+\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}F_{\rm L}(k)\,,

(C.3)

where
P_{ij}(\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{})=\delta_{ij}-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}
is the projection
tensor perpendicular to
\hat{k}
.
The relation of the components of
{\cal F}_{ij}

to the longitudinal and normal components of
B_{ij}
is

[
91
]

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}(k)=

\displaystyle\,4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigl(j_{0}(kr)B_{\rm L}(r)+\frac{2j_{1}(kr)}{kr}\bigl[B_{\rm N}(r)-B_{\rm L}(r)\bigr]\Bigr)\,r^{2}{\rm\,d}r\,,

(C.4a)

\displaystyle F_{\rm N}(k)=

\displaystyle\,4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigl(j_{0}(kr)B_{\rm N}(r)+\frac{j_{1}(kr)}{kr}\bigl[B_{\rm L}(r)-B_{\rm N}(r)\bigr]\Bigr)\,r^{2}{\rm\,d}r\,,

(C.4b)

where
j_{n}(x)
is the
n
-th order spherical Bessel function
of the first kind.
In particular,
j_{0}(x)=\sin x/x
and
j_{1}(x)=\sin x/x^{2}-\cos x/x
.
We note that the value of
B_{ii}
at
r=0
corresponds to the
average value of the squared field
v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\langle v^{2}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\rangle
,

v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=B_{ii}(0)=2B_{\rm N}(0)+B_{\rm L}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{ii}(k)\,k^{2}{\rm\,d}k=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl[2F_{\rm N}(k)+F_{\rm L}(k)\bigr]k^{2}{\rm\,d}k\,.

(C.5)

The spectrum of the velocity field
\textstyle v
,
E_{v}(k)
, is defined from the trace of
the two-point correlation function in momentum space [see Eq. (
C.2
)]

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v_{i}^{*}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\rangle=(2\pi)^{6}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,\frac{E_{v}(k)}{4\pi k^{2}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad E_{v}(k)=\frac{k^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\bigl[2F_{\rm N}(k)+F_{\rm L}(k)\bigr]\,.

(C.6)

The spectrum is defined such that its integral over momenta
yields the average value of the squared field in physical space,

\displaystyle v_{\text{rms}}^{2}=\langle v^{2}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\rangle=

\displaystyle\,\int\frac{{\rm\,d}^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{{\rm\,d}^{3}k^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}\cdot(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})}\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v_{i}^{*}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{0}^{\infty}E_{v}(k){\rm\,d}k=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\cal P}_{v}(k){\rm\,d}\ln k\,,

(C.7)

where we have also defined the spectrum
{\cal P}_{v}(k)=k\,E_{v}(k)
, normalized
per unit of
\ln k
.
In the case of an irrotational velocity field with
F_{\rm N}=0
, as the
one we find in the bubble expansion phase, the relation

E_{v}(k)=k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k)/(2\pi)^{2}
is often given as a definition of the spectrum,
see, e.g., refs.
[
23
,
60
]
.
Similarly, solenoidal fields with
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}=0
(e.g., purely vortical fluids or magnetic fields
[
23
,
100
,
11
]
)
have
F_{\rm L}=0
and it is common to identify the
spectrum with the normal component,
F_{\rm N}
,
which is also required to be proportional to
k^{2}
or steeper (see discussion in
Sec.
C.2
and refs.
[
91
,
22
]
).
However, we note that the relation to the velocity spectrum has then
a factor of
1
instead of
2
in the denominator,
E_{v}(k)=k^{2}F_{\rm N}(k)/\pi^{2}
,
due to the different values of the trace of the tensors
P_{ij}
and
\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}
.

C.2
Large-scale limit and causality

The asymptotic limit at large scales
k\rightarrow 0
of the spectral density functions

F_{\rm L}(k)
and
F_{\rm N}(k)
is obtained
by Taylor-expanding Eqs. (
C.4
)
[
91
]
,

\displaystyle F_{\rm A}(k)=

\displaystyle\,f_{\rm A}^{(0)}+k^{2}f_{\rm A}^{(2)}+{\cal O}(k^{4})\,,\qquad{\rm where\ A}={\rm L\ and\ N}\,,

(C.8a)

with

\displaystyle f_{\rm L}^{(0)}=f_{\rm N}^{(0)}=\frac{4\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl[B_{\rm L}(r)+2B_{\rm N}(r)\bigr]\,r^{2}{\rm\,d}r\,,

(C.8b)

\displaystyle f_{\rm L}^{(2)}=-\frac{2\pi}{15}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl[3\,B_{\rm L}(r)+2\,B_{\rm N}(r)\bigr]\,r^{4}{\rm\,d}r\,,\quad f_{\rm N}^{(2)}=-\frac{2\pi}{15}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl[4\,B_{\rm N}(r)+\,B_{\rm L}(r)\bigr]\,r^{4}{\rm\,d}r\,.

(C.8c)

Since
f_{\rm L}^{(0)}=f_{\rm N}^{(0)}
,
the term
F_{\rm L}-F_{\rm N}
that multiplies
\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}

in Eq. (
C.3
) vanishes as
k^{2}
or faster in the
k\to 0
limit.
This condition requires that the trace

B_{ii}(r)=B_{\rm L}(r)+2B_{\rm N}(r)
decays faster than
r^{-3}
when
r\to\infty
,
such that the integral for
f_{\rm L}^{(0)}=f_{\rm N}^{(0)}
converges.
More generally, all the coefficients of the Taylor expansions of Eqs. (
C.8
)
are bounded if the correlators in coordinate space
decay faster than
any power law as
r\to\infty
, as expected for any causal field.
This corresponds to the analyticity causal condition described, for example,
in refs.
[
91
,
44
,
45
]
.
In particular, as we show in Sec.
C.3
, when the field is irrotational or solenoidal,
F_{\rm N}
or

F_{\rm L}
respectively becomes zero, and the resulting non-zero component
then decays as
k^{2}
or faster in the
k\to 0
limit.

C.3
Irrotational fields

An irrotational (or potential) field
\textstyle v
has zero vorticity

{\boldsymbol{{\bf\omega}}}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}=0
.
Under this condition, the field trivially satisfies
\langle R_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})\,v_{l}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle y$}}{})\rangle=0
,
where
R_{ij}=\partial_{i}v_{j}-\partial_{j}v_{i}=\varepsilon_{ijk}\,\omega_{k}

is the rotation tensor
[
84
]
.
From this expression, one can derive an equation for the corresponding two-point
correlation function
B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})

[
91
]
,

\partial_{l}B_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})-\partial_{j}B_{il}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{})=0\,.

(C.9)

Using the decomposition of
B_{ij}
in Eq. (
C.3
)
yields a differential equation that relates the longitudinal and normal components of the correlator
[
91
]
,

B_{\rm N}(r)+r\partial_{r}B_{\rm N}(r)=B_{\rm L}(r)\,,

(C.10)

which must be satisfied when the field is irrotational,
such that

B_{\rm L}(r)
and
B_{\rm N}(r)
are both non-zero and related
to each other.

Using the relation in Eq. (
C.10
), the lateral
component of the spectral density [see Eq. (
C.4b
)] becomes zero,

\displaystyle F_{\rm N}(k)=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\biggl[j_{0}(kr)B_{\rm N}(r)+\frac{j_{1}(kr)}{k}\partial_{r}B_{\rm N}(r)\biggr]\,r^{2}{\rm\,d}r=0\,.

(C.11)

This result is found after integrating by
parts Eq. (
C.11
) and using the following property of the spherical Bessel functions,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm\,d}}{{\rm\,d}r}\!\left[\frac{r^{n+1}}{k}j_{n}(kr)\right]=r^{n+1}j_{n-1}(kr)\,,

(C.12)

applied to
j_{1}
.
Therefore, the longitudinal component
F_{\rm L}(k)
is the
only non-zero contribution to the spectral density,
and can be obtained from the trace of Eq. (
C.3
),

\displaystyle F_{\rm L}(k)={\cal F}_{ii}(k)=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}j_{0}(kr)\bigl[B_{\rm L}(r)+2B_{\rm N}(r)\bigr]\,r^{2}{\rm\,d}r\,.

(C.13)

In the same way, the components
B_{\rm L}(r)
and
B_{\rm N}(r)

can be computed from
F_{\rm L}(k)
using Eq. (
C.3
),

B_{\rm L}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\biggl[j_{0}(kr)-\frac{2j_{1}(kr)}{kr}\biggr]\,k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k){\rm\,d}k\,,\quad B_{\rm N}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{j_{1}(kr)}{kr}\,k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k){\rm\,d}k\,,

(C.14)

such that adding up
B_{\rm L}(r)+2B_{\rm N}(r)
, we recover the
inverse Fourier transform of
{\cal F}_{ii}(k)=F_{\rm L}(k)
.
In particular, their values at
r=0
are

B_{\rm L}(0)=B_{\rm N}(0)={\textstyle{1\over 3}}B_{ii}(0)=\frac{1}{6\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k){\rm\,d}k\,.

(C.15)

Then, the spectrum
E_{v}(k)
, given in Eq. (
C.6
), is

E_{v}(k)=\frac{k^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}F_{\rm L}(k)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(kr)\bigl[2B_{\rm N}(r)+B_{\rm L}(r)\bigr]\,kr{\rm\,d}r\,,

(C.16)

and the two-point correlation function in Fourier space can be expressed as

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})\,v^{*}_{j}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,{\cal F}_{ij}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{})=(2\pi)^{6}\,\delta^{3}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}{}^{\prime})\,\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}\,\frac{E_{v}(k)}{4\pi k^{2}}\,.

(C.17)

We have shown that for any causal field,

F_{\rm L}(k)-F_{\rm N}(k)
is proportional to
k^{2}

(or steeper) in the
k\to 0
limit [see text below Eq. (
C.8c
)].
Then, as
F_{\rm N}=0
, it immediately follows that the
spectrum of irrotational fields is proportional to
k^{4}
(or steeper)
in the large-scale limit
[
91
]
,

E_{v}(k)=\frac{k^{2}F_{\rm L}(k)}{2\pi^{2}}\sim k^{2p},\quad{\rm with\ }p\geq 2\,,

(C.18)

which implies
{\cal P}_{v}(k)=k\,E_{v}(k)\sim k^{5}
or steeper.
This condition is equivalent to the analyticity condition claimed in refs.
[
44
,
60
]
.

A similar result is found for solenoidal fields with
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}{}=0

(e.g., magnetic or incompressible velocity fields).
In this case, it can be shown that
F_{\rm L}(k)=0
and,
hence,
E_{v}(k)=k^{2}F_{\rm N}(k)/\pi^{2}\sim k^{2p}
with
p\geq 2

is also required when
k\to 0

[
91
,
44
]
.

Appendix D
Statistical distribution of bubbles in time

In this section, we review the statistical distribution of
bubble nucleation times and lifetimes.
The probability of nucleating a broken-phase bubble at a time
t
, per unit volume in the symmetric phase, is
computed from the action
[
87
]

p(t)\sim e^{-S(t)}\,.

(D.1)

The Euclidean action,
S(t)
, decreases from infinity to a finite value at the critical time
t_{c}
,
when the two vacua in a first-order
phase transition become degenerate
[
47
]
.
The nucleation rate is then
\Gamma(t)=p(t)\,h(t)
, where
h(t)

is the volume fraction in the symmetric phase
[
47
]
,

h(t)=\exp\biggl[-\int_{t_{c}}^{t}p(t_{0})\,{\cal V}_{w}\,(t-t_{0})^{3}{\rm\,d}t_{0}\,\Theta(t-t_{c})\biggr]\,,

(D.2)

where we have defined the bubble
self-similar volume
{\cal V}_{w}\equiv V_{w}/(t-t_{0})^{3}=\tfrac{4}{3}\pi\xi_{w}^{3}
.

The number density of bubbles in a volume
V
as a function of time is

n_{b}(t\geq t_{c})=\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}=\int_{t_{c}}^{t}p(t_{0})\,h(t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}\,.

(D.3)

Then, at any time,
the mean bubble separation can be computed as
R_{\ast}(t)=n_{b}^{-1/3}(t)
.
The asymptotic value of the number density of bubbles
n_{b}
is

n_{b}\equiv\frac{N_{b}}{V}=\lim_{t\to\infty}n_{b}(t)=\int_{t_{c}}^{\infty}p(t_{0})\,h(t_{0}){\rm\,d}t_{0}\,.

(D.4)

The asymptotic bubble mean separation,
R_{\ast}=n_{b}^{-1/3}
, is the
length scale that is commonly used to
characterize the fluid perturbations and the resulting GW signal

[
30
,
31
,
60
,
28
,
29
]
.
On the other hand, refs.
[
10
,
32
]

suggest the characteristic length scale of the resultant
GW spectrum corresponds to

R_{\ast}(t_{p})
evaluated at the percolation time,

t_{p}
, which is defined as the time when a connected group of bubbles spans
the entire Universe,
h(t_{p})\simeq 0.71

[
10
,
32
]
.
In our work, we investigate the resulting length scales
2\pi\beta/k_{1}

and
2\pi\beta/k_{2}

that characterize the fluid perturbations (see Sec.
5.2
),
where
\beta
is the rate that characterizes the inverse
time scale of the phase transition, defined in Secs.
D.1
and
D.2
, respectively,
for exponential and simultaneous nucleations.
We extend the analysis
to the GW spectrum from sound waves in ref.
[
104
]
.

We define the nucleation time
t_{n}
as that at which the average number of bubbles
in a Hubble volume is one
[
47
]
,

N_{b}(t_{n})=\frac{4\pi}{3}H_{\ast}^{-3}\,n_{b}(t_{n})=1\,.

(D.5)

Note that
t_{n}
can be significantly different from,
for example, the percolation time
t_{p}

[
10
,
32
]
,
which provides a better characteristic time for GW production,
which starts only after the broken-phase bubbles start to collide.

D.1
Exponential nucleation

When the potential barrier is generated by thermal
fluctuations, the nucleation probability,
p(t)
, is often assumed to increase
exponentially in time
[
65
,
67
]
.
Indeed, this is found when the action decreases and it is Taylor expanded around
a characteristic time
t_{\ast}
,

S(t>t_{c})\simeq S(t_{\ast})-\beta(t-t_{\ast})\Rightarrow p(t>t_{c})\simeq p_{\ast}\,e^{\beta(t-t_{\ast})}=p_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\,,

(D.6)

where
\tilde{t}=\beta t
,
\tilde{t}_{\ast}=\beta t_{\ast}
,
and
p_{*}\equiv p(t_{*})=e^{-S(t_{*})}
.
Then, the parameter
\beta=-S^{\prime}(t_{\ast})\simeq\left[{\rm\,d}\ln p(t)/{\rm\,d}t\right]_{t=t_{*}}
is the rate
that characterizes the inverse
time scale of the phase transition.
A physical choice of
t_{\ast}
is,
for example, the percolation time,
t_{p}
, at which
h(t_{p})\simeq 0.71

[
10
,
32
]
.
An alternative is
t_{\ast}=t_{f}
with

h(t_{f})=1/e

[
47
,
60
,
59
]
,
which corresponds to the moment
when the rate of change of the symmetric volume,

|h^{\prime}(t)|
, reaches its maximum if
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
is satisfied.
For simplicity, we
restrict our analysis in Sec.
3.3

to Sec.
5

to this asymptotic limit. However, for the results presented
in this section, we
consider a generic finite value of

\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
.

For the exponential nucleation case, we can express
h({t})

introducing Eq. (
D.6
) into Eq. (
D.2
),

h(\tilde{t})=\exp\biggl[-\frac{{\cal V}_{w}}{\beta^{4}}\,p_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\,\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})\,\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})\biggr]\,,

(D.7)

where we have used the change of variable
z=\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}^{\prime}
to introduce
the lower incomplete Gamma function
\Gamma(4,\tilde{t})
,

\Gamma(4,\tilde{t})\equiv\int_{0}^{\tilde{t}}z^{3}e^{-z}{\rm\,d}z=6-e^{-\tilde{t}}\,(\tilde{t}^{\,3}+3\tilde{t}^{\,2}+6\tilde{t}+6)\,.

(D.8)

Note that the
\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})

function reduces to the Gamma function
\Gamma(4)=3!=6
when
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c}\to\infty
,
and it should not be confused
with the nucleation rate
\Gamma(t)=p(t)\,h(t)
.

Equation (
D.7
) can be expressed in
such a way that it manifestly depends only on the choice
of
\ln h_{\ast}
, with
h_{\ast}=h(t_{\ast})
,
and
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
,

\displaystyle h(\tilde{t})=

\displaystyle\,\exp\Biggl[\ln h_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\frac{\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})}{\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}_{*}-\tilde{t}_{c})}\,\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})\Biggr]\,.

(D.9)

The number density of bubbles
is found introducing Eq. (
D.9
) into Eq. (
D.3
),

\displaystyle n_{b}(\tilde{t})

\displaystyle=\,\frac{p_{\ast}}{\beta}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}e^{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\,\exp\Biggl[\ln h_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\frac{\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{c})}{\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}_{*}-\tilde{t}_{c})}\Biggr]\,\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}^{\prime}\,.

(D.10)

Introducing the normalized nucleation rate
\gamma=\Gamma/(\beta n_{b})
,
its integral over
\tilde{t}
corresponds to the ratio of
n_{b}(\tilde{t})
and
its asymptotic value

n_{b}=n_{b}(\tilde{t}\to\infty)
,

\frac{n_{b}(\tilde{t})}{n_{b}}=\frac{1}{n_{b}}\int_{t_{c}}^{t}\Gamma(t^{\prime}){\rm\,d}t^{\prime}=\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}\gamma(\tilde{t}^{\prime}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}^{\prime}\,.

(D.11)

We show
the resulting
h(\tilde{t})
and
\gamma(\tilde{t})
in Fig.
23
,
and
n_{b}(\tilde{t})/n_{b}
in the left panel of Fig.
24
,
for different values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}

and for
t_{\ast}=t_{f}
and
t_{p}
, i.e.,
h_{\ast}=1/e
and
h_{\ast}\simeq 0.71
.
We take
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}=3
as the minimum separation
between
\tilde{t}_{\ast}
and
\tilde{t}_{c}
.
For this separation,
p(t_{c})\simeq 0.05\,p_{\ast}
, so it can be
assumed that
p(t_{c})\ll p_{\ast}
.
The dependence on
\beta
and
\xi_{w}
remains then in
n_{b}
via
the relation between
p_{\ast}
and
\ln h_{\ast}
,
which can be found from Eq. (
D.7
),

\displaystyle p_{\ast}=-\frac{\beta^{4}}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{\ln h_{\ast}}{\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}_{*}-\tilde{t}_{c})}\,.

(D.12)

Figure 23
:

Left panel:
Average fraction of the volume in the symmetric phase
as a function of time
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
for different
values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
, considering
an exponentially increasing nucleation distribution function with time,
for two choices of
t_{\ast}
(
t_{f}
and
t_{p}
).

Right panel:
Normalized nucleation rate
\gamma\equiv\Gamma/(\beta n_{b})

[see Eq. (
D.11
)].
We observe that, in the limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,

\gamma
peaks at
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{*}+C_{\rm exp}
, where
C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})
,
reducing to
C=0
and
C\simeq 1.08

for
t_{\ast}=t_{f}
and
t_{\ast}=t_{p}
, respectively.
We take
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\geq 3

to ensure
p_{c}/p_{\ast}\lesssim 0.05
.
Black solid lines correspond to the limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
.
We observe that
h_{\rm exp}
and
\gamma_{\rm exp}
collapse when shown
as a function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
only in the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
limit,
as it can be inferred from Eq. (
D.14
),
with the asymptotic form of
\gamma_{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})
shown in Fig.
10
.

Figure 24
:

Average number of bubbles for exponential (left)
and Gaussian (right) nucleations
as a function of time
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
,
normalized by the asymptotic number of bubbles,
\bar{n}_{b}
,
evaluated at
\tilde{t}\to\infty
and taken
either
in the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
limit (left)
or in the simultaneous limit
h_{\ast}\to 1
(right) [see Eqs. (
D.16
) and (
D.30
)].
In the left panel (exponential), we show
n_{b}

for different values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
,
and for
t_{\ast}=t_{f}
(upper lines) and
t_{p}
(lower lines).
The black solid line corresponds to

n_{b}^{\rm exp}(\tilde{t})/\bar{n}_{b}^{\rm exp}
, which becomes
1-h

at
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
[see Eq. (
D.15
)].
In the right panel, we show different choices of
\ln h_{\ast}

for the Gaussian case, in the regime
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})>3
, where
we observe no dependence on
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})
.

n_{b}^{\rm Gauss}
is shown as a function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
, where times
are normalized with
\beta_{\rm eff}
(see details in Sec.
D.2
).
The black line corresponds to the limit
h_{\ast}\to 1
, for which

n_{b}
becomes a step function at
t=t_{\ast}
(simultaneous nucleation).
We observe that the approximations in the corresponding limits
affect the asymptotic number of bubbles by a factor that can be as large as
150% and 80% for exponential nucleation choosing
t_{\ast}=t_{f}

and
t_{p}
respectively with
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}=3
, and as large as 50% for Gaussian
nucleation when
h_{\ast}\geq 1/e
compared to its asymptotic value
when
h_{\ast}\to 1
; see exact values in Table
4
.

Both
p(\tilde{t})
and
h(\tilde{t})
include the function
\Gamma(4,\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c})
,
which, for
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
, converges to the value

\Gamma(4)=6
.
We find in Fig.
23
that it takes values of around
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gtrsim 8

to reach the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\to\infty

asymptotic forms of
p
and
h
.
For this time difference, the lower incomplete Gamma function
already takes a value close to 6,
\Gamma(4,8)\simeq 5.75
.
The limits
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
and
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1

yield the distribution often considered in the literature

[
67
,
60
]
, with

\displaystyle\lim_{\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1}p(\tilde{t})=-\frac{\beta^{4}}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}\ln h_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\,,\qquad\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c},\,\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1}h(\tilde{t})=\exp\bigl(\ln h_{\ast}\,e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}\bigr)\,,

(D.13)

where
6{\cal V}_{w}=8\pi\xi_{w}^{3}
is the common factor
that determines

(R_{\ast}\,\beta)^{3}
in GW literature
[
31
,
28
]
.
In this limit, the maximum value of the nucleation rate

\gamma(\tilde{t})=p(\tilde{t})\,h(\tilde{t})/(\beta n_{b})

occurs
at the time
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}+C_{\rm exp}
,
where
C_{\rm exp}=-\ln(-\ln h_{\ast})
.
Then, using the time interval
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
, we can
reexpress
p(\tilde{t})
and
h(\tilde{t})
as

\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c},\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1}p(\tilde{t})=\frac{\beta^{4}}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\,,\qquad\lim_{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c},\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1}h(\tilde{t})=\exp\bigl(-e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\bigr)\,,

(D.14)

which only depends on
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
,
indicating that the lines in Figs.
23
and
24
for different values of
h_{\ast}

in the exponential nucleation case
collapse after a time shift.
Hence, the results presented in our work can be generalized to any
choice of
h_{\ast}
when expressed
as a universal function of
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}
.
On the other hand, the
integrand
\gamma(\tilde{t}^{\prime})
becomes dominated by
large values of
\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1

around its peak value at
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}=\tilde{t}_{\ast}+C_{\rm exp}
,
as can be seen in Fig.
23
,
such that for sufficiently large
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}-\tilde{t}_{c}
, all relevant non-zero
values of
\gamma
are going to satisfy the asymptotic limits,
and the bubble number density becomes

\displaystyle\bar{n}_{b}(\tilde{t})\equiv\lim_{\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1}n_{b}(\tilde{t})=\frac{\beta^{3}}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\tilde{t}}e^{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\exp\bigl(-e^{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\bigr){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}^{\prime}=\frac{\beta^{3}}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}\bigl[1-h(\tilde{t})\bigr]\,,

(D.15)

where we use a bar to denote
n_{b}(\tilde{t})
computed in this limit,
with

\bar{n}_{b}=\frac{\beta^{3}}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}=\frac{\beta^{3}}{8\pi\xi_{w}^{3}}\,.

(D.16)

In the limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,
the number of bubbles at
\tilde{t}\gtrsim\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}

can then be approximated as
\bar{n}_{b}(\tilde{t})
;
see black lines in Fig.
24
.
The relative error of the asymptotic
n_{b}=n_{b}(\tilde{t}\to\infty)

with respect to
\bar{n}_{b}=\bar{n}_{b}(\tilde{t}\to\infty)
,

\varepsilon=1-n_{b}/\bar{n}_{b}
,
is given in Table
4
for different values of

\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}

and
\ln h_{\ast}
.
We can thus explicitly relate the
asymptotic
mean bubble separation
to
\beta
and
\xi_{w}
,

R_{\ast}\equiv\lim_{\tilde{t}\to\infty}R_{\ast}(\tilde{t})=\bar{n}_{b}^{-1/3}=(8\pi)^{1/3}\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta}\simeq 2.9\,\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta}\,.

(D.17)

Alternatively, if one considers the characteristic length scale

R_{\ast}
as the mean
bubble separation
at the percolation time
[
32
]
, we find

R_{\ast}(\tilde{t}_{p})=\bar{n}_{b}^{-1/3}(\tilde{t}_{p})=\biggl[\frac{8\pi}{1-h(\tilde{t}_{p})}\biggr]^{1/3}\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta}\simeq 4.4\,\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta}\,.

(D.18)

Table 4
:

Relative difference
\varepsilon\equiv 1-n_{b}/\bar{n}_{b}

of the asymptotic number of bubbles
n_{b}=n_{b}(t\to\infty)

for finite values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
(or

\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})
for the Gaussian case)
and
h_{\ast}
, compared to their values
\bar{n}_{b}
,
evaluated in
the following asymptotic limits:

\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1

in the case of exponential nucleation, and
\beta_{2}\to\infty

(simultaneous) for the case of Gaussian nucleation.
The asymptotic number of bubbles is

\bar{n}_{b}=\tfrac{1}{6}\beta^{3}/{\cal V}_{w}
and
\bar{n}_{b}=\tfrac{1}{6}\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}/{\cal V}_{w}

for the cases of exponential
and simultaneous nucleation, respectively.

D.2
Gaussian and simultaneous nucleation

An alternative scenario occurs when all
the bubbles nucleate almost simultaneously around the characteristic time

t_{\ast}
.
This scenario can be used to represent first-order
phase transitions in vacuum, like supercooled phase transitions
[
37
,
60
]
.

The action
S(t)
decreases until it reaches a minimum around
t_{\ast}
and then it
increases again to a larger zero-temperature level, yielding
a probability of bubble nucleation that strongly peaks around
t_{\ast}
.
The action can be Taylor expanded around
t_{\ast}
as

\displaystyle S(t>t_{c})\simeq S(t_{\ast})+{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\beta_{2}^{2}(t-t_{\ast})^{2}\Rightarrow p(t>t_{c})\simeq p_{\ast}e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\beta_{2}^{2}(t-t_{\ast})^{2}}\,,

(D.19)

with
\beta_{2}^{2}=S^{\prime\prime}(t_{\ast})
.
The volume
fraction in the metastable phase from Eq. (
D.2
) is

h(t)=\exp\biggl[-\frac{{\cal V}_{w}}{\beta_{2}^{4}}\,p_{\ast}\,\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)\,\Theta(t-t_{c})\biggr]\,,

(D.20)

where we have introduced the changes of variables
z=\beta_{2}(t^{\prime}-t_{\ast})
and
y=\beta_{2}(t-t_{\ast})
,
and defined the following integral,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)=\int_{y_{c}}^{y}e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}(y-z)^{3}{\rm\,d}z\,,

(D.21)

which we name
\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}
in
analogy to the lower incomplete Gamma function found in the exponential case [see Eq. (
D.8
)].
This integral can be computed by evaluating the following indefinite integral

\displaystyle\int e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}

\displaystyle\,(y-z)^{3}{\rm\,d}z=e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}(3y^{2}-3yz+2+z^{2})+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}y\,(y^{2}+3)\,{\rm erf}\biggl(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)\,,

(D.22a)

where erf is the error function,

{\rm erf}(z)\equiv\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{z}e^{-x^{2}}{\rm\,d}x\,.

(D.22b)

Taking the value
\ln h_{\ast}
as a parameter,
we can reexpress
h(t)
as

h(t)=\exp\Biggl[\ln h_{\ast}\,\frac{\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)}{\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)}\,\Theta(t-t_{c})\Biggr]\,,

(D.23)

where

\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)=\int_{0}^{-y_{c}}e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}z^{3}{\rm\,d}z=2-e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}y_{c}^{2}}(y_{c}^{2}+2)\,.

(D.24)

We then also have, as for the exponential nucleation case in Eq. (
D.12
),

\displaystyle p_{*}=-\frac{\beta_{2}^{4}}{{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{\ln h_{\ast}}{\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)}\,.

(D.25)

In the asymptotic limit of large
separation in time,
-y_{c}\equiv\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
,

\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)\to 2
, and the asymptotic limit
of
\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)\equiv\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}[\beta_{2}(t-t_{\ast})]

becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{c}\to-\infty}\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)=e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}y^{2}}(y^{2}+2)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,y\,(y^{2}+3)\biggl[{\rm erf}\biggl(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)+1\biggr]\,.

(D.26)

Furthermore, to introduce the simultaneous case,
we also take the limit
y\to\infty
,

\lim_{-y_{c},\,y\to\infty}\,\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)=\sqrt{2\pi}\,y^{3}\,,

(D.27)

as usually considered in the literature, see, e.g.,
ref.
[
60
]
.
Both
\beta_{2}(t-t_{\ast})
and
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})

asymptotic limits are
found if one takes
\beta_{2}\to\infty
,
such that the probability distribution becomes

\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}\,p(t)=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\beta_{2}}\,p_{\ast}\,\delta(t-t_{\ast})\,,

(D.28)

and the volume in the symmetric phase is

\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}h(\tilde{t})=\exp\biggl[-\frac{{\cal V}_{w}}{\beta_{2}}\,\sqrt{2\pi}\,p_{\ast}(t-t_{\ast})^{3}\,\Theta(t-t_{\ast})\biggr]\,.

(D.29)

The numerically-computed values
of
h(\tilde{t})
and
\gamma(\tilde{t})
for two choices of
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})
= 3 (dashed lines) and 4 (solid lines),
and different values of
h_{\ast}
,
are shown in Fig.
25
.
We find that for
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gtrsim 4
, the asymptotic
limit
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1

has already been reached.
For the minimum separation considered,
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})=3
,
the value of
p
at
t_{c}
is negligible,

p_{c}/p_{\ast}\simeq 0.01
.

Using Eq. (
D.3
), the asymptotic number of bubbles in the
simultaneous case becomes

\bar{n}_{b}=\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}n_{b}=\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\beta_{2}}\,p_{\ast}\,.

(D.30)

The numerical errors for different values of
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})
and
\ln h_{\ast}

in the asymptotic number of bubbles,

\varepsilon=1-n_{b}/\bar{n}_{b}
, are given in Table
4
.
The normalized distribution function
\gamma(t)
is shown in
the right panel of Fig.
25
and the cumulative number of
bubbles,
n_{b}(t)/\bar{n}_{b}
,
in the right panel of Fig.
24
for different values of
h_{\ast}
.

Figure 25
:

Left panel:
Average fraction of the volume in the symmetric phase
as a function of time
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
,
normalized with
1/\beta_{\rm eff}
[see Eq. (
D.33
)],
for
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})=3
(dashed lines)
and 4 (solid lines), considering
a Gaussian nucleation distribution function with time,
for different choices of
h_{\ast}
.
The functions collapse to a unique dependence on

\beta_{\rm eff}(t-t_{\ast})=\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}
as
h_{\ast}\to 1
:

h(\tilde{t})=\exp\bigl[-{\textstyle{1\over 6}}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})^{3}\,\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})\bigr]
,
given in Eq. (
D.31
).

Right panel:

Same as for left panel for the normalized nucleation rate
\gamma\equiv\Gamma/(\beta_{\rm eff}\,n_{b})
.
As
h_{\ast}\to 1
,

\gamma(\tilde{t})\to\delta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})
,
found using Eq. (
D.28
), is recovered.

Based on the asymptotic number of bubbles
\bar{n}_{b}
obtained for
exponential nucleation [see Eq. (
D.16
)],
we can define an effective
\beta_{\rm eff}
, such that
\bar{n}_{b}=\tfrac{1}{6}\,\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}/{\cal V}_{w}
.
Then, we
can introduce Eq. (
D.30
) in Eq. (
D.29
) and find

\lim_{\beta_{2}\to\infty}h(\tilde{t})=\exp\bigl[-\tfrac{1}{6}(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})^{3}\,\Theta(\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})\bigr]\,,\qquad\text{where \ }\tilde{t}=t\,\beta_{\rm eff}\quad\text{and \ }\tilde{t}_{\ast}=t_{\ast}\,\beta_{\rm eff}\,.

(D.31)

For any time
\tilde{t}
, when
the asymptotic limit
\beta_{2}(t-t_{\ast})\gg 1
does not
need to hold, we introduce this normalization back in Eq. (
D.20
) to find

h(\tilde{t})
in the case of Gaussian nucleation,

h(\tilde{t})=\exp\biggl[-\frac{1}{6\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}}{\beta_{2}^{3}}\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(y)\biggr]\,.

(D.32)

Therefore,
the fraction of the volume in the symmetric phase at the characteristic
time
\tilde{t}_{\ast}
is determined by the ratio
\beta_{\rm eff}/\beta_{2}
,

\frac{\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}}{\beta_{2}^{3}}=-\ln h_{\ast}\frac{6\,\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)}\simeq-\ln h_{\ast}\,3\sqrt{2\pi}\,,

(D.33)

where the last approximation holds in the limit
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
.
This implies that for a fixed number of nucleated bubbles (hence,
fixed value of
\beta_{\rm eff}
), we require
h_{\ast}
to
approximate one in the
\beta_{2}\to\infty
limit, such that most of
the bubbles are almost simultaneously nucleated around
t\to t_{\ast}^{+}
.
This limit corresponds to
\beta_{\rm eff}^{3}/\beta_{2}^{3}=\sqrt{2\pi}\,8\pi\,p_{\ast}\,\xi_{w}^{3}/\beta_{2}^{4}\ll 1
,
then
the approximation in Eq. (
D.29
) will hold for a broader range
of the phase transition duration (see Figure
25
).

D.3
Ensemble average over bubble lifetimes

Characterizing the fluid perturbations
across bubble collisions and during the sound-wave regime requires
a precise description of the collisions,
as we discuss in Secs.
4.3
and
5
.
According to the sound shell model, the correlation
function of the velocity field is described by averaging over
the lifetimes of the broken-phase
bubbles
[
60
]
.
Within the sound shell model, the required ingredients for computing
the lifetime distribution are the bubble nucleation rate per unit
volume
p(t)
, given in Eqs. (
D.6
) and (
D.19
) respectively for exponential
and Gaussian nucleation, and the area per
unit volume of the phase boundary,

A(t)=-\frac{1}{\xi_{w}}\frac{{\rm\,d}h}{{\rm\,d}t}\,,

(D.34)

where
h(t)
is the fraction of the Universe in the metastable
phase, given in Eqs. (
D.9
) and (
D.23
) for exponential and
Gaussian nucleations, respectively.

One of the
main assumptions of the sound shell model is that the bubbles drive unperturbed fluid
profiles until they disappear due to collisions once that the
bubble wall reaches the nucleation location of another bubble.
Consider all bubbles nucleated between times
[t,t+{\rm\,d}t]
,
whose walls are separated by a distance
[l,l+{\rm\,d}l]
.
For these bubbles, the time between nucleation and the first collision is
contained in
[l/(2\xi_{w}),(l+{\rm\,d}l)/(2\xi_{w})]
,
and their collision time in
[t+l/(2\xi_{w}),t+{\rm\,d}t+(l+{\rm\,d}l)/(2\xi_{w})]
,
with their radii in the interval
[l/2,(l+{\rm\,d}l)/2]
.
The wall of a bubble then reaches the nucleation point of the other bubble in
a time interval between

[t+r/\xi_{w},t+{\rm\,d}t+(r+{\rm\,d}r)/\xi_{w}]
,
when the radii of the collided bubbles lie within
[r,r+{\rm\,d}r]
.
The approximation proposed in ref.
[
60
]
,
consists of taking the time
T\equiv r/\xi_{w}

as the lifetime of the bubbles.
The fraction of volume over which the bubbles disappear at the time
T
is then given by

A(t+r/\xi_{w}){\rm\,d}r
.
As a consequence, the differential number of bubbles
nucleated at

[t,t+{\rm\,d}t]
and disappearing with
radii in
[r,r+{\rm\,d}r]
, is

\displaystyle{\rm\,d}n

\displaystyle=p(t){\rm\,d}t\,A\biggl(t+\frac{r}{\xi_{w}}\biggr){\rm\,d}r\,,

(D.35)

and the lifetime distribution is

\displaystyle\nu(\tilde{T}\equiv\beta T)\equiv\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta n_{b}}\frac{{\rm\,d}n}{{\rm\,d}r}=\frac{\xi_{w}}{\beta n_{b}}\int_{t_{c}}^{\infty}A(t+T)\,p(t){\rm\,d}t=-\frac{1}{\beta n_{b}}\int_{t_{c}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm\,d}h(t+T)}{{\rm\,d}t}\,p(t){\rm\,d}t\,,

(D.36)

which is already normalized by
\beta n_{b}
,
defined such that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T})\,{\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=1

(see Eq. (
D.11
) for an analogous normalization).
Indeed, notice that, using the change of variable
T^{\prime}=t+T
,
the following relation holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm\,d}h({t}+{T})}{{\rm\,d}{t}}{\rm\,d}{T}=\int_{{t}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm\,d}h({T}^{\prime})}{{\rm\,d}{T}^{\prime}}{\rm\,d}{T}^{\prime}=h(\infty)-h({t})=-h({t})\,,

(D.37)

and we can show that
\nu({\tilde{T}})

is properly normalized,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\tilde{T}){\rm\,d}\tilde{T}=-\frac{1}{\beta n_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}p(\tilde{t}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dh(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T})}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}}{\rm\,d}{T}=\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}\gamma(\tilde{t}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}=1\,.

(D.38)

To simplify the notation in this section, we use
\beta
for both
cases of exponential and simultaneous nucleation, where
for the latter we set
\beta=\beta_{\rm eff}
.

Figure 26
:

Normalized distribution function of the bubble lifetimes for
an exponentially increasing distribution
of nucleation times (EXP, upper panels) with the choices
h_{\ast}=1/e
(left) and
h_{\ast}=0.71
(right),
and for a Gaussian nucleation
probability (GAUSS, lower panels) with
\ln h_{\ast}=-0.01
(left)
and
\ln h_{\ast}=-10^{-6}
(right), which approaches the simultaneous (SIM) limit
when
h_{\ast}\to 1
.
The distribution of lifetimes is computed for
different values of

\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}

(or
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})
for the Gaussian case, in which
times
\tilde{t}
are normalized by
1/\beta_{\rm eff}
).
Note that for the exponential
case, the lines tend to the lifetime distribution
found in the asymptotic limit
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,

\nu_{\rm exp}(\tilde{T})=e^{-\tilde{T}}

[see Eq. (
D.41
)],
regardless of the choice of
h_{\ast}
.
For the case of Gaussian nucleation,
the asymptotic limit
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1

tends to the form
\nu_{\rm sim}(\tilde{T})={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\tilde{T}^{2}\exp(-{1\over 6}\tilde{T}^{3})
[see Eq. (
D.45
)] only
in the simultaneous case with
h_{\ast}\to 1
(bottom right panel).
Therefore, for other values of
h_{\ast}
,
\nu_{\rm Gauss}(\tilde{T})
takes a different
form than
\nu_{\rm sim}(\tilde{T})
in the
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
limit.

Integrating by parts Eq. (
D.36
),
\nu
can be expressed as

\nu({\tilde{T}})=\frac{1}{\beta\,n_{b}}\Biggl[h({\tilde{t}_{c}}+{\tilde{T}})\,p({\tilde{t}_{c}})+\int_{{\tilde{t}_{c}}}^{\infty}h({\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}})\frac{{\rm\,d}p({\tilde{t}})}{{\rm\,d}{\tilde{t}}}{\rm\,d}{\tilde{t}}\Biggr]\simeq\frac{1}{\beta\,n_{b}}\int_{{\tilde{t}_{c}}}^{\infty}h({\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}})\frac{{\rm\,d}p({\tilde{t}})}{{\rm\,d}{\tilde{t}}}{\rm\,d}{\tilde{t}}\,,

(D.39)

where we have assumed that
p(t_{c})\ll 1
.
This approximation holds exactly only
in the
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1

limit.
For finite values of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
, the boundary term should be included,
but it quickly vanishes as one increases
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
.
In the case of exponential nucleation, using Eq. (
D.39
), we find

\nu(\tilde{T})=\frac{p_{\ast}}{\beta\,n_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\ast}}h(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}\,,

(D.40)

where
h(\tilde{t})
is given in Eq. (
D.9
).
The numerical results, for different choices of
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}
and two values of
h_{\ast}
,
corresponding to
h_{\ast}=1/e
and
h_{\ast}=0.71
,
are shown in the top panels of Fig.
26
.

In the asymptotic limit
\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
and
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
, we can use
the asymptotic expression of
p
and
h
in
Eq. (
D.14
)
to compute the bubble lifetime distribution
when the nucleation time distribution
is exponential as

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm exp}(\tilde{T})=\frac{1}{6\,{\cal V}_{w}}\frac{\beta^{3}}{\bar{n}_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}e^{\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\,\exp\bigl(-e^{\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}}\bigr){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}=e^{-\tilde{T}}\int_{x_{c}}^{\infty}e^{-x}{\rm\,d}x\simeq e^{-\tilde{T}}\,,

(D.41)

where we have defined

x=\exp(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})

and
x_{c}=\exp(\tilde{t}_{c}+\tilde{T}-\tilde{t}_{\rm peak})
.
In the last approximation, we assume
\tilde{t}_{\rm peak}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gg 1
,
which implies
x_{c}\simeq 0
and yields the normalized bubble lifetime distribution

\nu_{\rm exp}(\tilde{T})\approx e^{-\tilde{T}}

[
60
]
.
We note that this function is independent of the choice
h_{\ast}
.
In Fig.
26
, we show that choosing
\tilde{t}_{\ast}-\tilde{t}_{c}\gtrsim 4
is sufficient to reach the approximation in Eq. (
D.41
).
In this limit, also the choice of
h_{\ast}
becomes irrelevant, and for any
choice, one finds
\nu_{\rm exp}(\tilde{T})=e^{-\tilde{T}}
.

In the case of Gaussian nucleation,
p
and
h
are given in Eqs. (
D.19
) and (
D.32
) respectively.
Hence, Eq. (
D.36
) yields

\nu_{\rm Gauss}(\tilde{T})=-\frac{\ln h_{\ast}}{n_{b}\,\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}(0)}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}\,{\Gamma}_{\rm Gauss}^{\prime}(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T}-\tilde{t}_{\ast})\,h(\tilde{t}+\tilde{T})\,p(\tilde{t}){\rm\,d}\tilde{t}\,,

(D.42)

where the derivative
\Gamma_{\rm Gauss}^{\prime}
[see Eq. (
D.21
)] is

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{\rm Gauss}^{\prime}(y)=

\displaystyle\,3\,\int_{y_{c}}^{y}e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}(y-z)^{2}{\rm\,d}z\,,

(D.43)

and can be computed using the following indefinite integral

\int e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}(y-z)^{2}{\rm\,d}z=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}{\rm erf}\biggl(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)(y^{2}+1)-e^{-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}z^{2}}(z-2y)\,.

(D.44)

However, in the limit of infinitesimally short phase transitions,

\beta_{2}\rightarrow\infty
, which implies
\beta_{2}(t-t_{\ast})\gg 1
and
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1
, we can use the delta approximation for
p(\tilde{t})
, as in Eq. (
D.28
),
and
h(\tilde{t})
in Eq. (
D.31
)
to find the
bubble lifetime distribution
when the nucleation time distribution
is simultaneous as

[
60
]

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm sim}(\tilde{T})

\displaystyle=-\ln h_{\ast}\,3\pi\frac{p_{\ast}}{\bar{n}_{b}}\int_{\tilde{t}_{c}}^{\infty}\delta(t-t_{\ast})\,\beta_{2}^{2}(t+T-t_{\ast})^{2}\exp\biggl[\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\ln h_{\ast}\beta_{2}^{3}(t+T-t_{\ast})^{3}\biggr]{\rm\,d}\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=-\ln h_{\ast}\,3\pi\frac{p_{\ast}}{\bar{n}_{b}}\,(\beta_{2}T)^{2}\exp\biggl[\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\ln h_{\ast}(\beta_{2}T)^{3}\biggr]={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\tilde{T}^{2}\exp\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{T}^{3}\bigr)\,.

(D.45)

The lifetime distribution for Gaussian and simultaneous nucleation
is shown in the bottom panels of Fig.
26
, where we see how the simultaneous
expression in Eq. (
D.45
) is found only when
\beta_{2}(t_{\ast}-t_{c})\gg 1

and
h_{\ast}\to 1
(bottom right panel).

Appendix E
Analytical computation of the integral of the single and double broken power law templates

In this section, we compute analytically the integral over the functions
used to provide templates of the velocity spectrum in Sec.
5.2
.
In particular, we choose a single (SBPL) and a double (DBPL) broken power law template
[see Eqs. (
5.16
)], equivalent to those used in the LISA Cosmology Working group
work on phase transitions
[
29
]
,

\displaystyle S_{\rm SBPL}(k)=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{k}{k_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{-6/b_{1}}\,,

(E.1a)

\displaystyle S_{\rm DBPL}(k)=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{k}{k_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\Biggr]^{\frac{\sigma-2}{b_{1}}}\,\Biggl[1+\biggl(\frac{k}{k_{2}}\biggr)^{b_{2}}\Biggr]^{\frac{-\sigma-4}{b_{2}}}\,,

(E.1b)

where
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
are smoothness parameters,

k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

are the locations of the transitions, and

\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
is the intermediate slope of the DBPL.

The normalization coefficient
{\cal N}
, included in Eq. (
5.15
), is
defined such that

{\cal N}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}S(k)\,k^{2}{\rm\,d}k\,.

(E.2)

Let us start computing this integral for the SBPL case,

\displaystyle{\cal N}^{-1}_{\rm SBPL}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{4}\bigl[1+(k/k_{1})^{b_{1}}\bigr]^{-6/b_{1}}{\rm\,d}k=\frac{k_{1}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{4}\,(1+x^{b_{1}})^{-6/b_{1}}{\rm\,d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k_{1}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}b_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{5/b_{1}-1}(1+y)^{-6/b_{1}}{\rm\,d}y=\frac{k_{1}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}b_{1}}B\bigl(\tfrac{5}{b_{1}},\tfrac{1}{b_{1}}\bigr)=\frac{k_{1}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}b_{1}}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{5}{b_{1}}\bigr)\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{1}{b_{1}}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{6}{b_{1}}\bigr)}\,,

(E.3)

where we have used the changes of variables
x=k/k_{1}
and
y=x^{b_{1}}
,
and we have defined the Euler beta function,

B(a,b)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{a-1}(1+y)^{-(a+b)}=\frac{\Gamma(a)\,\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a+b)}\,.

(E.4)

For the DBPL template,
let us first consider the case in which
b_{1}=b_{2}=b
for simplicity.
Then,
the normalization factor is

\displaystyle{\cal N}_{\rm DBPL}^{-1}(b_{1}=b_{2}=b)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{4}\bigl[1+(k/k_{1})^{b}\bigr]^{(\sigma-2)/b}\,\bigl[1+(k/k_{2})^{b}\bigr]^{-(\sigma-4)/b}{\rm\,d}k

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k_{2}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}b}\int_{0}^{1}t^{5/b-1}\bigl[1-(1-k_{2}^{b}/k_{1}^{b})\,t\bigr]^{(\sigma-2)/b}(1-t)^{1/b-1}{\rm\,d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k_{2}^{5}}{2\pi^{2}b}B\bigl(\tfrac{5}{b},\tfrac{1}{b}\bigr){}_{2}F_{1}\Bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b},\tfrac{5}{b}\,;\tfrac{6}{b}\,;1-\tfrac{k_{2}^{b}}{k_{1}^{b}}\Bigr)\,,

(E.5)

where we have used the change of variable
(k/k_{2})^{b}=t/(1-t)
and
defined the
integral representation of the Gauss hypergeometric function

{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha,\beta\,;\gamma\,;z)=\frac{1}{B(\beta,\gamma-\beta)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\beta-1}\,(1-t)^{\gamma-\beta-1}\,(1-z\,t)^{-\alpha}{\rm\,d}t\,,

(E.6)

for
{\rm Re}(\gamma)>{\rm Re}(\beta)>0
.
In the limit
k_{1}=k_{2}
, we recover the expression corresponding to the
SBPL in Eq. (
E.3
).
This is due to the fact that the normalized hypergeometric function
is normalized such that
{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha,\beta\,;\gamma\,;0)=1
for any
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
, as can be seen in Eq. (
E.6
).

Let us now consider the DBPL in
the general case, where we can have
b_{1}\neq b_{2}
.
The integral can be performed by making use of the
Mathai-Saxena-Haubold identity (see Eq. (1.43) of ref.
[
90
]
),

(1+x)^{-\lambda}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\lambda)}\,H^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left[x\;\middle|\;\begin{matrix}(1-\lambda,\,1)\\
(0,\,1)\end{matrix}\right],\qquad\text{with \ }{\rm Re}(x)>0,\,{\rm Re}(\lambda)>0\,,

(E.7)

by which the factors
\bigl[1+(k/\alpha)^{\beta}\bigr]^{\gamma}
in the integral can be expressed using the Fox
H
-function. This leads to the following integral

\displaystyle{\cal N}_{\rm DBPL}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}

\displaystyle\,\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{4}\bigl[1+(k/k_{1})^{b_{1}}\bigr]^{(\sigma-2)/b_{1}}\bigl[1+(k/k_{2})^{b_{2}}\bigr]^{-(\sigma-4)/b_{2}}{\rm\,d}k=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{\sigma+4}{b_{2}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\,\int_{0}^{\infty}k^{4}H^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left[\biggl(\frac{k}{k_{1}}\biggr)^{b_{1}}\;\middle|\;\begin{matrix}\bigl(1-\frac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}},\,1\bigr)\\
(0,\,1)\end{matrix}\right]\,H^{1,1}_{1,1}\!\left[\biggl(\frac{k}{k_{2}}\biggr)^{b_{2}}\;\middle|\;\begin{matrix}\bigl(1-\frac{\sigma+4}{b_{2}},\,1\bigr)\\
(0,\,1)\end{matrix}\right]{\rm\,d}k\,.

(E.8)

We then recognize the Mellin transform of the product of two Fox
H
-functions,
for which we have the result presented
in Sec. 2.3 of ref.
[
90
]
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{s-1}H_{p,q}^{m,n}\!\left[zx^{\sigma}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}(a_{p},A_{p})\\
(b_{q},B_{q})\end{array}\right]H_{p_{1},q_{1}}^{m_{1},n_{1}}\!\left[\eta x\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}(d_{p_{1}},D_{p_{1}})\\
(e_{q_{1}},E_{q_{1}})\end{array}\right]{\rm\,d}x

\displaystyle=\eta^{-s}H_{p+q_{1},q+p_{1}}^{m+n_{1},n+m_{1}}\!\left[z\eta^{-\sigma}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}(1-e_{q_{1}}-sE_{q_{1}},\,\sigma E_{q_{1}}),\,(a_{p},A_{p})\\
(b_{m},B_{m}),\,(1-d_{p_{1}}-sD_{p_{1}},\,\sigma D_{p_{1}}),\,(b_{m+1},B_{m+1}),\,\ldots,(b_{q},B_{q})\end{array}\right]\,.

The integral in Eq. (
E
) can be expressed using Eq. (
E
) with
the change of variable
x=(k/k_{2})^{b_{2}}
and for the values

m=n=p=q=m_{1}=n_{1}=p_{1}=q_{1}=1
,
z=k_{1}^{-b_{1}}
,

\eta=k_{2}^{-b_{2}}
,
x=k^{b_{2}}
,
s=5/b_{2}
,
\sigma=b_{1}/b_{2}
,

a_{1}=1-(2-\sigma)/b_{1}
,
A_{1}=1
,
b_{1}=0
,
B_{1}=1
,

d_{1}=1-(\sigma+4)/b_{2}
,
D_{1}=1
,
e_{1}=0
, and
E_{1}=1
.
Hence, the
normalization
factor relating the integral and the amplitude of the DBPL fit becomes

{\cal N}^{-1}_{\rm DBPL}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}b_{2}}\frac{k_{2}^{5}}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{\sigma+4}{b_{2}}\bigr)}\,H_{2,2}^{2,2}\!\left[\left(\frac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\right)^{b_{1}}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\bigl(-4,\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\bigr),\;\bigl(1-\frac{2-\sigma}{b_{1}},1\bigr)\\
(0,1),\;\bigl(\frac{\sigma+4}{b_{2}}-5,\frac{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\bigr)\end{array}\right]\,.

(E.15)

We note that when
b_{1}=b_{2}=b
, this expression becomes

{\cal N}^{-1}_{\rm DBPL}(b_{1}=b_{2}=b)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}b}\frac{k_{2}^{5}}{\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{2-\sigma}{b}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{\sigma+4}{b}\bigr)}\,H_{2,2}^{2,2}\!\left[\left(\frac{k_{2}}{k_{1}}\right)^{b}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\bigl(-4,1\bigr),\;\bigl(1-\frac{2-\sigma}{b},1\bigr)\\
(0,1),\;\bigl(\frac{\sigma+4}{b}-5,1\bigr)\end{array}\right]\,.

(E.16)

Plugging this back into Eqs. (
E
) and (
E
), we recover the definition
of the hypergeometric function, and, hence, Eq. (
E.5
).
